# Tesseract Logic Calculator

Oct 11, 2026 · @Tiffany Taylor

Tiffany Taylor (Tifinchi), Boise State University. Calculations, literature search and drafting with Claude (Anthropic); section 2 states who originated each piece.

## Abstract

The Tesseract Logic Calculator is the author's system for computing with a cube of side 2. Its 27 points sit at distances √k from the centre, its eight corners carry delimiters that in the electrical frame name charge, current, flux and voltage, and every derivative, inverse or change of scale is a quarter or half turn of the tesseract that holds the cube. This paper uses it to compute the structure of reality silo by silo, from the vacuum to the cosmos, and scores every result against measurement on one yardstick, beside the standard theories' result for the same quantity. Where it restates established law it is exact: the Standard Model's generation of matter as a 16-state register, the energy–momentum relation and interval of special relativity, the classical tests of gravity, the shells of the atom, and a law of motion that, integrated exactly, gives Mercury's perihelion, light's bending at the Sun, the GPS clock rate and hydrogen's Lyman-α thousands of times faster than step by step. Where the standard theories take inputs it gives numbers, all 22 within 1.6σ of their primary measurements, among them the τ mass from the electron and muon (1,776.969 MeV, −0.4σ), the top and Higgs masses (172.57 ± 0.38 and 125.20 ± 0.92 GeV), neutrino mixing from a 15° tilt (sin²θ₁₂ = 0.318, −1.5σ), the matter share π²/32 (−1.2σ) and the vacuum's energy scale (2.02 ± 0.20 meV, +1.1σ, where quantum field theory is 10¹²⁰ too large). The layout that gives them was tested against every alternative inside the cube's own sets, 4.4 × 10²⁰ arrangements in nine families, under the author's behavior rule: an alignment stays only if nothing downstream of it contradicts a measurement. The rule leaves 76,800, one in 5.7 × 10¹⁵; in every family the calculator's layout survives, no survivor does better on measurements it was not chosen with, and the choices the families share agree. Three readings that broke downstream are withdrawn; their numbers stay. The same rigour is turned on the matches themselves: a calibration with alphabets built from random ingredients shows that a loose match to a measured constant carries no weight at this density, so none is counted, and the layout's own numbers carry 5.1 bits against the chance that families of this size match random values as well, beside the lepton balance it explains, Koide's relation, at 9.7; the standard theories' eleven predictions of independent measurements, made over a century, carry 102 bits, and the standard theories carry six failures, among them the vacuum's energy and the Hubble constant. Under the author's Quad-Core rule the calculator stands as a correct notation for the laws it restates and as the one arrangement of its own sets that observation leaves standing; whether that arrangement is reality's own mechanism is decided by nine predictions frozen with a Bayes-factor rule and deposited before their data. If the layout's numbers are right they become decisive with probability 0.434 by 2031, when JUNO's sin²θ₁₂ decides first, and 0.685 by the 2040s.

*Keywords:* tesseract; difference of squares; root families; Koide relation; tribimaximal mixing; vacuum energy; behavior rule; exhaustive permutation search; look-elsewhere effect; pre-registration; Bayes factor; theory comparison.

## 1. Introduction

The universe, as the Tesseract Logic Calculator computes it, is a reactive board, a capacitance and an inductance at every point. Every state of the board sits on one of the 27 points of a cube of side 2, and every change of state is a quarter or half turn through the four cells of the tesseract that holds the cube. The calculator's layout, the choice it makes at each of its relations, is the arrangement of the cube's own options that observation leaves standing (sections 1.2 and 15). With that layout and two rules, balance and the three-phase start, it rebuilds four established structures exactly: the Standard Model's generation of matter, the two tables of special relativity, the classical tests of gravity and the shells of the atom. Where the standard theories take inputs, it gives numbers. This paper carries out that computation silo by silo, from the vacuum to the cosmos, names what each silo still lacks, and scores every result on one yardstick against the measurement, beside the standard theories' result for the same quantity.

### 1.1 The calculator

The Tesseract Logic Calculator has four layers, all the author's in conception:

1. **A geometry.** The side-2 cube's 27 points sit at distances √k from the centre, k = 0 to 3, with a held fourth level at 2. The difference-of-squares tables, root families and the dimension converter come from the author's trigonometry (section 3).
2. **A cipher and its mechanics.** Eight delimiters name the corners. In the electrical frame they carry charge, current, flux and voltage. The cube never moves: a derivative is a quarter turn into the next cell of a tesseract, an inverse is a half turn into the opposite cell, and a change of scale is the second turn (section 4).
3. **A physical reading.** The vacuum is a reactive board of capacitance and inductance. A balanced state carries equal shares; a state in motion sits slightly past balance, held by a load of one coupling squared over 8π² per loading phase. Three phases cannot start together (sections 5 and 7).
4. **Numbers.** The reading commits to values where standard theory has inputs (sections 9 to 13), each fixed before it was compared and each scored in section 16.

Its law of motion is the established one, written in its own terms: the quarter turn applied to the gradient of the sum table on two legs moves every state (section 4.11).

Sections 5 to 14 compute reality silo by silo, from the vacuum to the atom. Section 15 tries every alternative to the layout inside the cube's own sets and measures the calculator's precision and speed; section 16 scores the layout against measurement, section 17 weighs how much of its agreement chance could give, and section 18 freezes the predictions that decide. Section 20 names what the layout does not yet contain, silo by silo, and section 22 asks directly whether this is how the universe works, answering in three senses: whether the calculator computes, whether it explains, and whether it is complete.

### 1.2 The layout that survives

Each of the calculator's relations is built from choices, among them a balance angle, a phase, a tilt, a load, a split of the proton's spin, a share and a scale, and each choice has alternatives inside the cube's own sets. Section 15 tries them all.

- **The elimination.** Of 4.4 × 10²⁰ layouts in nine families, the behavior rule of section 1.3 leaves 76,800, one in 5.7 × 10¹⁵.
- **The frontier.** In every family the calculator's layout survives, and no surviving alternative does better on measurements it was not chosen with. The choices the families share, the balance Q = ⅔ and the 120° spacing of the three phases, agree on it. Three readings the calculator carried broke downstream and are withdrawn; the numbers they were attached to stay.
- **Agreement with observation.** In this layout every relation the rule retains meets its measurement: the restated laws exactly, and the 22 committed numbers within 1.6σ of their primary measurements. Where other data sets press, they press the standard cosmology the same way (section 16.3).
- **What is missing.** A few measurements miss for every arrangement of a family, and for the standard cosmology too, and some behaviours no arrangement reaches. Rearranging the layout removes neither. In the author's words, “some components, relationships or factors may be missing to achieve the correct layout”; section 20 names each one, silo by silo.
- **What remains open.** A frontier is what a correct layout looks like; it is also what the best fit of a large family looks like, and the elimination cannot tell the two apart on its own. Section 17 measures how much the layout's agreement exceeds what its sets could supply by chance, and the frozen predictions of section 18 decide the rest.

### 1.3 How the computation is kept honest

Matching measured constants with small integers, π and square roots can look like insight and carry none. Eddington's 1931 count of the universe's particles, Lenz's mₚ/mₑ ≈ 6π⁵ (now about 3 × 10⁵ standard deviations from 1,836.15267343(11); Mohr et al. 2024) and Wyler's 1971 formula for α (about 4,000 standard deviations from CODATA 2022) are the standard warnings (Jentschura and Nándori 2014). Koide's 1982 relation is the standard counter-example: it has grown more precise as the τ was measured, and it still has no accepted derivation.

The judges of such matches are statistical:

- **Odds.** Good (1990) scored formulas as Bayesian odds, weighing the digits they get right against the prior improbability of their ingredients.
- **Trial factors.** Particle physics pays a trial factor for searching in many places, the look-elsewhere effect (Gross and Vitells 2010).
- **Bayes factors.** Evidence against an alternative is set on a fixed scale (Kass and Raftery 1995).
- **Pre-registration.** Fixing the analysis before the data separates prediction from postdiction (Nosek et al. 2018).

This paper follows six rules that come from those judges and from the author:

1. **Definitions first.** Every number the calculator commits to was written down, with its formula and its comparison, before it was compared. The nine predictions of section 18 are deposited with a SHA-256 fingerprint.
2. **The behavior rule.** In the author's words, the calculator uses “the alignments that do not create down-wind mismatched observeables”: a number set can sit in range while what it produces in other alignments does not, so an alignment stays only if nothing downstream of it contradicts a measurement. Successes stay with their proof, and trials that failed are left out. So the calculator is unbiased toward any theory: an alignment stays, whatever its source, only if what it produces matches observed behaviour.
3. **One yardstick.** The calculator and the standard theories are scored the same way: the same pull, the same categories of standing, and the same tests for chance (sections 16 and 17).
4. **Paying for density.** Every match is weighed against how densely the calculator's numbers cover the region where it falls (section 17).
5. **Origination.** Every piece is credited to whoever originated it: the author's unpublished work is cited as hers, prior work to its authors, and each completion Claude added is marked (added) (sections 2 and 19).
6. **Inputs declared.** Numbers no theory derives are taken from measurement, the same set for every theory, and a relation counts only by removing one once the data confirm it (section 20.3).

### 1.5 Conventions

- **Pulls.** A pull is (measured − computed)/σ, with uncertainties combined in quadrature; for an asymmetric error the side facing the computed value is used.
- **“Same”.** The calculator's number equals the standard theories' number.
- **Standing.** Every result carries one of six: exact (an identity or theorem), same, new number, reading (a re-description that changes no number), prediction (a number not yet measured well enough to decide), or exposure (a commitment the data currently press on).
- **Silo.** A layer of reality as Table 1 divides it, from motion to atoms: the author's word for a domain in which the layout must hold.
- **Inherited and missing.** A miss every arrangement of a family shares is inherited: it stays on the record and eliminates nothing. A behaviour no arrangement reaches marks a missing component, relationship or factor (section 20).
- **Citations and data.** Citations are author–year. Constants are CODATA 2022 and PDG 2026 unless stated, as of 10 October 2026.

## 2. Origination, sources and method

The concepts, methods, mathematics and physical readings in this paper are the author's. Where the same result had been published before, the earlier work is cited at the point of use and in section 19. The derivation of the turns from her constraints, the computations, the literature search, the statistics and the drafting were done with Claude, Anthropic's AI model, as the tool, and every completion Claude added to her pages is marked (added).

### 2.1 Who originated what

**The author.** She devised and hand-compiled:

- the delimiter cipher and the cube it writes (Taylor, *ascii geometry*; *Tifinchi's Cypher Notes*);
- Tifinchi Trig, the difference-of-squares tables and root families (Taylor 2025; Taylor 2026e);
- the dimension converter, the cube cut into identical pyramids, the Balance Chart and the phase circle (*Tifinchi's Cypher Notes*);
- the element tables and the Rosetta fold (*Root Elements*; *Calc Elements*; Taylor 2026f);
- the Fibonacci options (Taylor 2026g) and the axis notebook (Taylor 2026b);
- the Quad-Core claim test and the reasoning framework around it (Taylor 2026a).

She also originated:

- **the physical tenets.**
  - The vacuum is a reactive board of capacitance and inductance, her electrical reading drawn on the emergent vacuum of Bass's papers (Bass 2015 to 2026). Bass wrote the dynamics of the vacuum's subcomponents; the circuit, inductance included, is hers.
  - Balance sits slightly past 45°, the way a balanced rock needs a little more than 45° to tip.
  - Three phases cannot start together.
  - The electron is a reaction of the board, and virtual particles are its reactive response, not particles.
  - Inductance comes from the √2 → √4 swing into the perpendicular dimension.
  - Gravity is a pull from what is held, with no dark-matter particle.
- **the mechanics.** The cube never moves; derivatives, inverses and changes of scale are turns of a tesseract; information folds rather than being lost; a misfit is found from its neighbours.
- **the behavior rule.** Keep only alignments that create no downstream mismatch.
- **the direction of every check,** including the demand that the calculator and the standard theories be scored without bias toward either.

Her rules are quoted verbatim where they are used (sections 3 and 4).

**Claude.** Claude did the following, each labelled as Claude's where it appears:

- derived the quarter turn J uniquely from her electrical frame, and the second turn K;
- computed every number, and wrote and ran the scripts and the statistical tests;
- searched and opened the literature;
- drafted the text.

**Marks.** Each authored piece ends with its standing against the literature searched on 9 and 10 October 2026:

- **N**, *novel*: not found in the literature searched;
- **M**, *matches*: the same result is published, and the paper says where;
- **D**, *diverges*: the author's version departs from the published one, and the paper says how.

Many pieces are composite: the algebra matches a classical result, and its use or reading is new.

**In the author's words:** ‘As the “author,” I want to be forthcoming about the level of AI usage in this document, and to do so I must be explicit about my neurodivergency. I came up with the methods and concepts; I understand and hand-compiled the original cube cipher math and geometry. The subsequent calculations and resources were compiled by Claude. Using AI is my method of translating how a neurodivergent mind understands into a precise language the rest of the world can hopefully read.’

**Prior dissemination.** Parts of this work were posted online as drafts between November 2025 and October 2026. None has been peer reviewed. This paper restates the material in full, recomputed, and supersedes those drafts.

### 2.2 How the author reasons: structure before sequence

The author's two research papers on cognition (Taylor 2026c; Taylor 2026d), written for a first-year writing course at Boise State University, describe the reasoning style that produced the calculator. They explain why this paper keeps her structures as drawn and builds the sequential proofs around them.

**Her definition.** She defines multimodal reasoning as “a neural pathway built from overlapping relationships between space, time, scale, repetition, sequence, geometry, pattern, linearity, angularity, and process”. It synthesises three existing uses of “multimodal”, each of which she cites:

- in cognitive science, perception across several senses at once (Lumen Learning, *Waymaker Psychology*);
- in computing, models that integrate text, images and sound (IBM);
- in business writing, a weaving of disciplines (Feng, in *International Business Times*, 2025).

**Her illustration.** Three students are asked to show with their hands that 2 + 2 = 4:

- the first holds up four fingers, the value;
- the second holds up two and two, the parts;
- the third, her own answer, merges two pairs by moving fingers from one hand to the other, the operation.

**Her argument.** Instructions such as “show the steps” fit sequential reasoners and over-constrain structural ones. Three sources carry it, cited as she quotes them:

- **Bor, Billington and Baron-Cohen (2007).** The savant Daniel Tammet showed no advantage for structured over unstructured digit sequences, whereas controls did; for him “all digit sequences have internal structure linked to his synaesthesia.”
- **Kargupta et al. (2025).** Language models “deploy behavioral strategies based on learned priors rather than adapting to problem-specific demands.”
- **Crompton et al. (2021).** Autistic groups passed a story on as accurately as non-autistic groups, and mixed groups less accurately.

**Two sources that support her thesis directly (added).**

- **Anthropic (2025).** For 36 + 59, Claude runs parallel paths inside, an approximate magnitude and an exact last digit, yet when asked how it added it describes carrying the 1. That is a measured gap between a non-sequential process and a sequential explanation.
- **Liu et al. (2025).** Chain-of-thought prompting lowers performance on tasks where verbal deliberation also hurts humans.

**What it means for the mathematics (added).** The 2 + 2 demonstration separates a value from its derivation, and the same split runs through her pages: √8, 2√2 and √(2³) are one value with three derivations (section 3.2). “Too literal” then means answering at the level of the derivation when the listener asked at the level of the value. That is a precise, testable description, not a deficit.

**Standing.** **M**: the uses of “multimodal” she synthesises. **N**: multimodal reasoning as a style to be diagnosed and benchmarked, and value against derivation as the account of “too literal”. Neither is tested here.

### 2.3 The author's decision rule: the Quad-Core test

Her reasoning framework decides whether a claim may stand: “Quad-Core evaluation runs four independent rejection checks in parallel. Stabilization is permitted only if ALL checks return NOT-REJECTED” (Taylor 2026a).

1. **Outcome.** Compare prediction with observation; on a mismatch, reject.
2. **Constraint.** Test the claim under changes of scale, time, cost, causality and context; “if claim breaks, REJECT.”
3. **Alternative.** Compare competing explanations; “if an alternative yields fewer violations under other cores, REJECT.”
4. **Consequence.** Project what follows if the claim is wrong; “if error is deferred rather than eliminated, REJECT or UNDETERMINED.”

Each core returns REJECT, NOT\_REJECT or UNDETERMINED. The synthesis is REJECTED if any core rejects, UNSTABLE if none rejects but one is undetermined, and STABILIZED if all four do not reject. “No core overrides another. No majority rule.” Her definition of proof follows: “Proof is the closure of all active rejection paths under quad-core evaluation.”

**Stopping rules (added).** An open domain never runs out of alternatives or consequences, so the cores need a finite list and a horizon. This paper fixes both:

- **the alternatives:** the standard theories, every arrangement of the cube's own choices (section 15), and alphabets built from random ingredients;
- **the horizon:** the data as of 10 October 2026.

Sections 17 and 22 apply the rule to the calculator and, on the same terms, to the standard theories.

**The framework around it.** *Tifinchi's Diagnostic and Reasoning Framework* is a 7,817-line plain-text specification meant to run in any reader or model without prior conversation. Its main parts:

- eight fixed passes, each leaving a record that cannot be altered;
- the Quad-Core veto;
- curiosity, run forwards from a gap and backwards from an outcome that cannot be denied;
- transfer between domains;
- mandatory revision by “prove-don't-prune”: add proof, never delete structure;
- reduction allowed only when the short form can regenerate the full process;
- an ethics pass ordered by irreversibility, consent, asymmetry of power and truth.

**Standing.** **M**: the Outcome core is falsification (Popper 1959); the Alternative core is the method of multiple working hypotheses (Chamberlin 1890) and strong inference (Platt 1964). **N**: four parallel vetoes with an explicit unstable state and no majority, as one procedure, were not found in this form.

### 2.4 Data

Every number is computed from the following, as of 10 October 2026:

- **constants:** CODATA 2022, with the exact SI values of h, e and c (Mohr et al. 2024);
- **particle data:** the Particle Data Group's 2026 tables (PDG 2026);
- **neutrino mixing:** NuFIT 6.0 and 6.1 (Esteban et al. 2026) and JUNO's first result (JUNO Collaboration 2025);
- **cosmology:** Planck 2018 (Planck Collaboration 2020), DESI DR2 (DESI Collaboration 2025), ACT DR6 (ACT Collaboration 2025) and the supernova compilations cited where used;
- **the proton:** the continuum-limit lattice results of Alexandrou et al. (2026) and Zhao et al. (2025);
- **the Standard Model's running:** two-loop couplings and three-loop QCD from the matching of Buttazzo et al. (2013), updated to PDG 2026.

### 2.5 One yardstick for every theory

The calculator and the standard theories are scored by the same rules.

1. **The pull.** For any computed value, the pull is (measured − computed)/σ, with the uncertainties of both combined in quadrature.
2. **What counts as a prediction.** A theory predicts a quantity when its value follows from the theory and measured inputs other than that quantity. When the quantity is itself an input, a free parameter, the theory scores *input* and earns no pull. The Standard Model's 19 parameters (26 with neutrino masses), and ΛCDM's densities, are inputs in this sense.
3. **Failures count the same.** A standard prediction that misses is scored like a missed number of the calculator. Examples are the vacuum energy, and the Higgs mass a vacuum sitting exactly on its edge would have.
4. **Standing.** Every result carries one of the six standings of section 1.5.
5. **Chance.** The same tests for chance, calibration on an outside list, the precise constants and the look-elsewhere effect, are applied to both (section 17).
6. **Downstream.** An alignment stays only if nothing downstream of it contradicts a measurement: the author's behavior rule, applied to every theory alike.

### 3.2 The difference of squares as a measure of imbalance

Her starting observation: “Instead of adding the squares of legs to find the hypotenuse √(a²+b²), I looked at the difference between them √(a²−b²) … The Pythagorean theorem asks for the size of the space a triangle occupies. The difference-of-squares asks for the size of the imbalance between the legs.”

For a right triangle with legs a ≥ b she writes four quantities:

- **c**, the hypotenuse: c² = a² + b².
- **d**, the virtual leg: d² = a² − b² = (a − b)(a + b). It is a leg of the “virtual triangle” whose hypotenuse is a and whose other leg is b.
- **h**, the leftover: h = a − b, what is left of the long side after the short side is taken off.
- **M**, the triangle's area: M = ½ab.

She draws each square as a grid of dots and reads its identities off the drawings:

1. **The gnomon.** Take a b × b square out of an a × a square; the L-shaped remainder slides into an h × (a + b) rectangle, so d² = h(a + b).
2. **The tilted square.** Four copies of the triangle inside a square of side a + b leave a tilted inner square of area c², so (a + b)² = c² + 4M.
3. **Sum and difference.** c² − d² = 2b² and c² + d² = 2a², so c² = 2a² − d². Halved, ½c² − ½d² = b², a difference of two half-squares, which are the areas of 45° triangles. Doubled, 2c² − 2d² = (2b)².
4. **The ladder.** d², a² and c² are always b² apart: three squares in equal steps.
5. **Path against chord.** At 45° the diagonal of the square on a is f = a√2 = (sin 45° + cos 45°)a. In general sin θ + cos θ = (a + b)/c is the ratio of the two-leg path to the straight path, and it equals √2 only at 45°.

**Worked example: the 3-4-5 triangle.**

- **The squares.** With a = 4, b = 3, c = 5 the dot squares hold 16, 9 and 25.
- **The virtual leg.** d² = 16 − 9 = 7 = (4 − 3)(4 + 3), drawn as an L of seven dots straightened into a strip.
- **The identities.** c² − d² = 18 = 2 · 9, and c² = 2 · 16 − 7 = 25.
- **The half-areas.** a²/2 = 8, b²/2 = 9/2, c²/2 = 25/2 and d²/2 = 7/2; sin A = 4/5 and sin B = 3/5.

**The lock.** In the square of side a cut by its 45° diagonal, she splits the half above the diagonal, of area a²/2, into three pieces:

- the corner triangle b²/2, with legs b on the two outer sides;
- a thin triangle e²/2 between the hypotenuse c and that corner triangle;
- the remainder, d²/2 − e²/2, between the hypotenuse and the diagonal.

For 3-4-5 her “e breakdown” cuts the thin triangle, whose sides are a − b = 1, b√2 = 3√2 and c = 5, along its altitude 3/5 into two right triangles:

- their areas are ½(4/5)(3/5) + ½(3/5)(21/5) = 12/50 + 63/50 = 3/2, so e² = 3;
- the remainder is then 7/2 − 3/2 = 2.

Her rules: “d&e give the original container information and lock a triangle's angles into position”, and “If (d²/2 − e²/2) is present, the angles of the inner triangle can't be configured any other way. Scaling info is retained.”

*Proof (added).* Place the square with corners (0, 0) and (a, a), the corner triangle at (a − b, a), (a, a), (a, a − b), and the hypotenuse from (0, a) to (a, a − b). The thin triangle then has area ½b(a − b), so e² = b(a − b), d² − e² = a(a − b), and d² − 2e² = (a − b)². Hence

```latex
a-b=\sqrt{d^{2}-2e^{2}},\qquad b=\frac{e^{2}}{\sqrt{d^{2}-2e^{2}}},\qquad a=\frac{d^{2}-e^{2}}{\sqrt{d^{2}-2e^{2}}},\qquad \frac{a}{b}=\frac{d^{2}-e^{2}}{e^{2}}
```

The pair (d², e²) fixes the ratio a/b, which sets the angles, and also the size, which is the container: for (7, 3) it returns a − b = 1, b = 3 and a = 4. Her lock holds, with a proof.

Two notes on her letters. The same page also writes e² = (½b)², which gives 9/4 rather than 3 for b = 3; the area construction gives 3 and is the one used here. On another page she writes e for the leftover a − b; this paper writes h for that.

**The 4 × 4 square on a 3 × 3 grid.**

- **The grid.** Since 4 = 3 × 4/3, a 4 × 4 square is a 3 × 3 grid of cells of side 4/3. Each cell has area 16/9 = (1 + ⅓)² = 1 + 2 · ⅓ + ⅓², so “length & area embed the original container (3×3).”
- **The box rule.** In the 3 × 3 box, n² = 2n + (n² − 2n) with n² − 2n = ⅓ of the area, true only at n = 3.

**Keep the factors.** Her principle, from two pages: “simplifying too soon loses info” and “simplifying root calculations also loses some information.”

- **Fractions.** (5/3)² = 9/9 + 12/9 + 4/9 keeps the parts 1 and ⅔ visible.
- **Roots.** A triangle with a = b = 2 has c = √8, which “can be 2√2 or √2³”. The second form “can now be used to scale the entire root family”, and √(2³) is the volume of a cube of edge √2: “lengths and area combo configured as cube.”

**SSA and the inverse grid.** “d&e create an SSA situation there all right triangles have 4 potential answers (dual, null, & 1 solution), and since all non-right triangles can be split into 2 right triangles SSA is always possible with the container info.” Classical trigonometry gives two sides and a non-included angle no triangle, one, or two (the ambiguous case); her four potential answers are these outcomes with the dual pair counted twice. She also notes that “a √2 × √2 grid is inverse of 1 × 1 grid”: the half-diagonal of a √2 square is 1, and that of a unit square is √2/2.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The gnomon is Euclid's *Elements* II.5. The tilted-square proof is the rearrangement drawn in Zhao Shuang's third-century commentary on the *Zhoubi Suanjing*. The equal-step ladder of squares is Fibonacci's congruum (*Liber Quadratorum*, 1225), and Fermat proved its step is never a square, so a, b, c and d are never all whole. The triangle area is Euclid I.41.
- **N.** Treating the difference of squares as a triangle's imbalance and tabulating it, the e construction and the (d, e) lock were not found in the literature searched.
- **Closest prior framing.** Wildberger's rational trigonometry (2005) also works in squared lengths and avoids square roots. Mansfield and Wildberger (2017) read the Old Babylonian tablet Plimpton 322 as “a completely different form of trigonometry that does not need the familiar modern concept of angles”, built on exact ratios of sides.
- **D.** None in the mathematics.

### 3.3 The Tifinchi Trig Method: a triangle becomes motion

She built a teaching sequence that plots a sine wave from one triangle before any ratio is named: “Tifinchi Trig is NOT intended to replace the Unit circle. Its purpose is to help students plot motion and understand the concepts of the Unit circle prior to angle ratio conversions.”

**Section 1: one 45° triangle onto a sine wave.**

1. **Compare.** A triangle compares two pieces of information. Collapse the legs of the 45° triangle together and “neither has lost or gained anything”.
2. **Plot and normalise.** Plot the base triangle with angles 45°, 45° and 90° and legs a = b = 3, with the 45° vertex B at (0, 0), A = (3, 3) and C = (3, 0). Multiply by 3 to go to Tifinchi Trig and divide by 3 to go to the unit circle; in general divide by the hypotenuse. The hypotenuse is 3√2, and scaling changes only the whole number while the root family stays.
3. **Rotate.** Turn the triangle 90° about the origin instead of flipping it between quadrants. The legs swap roles, which is “the 2D version of Manhattan steps”.
4. **Plot the wave.** The four 90° positions are enough to plot a full sine wave from 0° to 360°.

**Section 2: precision before new angles.**

1. **Half and double angles.** These come from rotation: moving the triangle back by half gives new points. Radians are written over 4 and then reduced, so the quarter step shows. Rotating in 45° steps gives eight positions per cycle, “which occurs in analyzing sound, compressing images, binary data”.
2. **The 30° and 60° triangles.** They keep their own sizes, the 30° smaller and the 60° larger, and are normalised to add points to the 45° wave.
3. **The unit circle.** Then it is taught: “the Tifinchi Trig method has been normalizing the Unit Circle values all along.”

**Her reason for 3:** “3 is the smallest integer that locks increments of 45 degrees into integers for the coordinates.” As stated this needs a qualification (added): any whole leg gives whole-number corners at the four 90° positions, and no whole leg gives them at the in-between 45° positions. What singles out 3 in her system is a sector: a 40° (2π/9) slice of the radius-3 circle has area ½ · 3² · (2π/9) = π, the area of the whole unit circle, which ties the two scales together. The other link is the one-third grid of section 3.2.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The sine as the shadow of a turning radius goes back to Euler's *Introductio* (1748); the radian to Cotes (1722).
- **N.** The sequence itself, rotation before ratio with the 30° and 60° triangles normalised onto the 45° wave, was not found as a published method. It shares rational trigonometry's choice to postpone angles (Wildberger 2005).

#### 3.3.1 Why 3, a ladder of angles, and the gap between the paths (added)

**Why 3 keeps appearing.** Two reasons, both on her pages, back her choice of 3 (section 3.3).

- **The grid.** 3 is the only square grid whose diagonal and the cells on either side of it are equal in number: n = n(n − 1)/2 only at n = 3. This is her “3×3 box only”.
- **The coordinates.** 3 is the smallest leg that makes the corners of the 30° and 60° triangles fraction-free: (3, √3) and (3, 3√3). Leg 1 gives √3/3. Her phase circle uses exactly (3, √3).

**A ladder of difficulty.** Angles whose coordinates stay in one root family compose without new roots:

1. Steps of 90° need only whole numbers.
2. 45° needs √2.
3. 30° and 60° need √3.
4. 15°, 75° and 105° mix √2 and √3: cos 75° = (√6 − √2)/4.
5. 22.5° nests one root inside another: cos 22.5° = √(2 + √2)/2.

Her teaching sequence puts 22.5° before 30° and 60° (section 3.3), while the ladder makes 22.5° the harder step. Her pyramid page's 7π/12, which is 105°, sits on the mixing step.

**The gap between the paths is the container's surface.**

- **The ratio.** The ratio of the two-leg path to the straight one is itself a sine wave: (a + b)/c = sin θ + cos θ = √2 sin(θ + 45°). It is 1 on the axes and √2 at 45°. Her “90° out of phase” describes the order of the moves, legs in sequence against one rotation, not this ratio, which is shifted by 45°.
- **In two dimensions.** The squared gap is (a + b)² − c² = 2ab = 4M.
- **In three dimensions.** It is (a + b + c)² − (a² + b² + c²) = 2(ab + bc + ca), the surface area of the box: 6 for the unit cube.

So the squared gap between the step path and the straight path measures the container's surface: a measured form of her “container information”.

### 3.4 The two tables and root families

Her term: “Root Families, a term I coined to explain the pattern I noticed, uses the exact same formula √(a²−b²) to ask ‘If we calculate the difference of many possibilities, is there a pattern?’”

**Definition.** Write each cell √N with N in prime powers, such as √20 = √(2²·5), rather than simplified. The part left after every squared factor is removed (here 5) names the family. “A ‘Root Family’ is a batch of triangles whose results share the same root, sometimes even the same answer from a different shape.”

**Three examples from her charts.**

- **The √3 family** of the difference table: (2,1) √3, (4,2) √12, (6,3) √27, (7,1) √48, (8,4) √48 and (10,5) √75. The line a = 2b is the 30-60-90 triangle (b : b√3 : 2b). The cell (7,1) gives the same √48 as (8,4) with a different shape (1 : 4√3 : 7): “not simplifying actually helps us see the full structure they originate from.”
- **The zero family.** “The 45 degree triangle (where a=b so √(a²−b²)=√0) is the first real ‘special’ triangle. It belongs to the zero difference family, the pure symmetry family (no loss, no gain on either leg).”
- **Prime factor families.** (3,1) gives √8 = √(2³); (5,3) gives √16 = √(2⁴); (4,2) gives √12 = √(2²·3).

Her summary: “triples classify shape existence and root families classify shape behavior.”

Tables 3 and 4 give her charts for legs 1 to 10, in her factored form. Where N is at most 118, the cell also carries the element with atomic number N, as on her *Root Elements* page.

**What the tables hold.** The author's chart of origins sets each fact beside its source, and every fact below was checked cell by cell.

1. **Which numbers enter the sum table.** N is a sum of two squares exactly when every prime of the form 4k + 3 divides it to an even power (Girard 1625; Fermat 1640; proved by Euler 1747–55). So 45 = 3²·5, 90 and 117 appear, but no cell carries a single 3, 7 or 11.
2. **Repeats in the sum table.** 50 = (7,1) = (5,5), 65 = (8,1) = (7,4) and 85 = (9,2) = (7,6) are different triangles with one hypotenuse. A product of two sums of two squares is a sum of two squares in two ways (Diophantus; Brahmagupta; Fibonacci 1225).
3. **Which numbers enter the difference table.** a² − b² = (a − b)(a + b) has two factors of equal parity, so it is never 2 more than a multiple of 4: 2, 6, 10 and 14 never appear. Its repeats (15, 24, 32, 45, 48) come from different factor pairs, as 48 = (7,1) = (8,4).
4. **Whole-number cells are Pythagorean triples.** In the sum table, 25 at (4,3) and 100 at (8,6); in the difference table, 9 at (5,4), 16 at (5,3), 36 at (10,8) and 64 at (10,6). The difference table finds the same triangles from the hypotenuse side: (5,4) → 3 is the 3-4-5 that (4,3) → 5 finds (Plimpton 322; Euclid X.29, Lemma 1).
5. **Numbers in neither table** (6, 14, 22, 30, …) need three squares. N is a sum of three squares unless N = 4ᵃ(8b + 7) (Legendre 1798; Gauss 1801). Every number that needs four squares is odd or a multiple of 4, so the difference table reaches it. Her reading: the gap between the tables is a “3D tier”, reached by the dimension converter of section 3.7, and a 4D tier is never needed.
6. **Lines from the corner.** Cells with the same ratio a : b share one angle (Euclid VI.4). The line a = b is the 45° line: the √2 family in the sum table, the zero family in the difference table. The line a = 2b is the 26.57° √5 line of the sum table and the 30-60-90 √3 line of the difference table. Her chart adds: “Root families do NOT share angles”. A family groups the irrational part of a length, not a shape.
7. **One complex square** (added). (a + bi)² = (a² − b²) + 2ab·i, with modulus a² + b². The difference table, a third table of 2ab, and the sum table are one squaring, and squaring doubles the angle: (2 + i)² = 3 + 4i, the 3-4-5. These are the three quadratic forms of Wildberger's chromogeometry (2008).

**Power and signals.** Her chart reads the tables as a power calculator.

- **The sum table** gives a total from two parts: apparent power from real and reactive power, S = √(P² + Q²), and impedance, |Z| = √(R² + X²).
- **The difference table** gives a missing part from the total and one part: Q = √(S² − P²), X = √(Z² − R²), and the damped frequency ω\_d = √(ω₀² − γ²), so ω₀ = 5 and γ = 3 give 4.
- **Clean readings** are the whole-number cells. P = 8 and Q = 6 give S = 10 and a power factor of 0.8, the standard generator rating; P = 3 and Q = 4 give a power factor of 0.6.
- **Each line from the corner** has one power factor: 0.707 at a = b and 0.894 at a = 2b in the sum table.

The sources are Heaviside (1886, the word impedance), Kennelly (1893, impedance as a right triangle) and Steinmetz (1893, complex numbers for alternating current).

**Fibonacci in the tables.**

- **Option 1.** Run the recurrence Fₙ = Fₙ₋₁ + Fₙ₋₂ in either direction: “This formula can be reversed to expand outward instead of winding inward.” Run backwards, it passes 0 and alternates sign.
- **Option 3.** Read the sequence off the two tables:
  - 1² − 0² = 1, 1² + 1² = 2, 2² − 1² = 3, 2² + 1² = 5, 3² − 1² = 8, 3² + 2² = 13;
  - 5² − 2² = 21, 5² + 3² = 34, 8² − 3² = 55, 8² + 5² = 89, 13² − 5² = 144, 13² + 8² = 233.
- **Option 2** is a fill-in chart, laid out with its entries computed in section 3.4.1.

In standard notation, Option 3 is two identities:

- **Sum cells** with consecutive Fibonacci legs give the odd-place terms: Fₙ² + Fₙ₊₁² = F₂ₙ₊₁.
- **Difference cells** with legs two places apart give the even-place terms: Fₙ₊₁² − Fₙ₋₁² = F₂ₙ. Here h = Fₙ and a + b = Lₙ, a Lucas number, so d² = FₙLₙ.

The sum cells √5, √13, √34 and √89 hide whole triangles: 5, 13, 34 and 89 are the hypotenuses of (3,4,5), (5,12,13), (16,30,34) and (39,80,89). One chart therefore holds both of Fibonacci's main contributions, the sequence and the congruum.

The (2,1) cell holds the golden ratio (added): with φ = (1 + √5)/2,

- φ + 1/φ = √5 and φ − 1/φ = 1;
- φ² + φ⁻² = 3 and φ² − φ⁻² = √5.

**Standing.**

- **M.** All of the number theory is classical, as cited. Root families are the square classes of number theory: a length √N with N = k²m lies in the field ℚ(√m). The Fibonacci identities are standard (Lucas 1878; Vajda 1989).
- **N.** The name “root family”, its use to group triangles by their imbalance, the tier reading of the gap between the tables, and the power-calculator reading of the chart were not found in the literature searched. Her own claim, that “this geometric framing does not appear in any standard literature”, agrees with that search.
- **D.** None.

#### 3.4.1 Fibonacci: the second option

Her *Fib Fam Fun* (Taylor 2026g) gives three methods for “the SAME Fibonacci Sequence”. Options 1 and 3 are in section 3.4. Option 2 is a chart that fills itself: “Use a Fibonacci Chart to find a Fibonacci sequence from the Hypotenuse and Difference tables. Start with n₁ = 1.” Table 5 lays it out as she does, with the entries computed.

**Table 5. Option 2.**

| Row | Difference cell | Sum cell |
| --- | --- | --- |
| n₁ = 1 | n₁² − (n₁ − 1)² = 1² − 0² = n₁ = 1 | n₁² + n₁² = n₂ = 2 |
| n₂ = 2 | n₂² − n₁² = n₃ = 3 | n₂² + n₁² = n₄ = 5 |
| n₃ = 3 | n₃² − n₁² = n₅ = 8 | n₃² + n₂² = n₆ = 13 |
| n₄ = 5 | n₄² − n₂² = n₇ = 21 | n₄² + n₃² = n₈ = 34 |
| n₅ = 8 | n₅² − n₃² = n₉ = 55 | n₅² + n₄² = n₁₀ = 89 |
| n₆ = 13 | n₆² − n₄² = n₁₁ = 144 | n₆² + n₅² = n₁₂ = 233 |

**What the chart is (added).** Her nₖ is Fₖ₊₁. Each row takes one term and returns two terms twice as far along:

- nₖ² − nₖ₋₂² = n₂ₖ₋₁, that is Fₖ₊₁² − Fₖ₋₁² = F₂ₖ;
- nₖ² + nₖ₋₁² = n₂ₖ, that is Fₖ₊₁² + Fₖ² = F₂ₖ₊₁.

Six rows reach the twelfth term, so the chart doubles the index at every row. These are the doubling identities (Lucas 1878; Vajda 1989), which compute Fₙ in about log₂ n steps rather than n. The chart's row labels are its own earlier answers, so it needs nothing but its start, n₁ = 1. Of Option 1 she writes that the formula “can be reversed to expand outward instead of winding inward”; Option 2 expands outward through the two tables.

Two more facts from the same chart (added):

- **The line from the corner.** The cells (Fₙ₊₁, Fₙ) lie on lines whose angle at the long leg tends to arctan(1/φ) = 31.7175°. Its double is arctan 2 = 63.4349°.
- **Inverted legs.** 1/Fₙ² + 1/Fₙ₊₁² = F₂ₙ₊₁/(FₙFₙ₊₁)², exactly.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The identities.
- **N.** Option 2 as a chart that fills itself through the two tables.

#### 3.4.2 One complex number per cell

For z = a + bi:

- **The sum table.** zz̄ = a² + b².
- **The difference table and a third.** z² = (a² − b²) + 2ab·i: the difference table, and a third table of 2ab, which is 4M on the Rosetta page. This holds in all 55 cells, and (a² + b²)² = (a² − b²)² + (2ab)². Squaring doubles the angle: (2, 1) gives 3-4-5, (3, 2) gives 5-12-13, and (8, 5) gives 39-80-89.
- **The turn.** Multiplying by i is a 90° turn in one step.

**Three behaviours.**

- A constant sum rides a circle or sphere: rotation and bound orbits.
- A constant difference rides a hyperbola: relativity and escape.
- A zero difference, the 45° line, is the boundary between them: light, the parabolic orbit, the turnaround.

**Inverted legs.**

- **Root families survive.** The legs (1/a, 1/b) return both tables' roots divided by ab, so root families survive inversion in all 55 cells.
- **The angle mirrors across 45°.** In circuit terms:
  - a resistance and a reactance in series give |Z| = c;
  - in parallel they give the altitude ab/c, since 1/a² + 1/b² = 1/(ab/c)²;
  - for (4, 3), 5 Ω in series and 2.4 Ω in parallel, with power factors 0.8 and 0.6, and 0.8² + 0.6² = 1.
- **Repeats split.** The value repeats of the sum table (50, 65, 85) split under inversion: families are the structure, and the repeats a coincidence of scale.
- **The flip through the unit circle.** 1/z̄ = z/|z|² keeps the direction and inverts the size. Adding a point at infinity makes the plane a sphere, on which the unit circle is the fixed equator while the centre and infinity swap.

**Inside out is negative.** Flipping every sign has determinant (−1)ⁿ:

- a rotation by 180° in two dimensions (i × i = −1);
- a mirror in three, which is not one of the cube's 24 rotations;
- a rotation again in four (section 4.4).

The weak force tells a process from its mirror image (Wu et al. 1957).

**The cube against its inside-out octahedron.** The side-2 cube's distances pair with the octahedron's under r → 1/r: 1 with 1, √2 with 1/√2, √3 with 1/√3, and the four-dimensional corner's 2 with ½. Each pair multiplies to 1, and each figure has 6, 12 and 8 points with the centre, 27 in all. The tesseract's 16 corners and 8 axis points form the self-dual 24-cell (section 4.6).

**Standing.**

- **M.** Gauss's complex numbers, chromogeometry's three forms (Wildberger 2008), circuit duality, the Riemann sphere and Wu's experiment.
- **N.** Their reading as one cell, its inverse and its inside-out.

### 3.5 The elements in the two tables

Her *Root Elements* page tags every whole-number cell of Tables 3 and 4 up to 118 with the element of that atomic number. With the rules of section 3.4, every element can be placed, which extends her page to all 118:

- **Squares (10):** H, Be, F, S, Mn, Kr, In, Gd, Tl, Fm.
- **Sum table only (12):** He, Ne, Ar, Fe, Se, Sn, Ce, W, Pb, Th, Cf, Sg. All are 2 more than a multiple of 4.
- **Both tables (28):** B, O, Al, Cl, Ca, Cu, Ge, Rb, Zr, Nb, Rh, Te, I, Pm, Tb, Er, Hf, Ta, Hg, At, Ac, Bk, Md, Rf, Mt, Nh, Lv, Ts.
- **Difference table only (50):** every other odd Z and multiple of 4. A prime 4k + 3 has only the gnomon route ((Z + 1)/2)² − ((Z − 1)/2)², so lawrencium is 52² − 51² and gold 40² − 39².
- **Three squares only (18):** C, Si, Ti, Zn, Sr, Mo, Pd, Xe, Sm, Dy, Yb, Pt, Rn, Pu, No, Ds, Fl, Og. Each is a sum cell plus one leg, the converter's step:
  - C 6 = B(2,1) + 1²; Si 14 = Al(3,2) + 1²; Ti 22 = Ar(3,3) + 2²; Zn 30 = Cu(5,2) + 1²;
  - Sr 38 = Se(5,3) + 2²; Mo 42 = Nb(5,4) + 1²; Pd 46 = Rh(6,3) + 1²; Xe 54 = Sn(5,5) + 2²;
  - Sm 62 = Pm(6,5) + 1²; Dy 66 = Sn(5,5) + 4²; Yb 70 = Pm(6,5) + 3²; Pt 78 = W(7,5) + 2²;
  - Rn 86 = Pm(6,5) + 5²; Pu 94 = At(7,6) + 3²; No 102 = Cf(7,7) + 2²; Ds 110 = At(7,6) + 5²;
  - Fl 114 = Cf(7,7) + 4²; Og 118 = Ts(9,6) + 1².

With differences allowed no element needs four squares, so her 4D tier is empty, checked to Z = 20,000.

On her page as printed, with legs 1 to 10:

- 75 elements sit in the two tables;
- 32 more are reached with a third leg of 10 or less;
- 11 (V, Ga, Ag, Lu, Au, Fr, U, Am, Lr, Rg, Cn) need legs beyond 10.

**What the tiers do not do.** The tier is a property of the integer Z, set by Z mod 4 and its prime factors, and it carries no chemistry; this was checked three ways (added).

- **Madelung exceptions.** The twenty exceptions to the filling rule fall 10 to 20% into every tier, as a random 17% would.
- **Unstable elements.** The elements with no stable isotope fall 10 to 36% into every tier.
- **Noble gases.** They split three sum-only (He, Ne, Ar), one square (Kr) and three three-square (Xe, Rn, Og). That split is the parity of the shell structure, not chemistry. The period lengths 2n² are the diagonal cells (n, n), and xenon is krypton plus one 18-electron period, 54 = 6² + (3,3).

**Standing.**

- **M.** The theorems (Fermat and Euler; Legendre and Gauss; Lagrange 1770; Euclid II.5) are classical.
- **N.** The labelling, and the observation that the four-square tier is empty once differences are allowed, a short corollary of Legendre's theorem, were not found stated.
- **D.** None; the tables predict no chemistry, as checked.

### 3.6 Dual solutions, and Manhattan against Euclidean distance

**Dual solutions.** “Have you ever wondered why the equation x² = 1 has two solutions (±1), while both 1² and √1 × √1 are taught as producing only +1? … using only the positive branch accidentally hides the deeper structure. Every root calculation inherently contains the possibility of two real solutions or no real solution.”

She gives two demonstrations:

- **The paper strip.** A strip coloured on one side can be joined with the colour outside, with it inside, or with a half twist into a Möbius strip, “the symmetrically balanced, one-answer solution (because it has one continuous surface, the color ‘side’ appears evenly everywhere)”.
- **The √2 grid.** Draw a triangle on graph paper, set the grid's scale to √2, and ask what did and did not change.

In a later paper: “For every root, we can surmise the possibility of a positive or negative answer. This means every triangle has a counterpart that forms a perfect square when paired along the hypotenuse.” (Added: two copies joined along the hypotenuse make the a × b rectangle, a square when a = b.)

**Manhattan and Euclidean distance.** “Manhattan Distance measures the distance by following how many orthogonal steps it took to get to a new location and Euclidean distance measures the shortest path to that same location (the hypotenuse). These two descriptions produce the same final location, but they are 90° out of phase. You cannot take the Manhattan forward-and-sideways step at the same time, because orthogonal motions add sequentially. Euclidean rotation captures what Manhattan has to do in two moves.”

- **Each dimension adds a phase.** “Each time you add a perpendicular dimension, you add a 90° phase to Manhattan measurements. Each new phase adds a new squared term, producing the next root family.”
- **Loops.** A single triangle cannot make the two walkers line up, but “it is possible to make a minimum set of three perfect 45 degree triangles loop back if they are 90 degrees out-of-phase with each other”.
- **Phase over boundary.** “phase and frequency could be the primary path of three dimensions rather than the boundaries (length, width, and height).”

Computed with these ideas (added):

- **The average cost.** A straight unit step at angle θ costs |cos θ| + |sin θ| in legs. Averaged over all directions that is 4/π = 1.2732 in two dimensions and 3/2 in three.
- **The worst case.** The worst case is the diagonal: √2, √3 and 2 in two, three and four dimensions (the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality).
- **The number of paths.** An a × b grid is crossed by C(a + b, a) shortest leg-paths, all equally long in the legs' own measure.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The taxicab metric is Minkowski's, and its geometry is set out by Krause (1975). The principal-root convention is standard. The one-sided strip is Möbius's (published 1865).
- **N.** Reading the two metrics as 90° out of phase, and each added dimension as an added phase, was not found.
- **D.** None.

### 3.7 The dimension converter and the root ladder

Her converter: “Dimension Converter (Nested Pythagorean)”,

```latex
\sqrt{(a^{2}+b^{2})+f^{2}}=\sqrt{a^{2}+(b^{2}+f^{2})}
```

and: “Nested Pythagorean usually is used for triplets but this also gives more information.”

**On the side-2 cube** (a = b = f = 2):

- **The faces.** Each face diagonal is √8 = 2√2 = √(2³), and she labels the three m, n and o: “motion (x)”, “motion (y)” and “motion (z)”.
- **The ladder.** “1D = 2 = line, 2D = 2√2 = plane, 3D = 2√3 = cube.” The triangle with legs 2 (a line) and 2√2 (a plane) has hypotenuse √12 = 2√3 (a cube).
- **Her two rules.** “a, b, f are physical lengths with 2 stages of perpendicular turns”, and “m, n, o are summaries of distance moved (so motion).”

**Its radians form.** A point on a circle of radius r at angle θ is (r cos θ, r sin θ). Adding a perpendicular axis a gives L = √(a² + r²(cos²θ + sin²θ)) = √(a² + r²), so motion on a circle plugs into three dimensions whole.

**The ladder of roots.** “Root families are the intrinsic dimensional ladder”:

- the square √(1² + 1²) = √2;
- the cube √(1² + (√2)²) = √3;
- and the 2 × 1 difference triangle √(2² − 1²) = √3 lands on the same rung.

Run axis by axis, P₁ = √(x² + y²), P₂ = √(P₁² + z²) and P₃ = √(P₂² + w²), with w a fourth space axis, so the unit diagonals are √2, √3 and √4 = 2, “a whole number, twice the edge”. Measured from the centre of a cube of half-side K the rungs are Rₖ = K√k: K, K√2, K√3 and 2K, to a face, an edge midpoint, a corner and a 4D corner. The last equals the edge.

**Time as a difference.** Her chart sets the spacetime interval in the same frame: D² = (ct)² − P₂² = (ct + P₂)(ct − P₂). A moving observer multiplies one factor by k and divides the other by k, so the product is unchanged. Reading D as her virtual leg d, with ct as the long leg, is her interpretation; the algebra is Minkowski's (1908) and the factor-k argument is Milne's and Bondi's.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The n-dimensional distance formula follows Grassmann (1844), Cayley (1845) and Schläfli (1852).
- **N.** Reading m, n and o as summaries of motion, the converter as the step that adds a phase, and the interval's D as the virtual leg were not found.
- **D.** None.

#### 3.7.1 The 2D-to-3D converter, and the leg it must be given

Her rule: going from two dimensions to three always needs one leg the two-dimensional data cannot supply. Each case below is proved; Table 6 at the end separates what is exact from what is a reading. The script appC.py (SHA-256 b19a7d28343721a5145212bb242ff9a8e0467218a09d40864f85759dc9707109) recomputes every number here and in sections 3.4.2, 3.7.2, 4.4.1 and 4.9.1 with the PDG 2026 inputs, and each symbolic statement was proved by reducing the difference of its two sides to zero.

**1. A two-dimensional view loses exactly the diagonal.** The Clarke matrix

```latex
K=\tfrac{2}{3}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\tfrac12&-\tfrac12\\[2pt]0&\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}&-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad K\,(1,1,1)^{\mathsf T}=(0,0)^{\mathsf T}
```

has rows that sum to zero, so it sends (1, 1, 1) to the origin. It has rank 2, so its kernel is exactly that line. Any amount γ on the diagonal gives the same two-dimensional point: γ = 0, +0.3 and −0.5 all return (0.7, −0.4) for the same input. So the third leg must be supplied from outside: a three-wire system forces γ = 0, and a four-wire system lets γ flow in the neutral.

**2. The Scott-T makes three phases from two.** The main winding carries V∠0° with a centre tap. The teaser, from that tap, carries V∠90° scaled by √3/2, so

- V\_A = j(√3/2)V, V\_B = −V/2, V\_C = V/2;
- V\_AB = V/2 + j(√3/2)V, V\_BC = −V, V\_CA = V/2 − j(√3/2)V.

Each line voltage has magnitude √(¼ + ¾)V = V, and since ½ + j√3/2 = e^(jπ/3), each is the last turned by 120°: a balanced three-phase set. The teaser ratio √3/2 is the unit cube's centre-to-corner distance.

**3. Three-phase power is flat** (section 7.2), and **third harmonics live on the diagonal.** Phase n sits at −2πn/3, so its third harmonic sits at −2πn ≡ 0: the three third harmonics are in phase, pure (1, 1, 1), invisible to the Clarke view and added in the neutral. With 30% third harmonic in each phase:

- the circle keeps radius 1.000000;
- the diagonal swings ±0.300 at three times the frequency;
- the neutral peaks at 0.900;
- from a balanced source the diagonal carries zero average power: it oscillates both ways and delivers nothing.

**4. Three roots.**

| Ratio | Value | Proof |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Peak over RMS of one phase | √2 | the mean of sin² over a period is ½ |
| Line over phase voltage | √3 | \|1 − e^(−j2π/3)\|² = (3/2)² + (√3/2)² = 3 |
| Single-phase power, peak over average | 2 = √4 | power goes as sin²: peak 1, average ½ |

**5. Four dimensions is the special case.** The centre-to-corner distance of the unit n-cube is √n/2, which equals the edge only at n = 4.

**6. The Higgs mechanism fills the third leg.** The Higgs field has four real components, and its vacuum sits on a 3-sphere of radius v at ⟨H⟩ = (0, v/√2), with v = (√2 G\_F)^(−1/2) = 246.220 GeV.

- **The mass matrix.** From |(gTᵃWᵃ + g′YB)⟨H⟩|², for (W₁, W₂, W₃, B):

  ```latex
  M^{2}=\frac{v^{2}}{4}\begin{pmatrix}g^{2}&0&0&0\\0&g^{2}&0&0\\0&0&g^{2}&-gg'\\0&0&-gg'&g'^{2}\end{pmatrix}
  ```

  Its eigenvalues are g²v²/4 twice (W⁺ and W⁻), (g² + g′²)v²/4 (the Z) and 0 (the photon).
- **Why the photon gets nothing.** The W₃–B block is (v²/4)(g, −g′)ᵀ(g, −g′): rank one, determinant zero. Its zero direction is A = (g′W₃ + gB)/√(g² + g′²). The reason is that the vacuum's charge is Q = T₃ + Y = −½ + ½ = 0.
- **The triangle.** m\_Z² = m\_W² + (g′v/2)² exactly: a right triangle with legs m\_W = 80.3625 GeV and g′v/2 = 43.094 GeV, and hypotenuse m\_Z = 91.1880 GeV. Its angle, 28.20°, is the weak mixing angle. At tree level g = 0.65277, g′ = 0.35005 and e = 0.30849.
- **The count, 12 = 12.** Before: four carriers with two legs each (8) and four Higgs components (4). After: three massive carriers with three legs each (9), the photon with two, and one Higgs boson. SU(2) × U(1) has four generators and keeps one, the charge, so three break. By Goldstone's theorem each broken direction is one Higgs angle on the 3-sphere, and each becomes the third leg of W⁺, W⁻ or Z. The radial direction left over is the Higgs boson.

**7. The filled leg rides the hyperbola, and the Higgs boson keeps it bounded.**

- **The polarisation.** ε\_L = (p, 0, 0, E)/m has ε\_L · ε\_L = (p² − E²)/m² = −1 and ε\_L · k = 0. With rapidity η, E/m = cosh η and p/m = sinh η. The two transverse legs turn on a circle (helicity ±1).

  | W energy | p/m, E/m | cosh² − sinh² |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 100 GeV | 0.741, 1.244 | 1.000000000 |
  | 1,000 GeV | 12.403, 12.444 | 1.000000000 |
  | 10,000 GeV | 124.432, 124.436 | 1.000000000 |
- **The amplitude.** With V = λ(H†H − v²/2)² and H = (w⁺, (v + h + iz)/√2), the couplings are w⁺w⁻z² → λ, hw⁺w⁻ → 2λv, hz² → λv, and m\_h² = 2λv². Then

  ```latex
  \mathcal M(w^{+}w^{-}\to zz)=-2\lambda-\frac{(2\lambda v)^{2}}{s-m_h^{2}}=-\frac{m_h^{2}}{v^{2}}\,\frac{s}{s-m_h^{2}}
  ```

  For s ≪ m\_h² it grows as s/v², the case without a Higgs boson. For s ≫ m\_h² it levels at −2λ. By the equivalence theorem (Cornwall, Levin and Tiktopoulos 1974; Chanowitz and Gaillard 1985) it equals longitudinal W⁺W⁻ → ZZ at high energy.
- **Unitarity.** The partial wave a₀ = 𝓜/16π must stay within ±½.

  | √s | a₀ without a Higgs boson | a₀ with it |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 300 GeV | +0.0295 | −0.00622 |
  | 1,000 GeV | +0.3282 | −0.00522 |
  | 3,000 GeV | +2.9534, broken | −0.00515 |
  | 10,000 GeV | +32.8159, broken | −0.00514 |
  - **Without a Higgs boson**, unitarity breaks at √(8π)·v = 1,234.4 GeV.
  - **With one**, a₀ levels at −2λ/16π = −0.00514, with λ = m\_H²/2v² = 0.12914.
  - **The bound.** Lee, Quigg and Thacker (1977) bound the Higgs mass at √(16π/3)·v = 1,007.9 GeV; it is measured at 125.13 GeV.

  The level −2λ uses the same λ that the running of section 9.3 follows, which turns negative near 5 × 10¹⁰ GeV where the gauge terms are as large. That link is qualitative.

**Table 6. Exact against interpretation.**

| Her picture | Standard result | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2D to 3D must be given the other leg | the Clarke kernel is the diagonal (1, 1, 1) | exact, proved |
| Fill it in phases | the Scott-T, with its √3/2 teaser | exact, proved |
| The diagonal oscillates both ways | third harmonics are pure (1, 1, 1) and carry zero average power | exact, proved |
| The action circle absorbs at √2 | peak over RMS is √2 | the identity is exact; “absorbs” is a reading |
| The physical holds at √4 | single-phase peak over average is 2; the four-dimensional centre-to-corner equals the edge | the identities are exact; “holds” is a reading |
| From the legs, the straight path looks zigzag | the legs are at most √n times the straight path; C(a + b, a) equal staircases (section 3.6) | exact, proved |
| Carriers go from 2D to 3D | two legs become three; 12 = 12 | exact, standard |
| The leg is filled by phases | the three Goldstone angles of the 3-sphere | exact, standard |
| The photon stays unfilled | the rank-one block; the vacuum's charge is 0 | exact, proved |
| The third leg rides the hyperbola | ε\_L = (sinh η, cosh η) | exact, proved |
| The Higgs boson keeps the leg bounded | 𝓜 → −2λ | exact at tree level |

**Standing.**

- **M.** Every proof is standard: the Clarke transform (Clarke 1943), the Scott-T (Scott, Westinghouse, 1890s), Goldstone's theorem, the Higgs mechanism (Englert and Brout 1964; Higgs 1964; Guralnik, Hagen and Kibble 1964), the equivalence theorem, and the unitarity bound.
- **N.** Her rule that every step up a dimension must be given its leg from outside, read across power, geometry and the Higgs mechanism.

#### 3.7.2 The dimension steps

**From the centre.** For a cube of side s with inscribed radius K = s/2, R₁ = K and Rₖ₊₁ = √(Rₖ² + K²), so Rₖ = K√k (section 3.7). Each step swings round the centre by an angle φ with tan φ = K/Rₖ = 1/√k:

- 1D to 2D: 45°;
- 2D to 3D: 35.26°;
- 3D to 4D: 30°.

The 3D-to-4D step triangle, (K√3, K, 2K), is the 30-60-90 triangle. The four-dimensional corner's radius is 2K = s, equal to the edge, and it runs K(2 − √3) past the three-dimensional corner. Each cubic cell of a tesseract has its centre at distance K from the tesseract's centre, on the inscribed sphere.

**Three phased cubes.** She draws cubes of sides 1, √2 and √3.

- **Her diamond.** Cutting each corner at half the edge (her corner pyramids) leaves a cuboctahedron, her “3D diamond”, whose radius equals its edge, s√2/2:

  | Side | Radius = edge of the diamond | Corner radius of the cube |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 1 | √2/2 = 0.7071 | √3/2 = 0.8660 |
  | √2 | 1: the diamond sits on the unit sphere | √6/2 = 1.2247 |
  | √3 | √6/2 = 1.2247 | 3/2 |
- **The Balance Chart.** The corner radii √3/2, √6/2 and 3/2 are the cube column of her Balance Chart (Table 7).
- **Radius equal to edge.** The shapes whose radius equals their edge are the hexagon in two dimensions, the cuboctahedron in three, and the tesseract and the 24-cell in four. The 24-cell, with vertices (±1, ±1, 0, 0), is the four-dimensional form of her diamond. The tesseract is where the cube family itself reaches radius = edge, since √4/2 = 1.

**From a corner.**

- **Step angles.** The step angle is tan φₙ = 1/√(n − 1): 45°, 35.26°, 30° and 26.57°.
- **Cubes meeting at a corner.** In n dimensions, n cubes of one dimension less meet at a corner. Their far diagonal ends form a regular simplex of edge √2 with centre ((n − 1)/n)(1, …, 1). In four dimensions this takes four cubes, not three, and the centre (¾, ¾, ¾, ¾) is 3/2 from the corner.
- **The step triangles.** Each recurs on her pages:

  | Step | Triangle | Where it recurs |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 1D to 2D | (1, 1, √2) | the Rosetta half-square S |
  | 2D to 3D | (√2, 1, √3) | the converter page |
  | 3D to 4D | (√3, 1, 2) | the Rosetta page's hidden 30-60-90 |
  | 4D to 5D | (2, 1, √5) | the Rosetta triangle M at the key cell |

**Standing.**

- **M.** The radii of n-cubes, the cuboctahedron's radius equal to its edge, the tesseract and the 24-cell (Coxeter 1973).
- **N.** The translation into her K and her phased cubes.

### 3.8 Centroids, the balance chart and the inside-out flip

**The four facts.** “A unit square in the first quadrant (1,1) has a centroid located at ½(√2) from the origin. A square (√2,√2) has a centroid located at 1 from the origin. A cube (1,1,1) has a centroid located at ½(√3) from the origin. A cube (⅔(√3),⅔(√3),⅔(√3)) has a centroid located at 1 from the origin. Geometrically this means if you flip a square or cube inside out, its new dimensions are root families scaled down!” With them: “You can multiply Root Families by grid-spacing to get derived dimensional diagonals (1, 1√2, 1√3, 2).”

*Why the flip gives the grids (added).* Inversion through the unit sphere, r → 1/r, sends a corner of the unit k-cube, at distance √k, to 1/√k. A cube of half-side s has its level-k points at s√k, so s√k = 1/√k gives s = 1/k. The half-sides of the flipped cubes are 1, ½, ⅓ and ¼, the grids of the calculator (section 7.1). Her square of side √2 and her cube of side ⅔√3 are the cases k = 2 and k = 3: the square and the cube whose centres sit at distance 1 from a corner.

**Her Balance Chart.** It lists the centroid distances by root level. Distances are measured from a corner, with the side length across the top and her own forms kept.

**Table 7. Tifinchi's Balance Chart.**

| Column | Shapes | Side 1 | √2 | √3 | 2 | 3 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Centroid distance, a√2/2 | triangle, square and circle | √2/2 | 1 | √3√2/2 | 2√2/2 | 3√2/2 |
| Centroid distance, a√3/2 | cube and sphere | √3/2 | √2√3/2 | 3/2 | 2√3/2 | 3√3/2 |
| ⅓ centroid distance, a√2/(2·3) | triangle and square | √2/(2·3) | ⅓ | √2·√3/(2·3) | √2/3 | √2/2 |
| ⅓ centroid distance, a/3 | triangle and square; circle | ⅓ | √2/3 | √3/3 | ⅔ | 1 |

She tags the columns with index labels (\[6 to 3\], \[6 to 8\], \[6 to 27\], \[6 to 7\], \[23 to 8\], \[17 to 26\]), kept here as written. Three columns, perimeter or circumference, ½ area and ⅓ area, are headed but left blank.

The chart lists the balance points of her square-on-the-long-leg figure (added):

- a√2/2 is the radius of the circle through a square's corners, and a√3/2 the cube's;
- the centroid of the half-square triangle sits a/3 from each leg and a√2/6 from the diagonal.

**Standing.**

- **M.** A triangle's centroid lies a third of the way along each median (Archimedes, *On the Equilibrium of Planes* I). Inversion in a sphere is classical.
- **N.** The chart that ties the root ladder to balance points, and the flip-and-scale reading of the grids, were not found.

2. **The √3 cube's third.** At side √3 the tetrahedron holds √3, the unit cube's body diagonal, and the band holds 1: a third of the √3 square is the unit square.

**Standing.**

- **M.** Each entry is classical.
- **N.** The columns as filled are a reading of her chart, marked as added.

### 3.9 The cube cut into identical pieces

Her page “Pyramid & Pyramid & Pyramid (cube cut into identical pieces)” draws three cuts, each with its net:

- **bi-4**, “bisect 4 corners”, two per cube: the plane through two opposite edges halves the cube into two triangular prisms.
- **tri-3**, “trisect 3 corners”, four per cube: the corner pyramid cut off through the three corners next to one corner, with three right angles at its tip.
- **tri-4**, “trisect 4 corners”, six per cube, “height ½ of cube”: the square pyramid on each face with its apex at the centre.

**The tetrahedron.** With a = 1 and c = √2, the cube minus four corner pyramids leaves the regular tetrahedron of edge c. She draws the four cuts one at a time, with the faces each cut leaves on the net.

*Volumes (added).* Each corner pyramid holds a³/6, so four remove 2a³/3 and leave the tetrahedron with a³/3, exactly a third of the cube; each face pyramid also holds a³/6. The face pyramids and the prisms tile the cube with identical pieces. The corner pyramids come off four at a time and leave the tetrahedron.

**The ladder.** The page ends with √8/2 → √5/2 → √2/2, its arrows marked √3, and with √2 · √2 = √4, √2 + √2 = √8, (√2 + √2)/2 = √8/2 and √4 · √2/2 = √8/2. In half-units the squares 8, 5, 2 are the sum-table cells (2,2), (2,1) and (1,1), and each step removes 3 = 2² − 1², the arrows' √3 (added).

*A correction (added).* The page labels a segment of the tetrahedron √5/2 = 1.118 and builds it from legs 1 and ½. In the tetrahedron of edge √2, opposite edges are 1 apart and half an edge is √2/2, not ½. The segment from a vertex to the midpoint of the opposite edge is therefore √(1 + ½) = √6/2 = 1.225, and the height above a face is 2/√3 = 1.155.

**Standing.**

- **M.** A prism divides into three equal pyramids (Euclid XII.7). The tetrahedron inscribed in a cube, and its third of the volume, are classical (Kepler's *stella octangula*, 1619).
- **N.** The labelling by corners cut and pieces per cube, and the step-by-step net, are hers.
- **D.** The √5/2 label, corrected above.

#### 3.9.1 One object across four pages (added)

The regular tetrahedron {+, ×, ”, .} of the delimiter cube (section 3.12) appears on four of her pages:

- **Root Patterns.** Its “minimum set of three perfect 45 degree triangles” that “loop back if they are 90 degrees out-of-phase” is the loop + → × → ” → +. That loop crosses Top, Right and Front, three mutually perpendicular faces, along three face diagonals of length √2, and closes one face of the tetrahedron.
- **The converter page.** Its m, n and o are three face diagonals √8 on mutually perpendicular faces of the cube of edge 2. They close the same triangle at side 2√2.
- **The pyramid page.** Its tetrahedron is what is left after four corner cuts. The four corners cut away, −, \*, ’ and ,, form the dual tetrahedron.
- **The electrical frame of section 4.2.** It puts charge, current, flux and voltage on the tetrahedron's four corners.

The volumes on the pyramid page fill the Balance Chart's ½ and ⅓ columns in three dimensions (Table 8):

- the bi-4 prism holds ½ of the cube;
- each corner pyramid and each face pyramid holds ⅙;
- the tetrahedron holds ⅓.

### 3.10 The phase circle

On a circle of radius r = 3 with an inscribed square she marks “(⅓) phase = 120°”, “πr distance along curve” and a “wedge” between the arc and the square's side. She then sets three right triangles side by side.

**Table 9. Her three triangles on the radius-3 circle.**

|  | Initial potential (Q1) | Container potential (Q2) | Change |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Angles A, B, C | 60°, 30°, 90° | 45°, 45°, 90° | 45°, 45°, 90° |
| Legs and hypotenuse a, b, c | 3, √3, 2√3 | 3, 3, 3√2 | √3, √3, √6 |
| d, e | √6, √3/2 | 0, 0: “total potential retains container” | 0, 3 |
| Wedge area | left blank | left blank | left blank |

*Checks (added).* d₁² = 9 − 3 = 6, c₁² = 12, c₂ = 3√2 and c₃ = √6 all hold. Two relations tie the triangles together:

- the change triangle's hypotenuse is the initial triangle's virtual leg, c₃ = d₁ = √6;
- its legs are the initial triangle's short leg, √3.

The change is the 45° triangle built on the initial triangle's imbalance, and the container is the 45° triangle on the radius. For the container, the wedge between the arc and one side of the inscribed square has area r²(π − 2)/4 = 2.5686 at r = 3. The e column follows this page's own construction, which differs from the one in section 3.2.

**Standing.**

- **N.** The potential, container and change triangles on one circle are hers.
- **M.** A third of a cycle is 120°, the displacement of three-phase power; the triangles themselves are classical.

### 3.11 The Rosetta fold

**The operation.** Her Rosetta page reads one operation through several mathematical scripts, as the Rosetta Stone carries one text in three:

```latex
R(x,y)=(x+y,\;x-y),\qquad R^{2}=2I
```

R is a fold across the 22.5° line with a √2 stretch. Applied twice it doubles everything, √2 × √2 = 2: her √2 grid.

**Where it acts.**

- **On the legs.** (a, b) → (a + b, a − b), the Manhattan path and the leftover h, and then to (2a, 2b).
- **On the squares.** (a², b²) → (c², d²) → (f², g²) = (2a², 2b²), where g = b√2 is the diagonal of the square on the short leg.
- **Two ladders.** R leaves two ladders of evenly spaced squares: d² → a² → c² in steps of b², and g² → c² → f² in steps of d².

**The seven scripts.**

1. **Area.** Every area is half of one length times another: M = ½ab, N = ½a·h, a sector ½(arc)(r), a circle ½(C)(r) (Archimedes, *Measurement of a Circle*).
2. **Trigonometry.** sin 45° = cos 45° = √2/2 is R's fold, and sin 45° + cos 45° = √2. Also (a + b)² = c² + 4M, h² = c² − 4M and d² = h(a + b).
3. **Complex numbers.** (a + bi)² = d² + 4M·i, with size c²; the example (4, 2) squares to (12, 16, 20) = 4 × (3, 4, 5).
4. **Circle and balance.** K = a/2 is the inscribed radius and f/2 = K√2 the radius through the corners, the Balance Chart's first column. The centroid of the half-square sits a/3 from each leg and a√2/6 from the diagonal, its second column.
5. **Dimension.** The converter adds one squared term per axis, 1 → √2 → √3. The unit diagonal (√3/3)(1, 1, 1) makes 54.74° with each axis.
6. **Tier.** c² is the sum tier, d² the difference tier and P₂ the three-square tier, all on one page; the example (4, 2) is the (2,1) cell doubled.
7. **Origami.** d is the swing fold: pin a corner and swing the point at distance a onto the line b away, and it lands d across.

**Six alignments at a = 2b.** Six things line up only when the long leg is twice the short one:

1. M = N.
2. The point where b meets h lands on the inscribed circle.
3. The virtual triangle (b, d, a) is 1 : √3 : 2, the 30-60-90.
4. b, g, d = b(1, √2, √3): the edge, face diagonal and body diagonal of the cube on the short leg.
5. The squared triangle is 3-4-5.
6. It is the (2,1) cell of both tables, √5 and √3.

**Standing.**

- **M.** R is the unnormalised Hadamard matrix (Sylvester 1867), and u = ct + x, v = ct − x are the light-cone coordinates of relativity (Dirac 1949). Each script is classical, as cited.
- **N.** The translation key, one figure read in seven scripts, and the six alignments at a = 2b were not found.

The last column says how her use differs from the source's, where it does. Lines marked def are definitions. Throughout, a is the long leg and b the short one; if b > a the two are swapped.
