**What it adds.** No number: every row is general relativity or quantum mechanics. Two things change. The quarter turn is a time derivative for every motion, not only at one frequency, which removes a limit the calculator carried until now (section 20). And the author's two legs are the metric's two factors: the time leg alone gives a third of Mercury's advance and half of light's bending, and both legs give all of each.

**Standing.**

- **M.** Hamilton's equations (Hamilton 1834), the mass shell in a static metric (Einstein 1916), Schrödinger's equation (Schrödinger 1926) and Dirac's (Dirac 1928). The measurements are Park et al. (2017), Shapiro et al. (2004), Ashby (2003), Maisenbacher et al. (2026) and the NIST Atomic Spectra Database (Kramida and Ralchenko).
- **N.** Reading the metric's two factors as the author's two legs, and Hamilton's symplectic turn as her quarter turn. It changes no number.

## 5. The board: what space is made of

Computed on the calculator's ring, the vacuum is a transmission line. Its capacitance ε₀ and inductance μ₀ per length give the speed c, its impedance is Z₀ = 2α·h/e² = 376.730 Ω, and it holds a gap of 2mₑc² = 1.022 MeV before anything acts. A reaction of the board carries four corners, charge, current, flux and voltage, joined by identities: for the electron q = e, φ = h/e, i = ωe and v = mₑc²/e, so qφ = h and qv = φi = mₑc². Photons, electrons and atoms are the board's reactions, and what quantum electrodynamics calls a virtual particle is its reactive response. Every number in this section is the standard one; what the calculator adds is one circuit in which they are a single device, and a bookkeeping of the electron's spin by its two stores.

### 5.1 The board

**Standard.** The vacuum's constants make a transmission line (Table 24).

**Table 24. The board's numbers.**

| Board quantity | Value | Standard name |
| --- | --- | --- |
| capacitance per length | ε₀ = 8.854 × 10⁻¹² F/m | vacuum permittivity |
| inductance per length | μ₀ = 1.2566 × 10⁻⁶ H/m | vacuum permeability |
| signal speed 1/√(L′C′) | c = 299,792,458 m/s | the speed of light |
| base ohms √(μ₀/ε₀) | Z₀ = 376.730 Ω = 2α · h/e² | the impedance of free space; the physics in it is α, and the ohms are an SI convention |
| gap before action | 2mₑc² = 1.022 MeV | the pair-creation threshold (Dirac 1930) |
| exciton | positronium: 6.80 eV binding, a 511 keV glow on recombination | positronium; the annihilation line |
| breakdown field | 1.32 × 10¹⁸ V/m | the Schwinger limit |
| kinetic inductance of the weak current | screening depth ħc/m\_W = 2.46 × 10⁻¹⁸ m | the W mass; the London depth of a superconductor is the same mechanism |

**How it works.**

- **Electric and magnetic are a quarter turn apart.** E ↔ H is the quarter turn (the duality J² = −1). In the near field Z\_E Z\_H = Z₀², with Z₀/√2 at −45° and √2·Z₀ at +45° where kr = 1.
- **Light is the board ringing on its 45° line.** For a plane wave Faraday's law gives E = cB, so u\_E = ½ε₀E² equals u\_B = B²/2μ₀ = E²/(2μ₀c²) = ½ε₀E², using c² = 1/(μ₀ε₀). On a transmission line the same statement is V/I = Z₀ everywhere: the electric store ½C′V² equals the magnetic store ½L′I² exactly when Z₀² = L′/C′, which defines the line's impedance.
- **The number in the ohms is α.** Z₀ = 2α · h/e² = 2 × 0.0072974 × 25,812.8 Ω: the vacuum's impedance is two fine-structure constants' worth of the quantum of resistance.

**The reading.** Space is a board with a capacitor and an inductor at every point, and everything else is what the board does when driven.

- **The sea.** Before anything acts, the board is a semiconductor: a filled sea 1.022 MeV below the conduction states. A photon above the gap lifts an electron out and leaves a hole, which is the positron.
- **Inductance.** Inductance is the store in the perpendicular dimension, the √2 → √4 swing of section 4.6.

**Virtual particles as the board's reactive response.** Around any charge the field has three parts:

- terms in 1/(kr)³ and 1/(kr)² whose energy flow is reactive: stored in one quarter cycle, returned in the next;
- a term in 1/(kr) whose flow is real and leaves;
- the boundary between them at kr = 1.

What quantum electrodynamics calls virtual-photon exchange is, in the circuit, capacitive and inductive storage; what it calls a real photon is the mode that carries power away. Three sources agree with this reading:

- W. E. Lamb's “Anti-photon” (Lamb 1995);
- Hobson's “there are no particles, there are only fields” (Hobson 2013);
- Jaffe (2005), who shows the Casimir force can be computed without reference to zero-point energy, as Bass and Krzysiak (2020) also note.

**Whose board.** The circuit is the author's. The vacuum it is drawn on is Steven D. Bass's emergent vacuum (Bass 2015, 2020, 2024):

- the Standard Model as the long-range tail of a statistical system near a critical point;
- gauge symmetries that emerge, and particles as stable collective excitations of unknown degrees of freedom;
- a cosmological constant that is the small residual left after condensate and zero-point energies cancel.

Bass does not write the vacuum as a circuit.

**The electron's three lengths are a flip.** The classical radius rₑ = αƛₑ = 2.8179 fm, the reduced Compton wavelength ƛₑ = 386.16 fm and the Bohr radius a₀ = ƛₑ/α = 52.918 pm stand in the ratio α : 1 : 1/α. So rₑa₀ = ƛₑ² = 1.49119 × 10⁻²⁵ m² exactly: the atom's radius and the charge's radius are each other's inversion through the Compton sphere.

**Standing.**

- **M.** Every number is standard. The reactive reading of virtual particles agrees with Lamb, Hobson and Jaffe, and the residual is Bass's.
- **N.** Drawing Bass's vacuum as a capacitive–inductive circuit, term by term, and reading rₑa₀ = ƛ² as an inversion were not found.

### 5.2 Worked example: the electron on the ring

1. **Frame.** The electrical frame puts q on ”, i on ., φ on + and v on ×.
2. **Anchor.**
   - The Right face (q, v) holds the electron's capacitance, Cₑ = e²/(mₑc²) = 4πε₀rₑ = 3.1354 × 10⁻²⁵ F.
   - The Left face (φ, i) holds its inductance, Lₑ = h²/(e²mₑc²) = πμ₀a₀ = 2.0891 × 10⁻¹⁶ H.
3. **The four corners.** With q = e, the turn gives:
   - i = ωe = 19.796 A, with ω = mₑc²/h = 1.2356 × 10²⁰ circuits a second;
   - φ = Lₑi = h/e = 4.1357 × 10⁻¹⁵ Wb;
   - v = q/Cₑ = mₑc²/e = 510,998.95 V.

   All six faces then read off. Resistance v/i and memristance φ/q are both h/e² = 25,812.807 Ω, the von Klitzing constant. The products are qv = φi = mₑc² and qφ = h.
4. **One quarter turn.** The charge goes to the current corner: one elementary charge per Compton period h/(mₑc²), a charge moving at c round the Compton circle of radius ƛ = 386.16 fm.
   - Its moment is i·πƛ² = eħ/2mₑ = μ\_B exactly, checked to 30 digits.
   - The measured moment is 1.00115965218059(13) μ\_B (Fan et al. 2023). The 0.116% excess is α/2π at first order, which the calculator files on the Left face as the load.
5. **The radii stay put.** At the same quarter turn the energy passes from the capacitor, at rₑ = 2.818 fm, to the inductor, at a₀ = 52.92 pm. The two radii multiply to ƛ² = 1.4912 × 10⁻²⁵ m² exactly, so nothing is resized.
6. **Two quarter turns.** Every sign turns: −q, −i, −φ, −v. For the electron this is the positron.
7. **Observed at every position.**
   - The positron's charge is equal and opposite to the electron's within 4 × 10⁻⁸, and its mass equal within 8 × 10⁻⁹.
   - Its g-factor equals the electron's within (−0.5 ± 2.1) × 10⁻¹² (Van Dyck, Schwinberg and Dehmelt 1987, via PDG).
   - On the flux circle, h/e is the Aharonov–Bohm period of gold rings (Webb et al. 1985), and mₑc²/e is the rest energy, 0.51099895069(16) MeV (Mohr et al. 2024).
8. **Provenance.** The Bohr magneton came from the charge corner by one quarter turn, and the positron by two.

**A tank caveat.** √(LₑCₑ) = h/mₑc² is one circuit's time, since the corners carry one charge and one flux quantum per circuit. A standard LC tank built from Lₑ and Cₑ, whose 1/√(LC) counts radians, rings at 1.9665 × 10¹⁹ Hz, 2π below the ring's 1.23559 × 10²⁰ circuits a second.

**The spin's half: the electron as a reaction.** The ring above carries angular momentum ħ, twice the electron's spin, so as it stands it reproduces the moment but not the spin. The author's rule supplies the half: the electron is a reaction of the board, not a mass on a ring.

- **Two stores.** Its rest energy sits in two stores. Half, ½qv = ½mₑc² = 255.499475 keV, is held on the capacitor; this equals e²/8πε₀rₑ, the energy of a charge on a sphere of radius rₑ. The other half, ½φi = ½mₑc², moves in the inductor: her share rule of action on the curve and none inside.
- **The half goes round.** Only the moving half goes round the Compton circle, so the angular momentum is (½mₑc²/c)ƛ = ħ/2.
- **The whole charge goes round.** The whole charge makes every circuit, so the moment stays μ\_B, and g = 2.
- **The 4π rule.** A state turned through 2π lands in the inverse cell: e^(−i2πJ/ħ) = −1 only for J = ħ/2. This is the 4π periodicity seen in neutron interferometers (Rauch et al. and Werner et al., 1975, as analysed by Sassoli de Bianchi 2017).

The Dirac field reaches the same factor by another split: its charge circulates twice as fast as its mass (Sebens 2019). Ohanian (1986, as described by Sebens) treats spin and moment as flows of energy and charge in the electron's wave field. The measured g cannot tell the two splits apart.

Three limits (added):

1. **Classical fields give ħ/3.** Classical field theory puts angular momentum in the field, ε₀E × B. A charge e on a sphere of radius rₑ with moment μ\_B at its centre holds μ₀eμ\_B/(6πrₑ) = ħ/3 in its field, and ħ/2 only at 2rₑ/3, the radius Blinder (2002) fits.
2. **The rate matters.** The half uses the ring's rate. At the tank's rate the moving half would carry πħ.
3. **g = 2 exactly.** The excess, aₑ = 0.00115965218059, is the next layer: QED's series, α/2π = 0.0011614097 at one loop, 0.0011596374 with two loops and 0.0011596522 with three.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The factor of two is known.
- **N.** The split by stores was not found in a short search. It is bookkeeping, not a field calculation.

### 5.3 Oscillation and holding: photons, electrons and atoms as reactions of the board

**Agreeing with Bass, in circuit terms.** Bass's vacuum (section 5.1) says what the particles are:

- “the quarks and leptons as well as the gauge bosons and Higgs boson would be the stable collective long-range excitations of some more primordial degrees of freedom that exist above the scale of emergence”;
- the electron quasiparticle “develops a mass gap from interaction with the condensate”, as in a superconductor;
- a photon in a superconductor becomes a massive plasmon, “a non-relativistic precursor” of the Higgs mechanism;
- the zero-point energy of the massless photon is zero, and the net zero-point energy of the gapped excitations is cancelled against the condensate's;
- the Casimir force, “sometimes claimed as experimental evidence for zero-point energies, can also be calculated without reference to” them (Bass and Krzysiak 2020, after Jaffe 2005).

The calculator agrees with each of these sentences and reads each as a circuit element: the board is ε₀ and μ₀, its gap is 2mₑc², its stable excitations are its resonant reactions, and its condensate is the held store. The one change is the name of what QED calls a virtual particle (section 5.1). The energy-time relation ΔE·Δt ≲ ħ is then the statement that a reactance returns what it stores within a cycle.

**The photon is the board ringing.**

- **One quantum.** A photon is one quantum of the board's ringing on its 45° line, E = hf, so the action quantum per cycle (section 7.2) is the energy per cycle of the mode.
- **Two legs.** The photon carries the two transverse legs, its two polarisations, and no third, which is why it is massless.
- **Near and far.** Around any charge the field's 1/(kr)³ and 1/(kr)² terms carry reactive power in and out, and the 1/(kr) term carries real power away. The boundary is kr = 1. The Coulomb force is the near field at zero frequency: an exchange that never leaves, the board's capacitance between two charges. At the electron's Compton frequency the boundary sits at ƛₑ = 386 fm, the electron's reactive zone.

**Table 25. The photon's observations.**

| Observation | Number | Source | The circuit reading |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| the photon has no third leg | m\_γ < 10⁻¹⁸ eV; the 1/r² law holds to 10⁻¹⁶ | PDG; Williams, Faller and Hill 1971 | two legs on the 45° line, the held leg empty |
| light scatters off light | σ(γγ → γγ) = 78 ± 13 ± 7 nb at 8.2σ; 107 ± 24 ± 13 nb, agreeing with the next-to-leading-order prediction | ATLAS Collaboration 2019; CMS Collaboration 2025 | the board's reactance is non-linear: the Euler–Heisenberg term is the vacuum's Kerr effect, a reaction with no particle emitted |
| two photons make a pair | σ(γγ → e⁺e⁻) = 263.5 ± 1.8 ± 17.8 μb, leading-order QED; the polarisation-dependent angular modulation | CMS Collaboration 2025; STAR Collaboration 2021 | breakdown of the gap: two photons above 2mₑc² lift an electron out of the sea and leave the hole |
| a magnetar's field makes the vacuum birefringent | X-ray polarisation 46 ± 4% at 2–8 keV and 59 ± 5% at 2–3 keV from 1E 1547.0−5408, inferred through atmosphere and geometry models | Stewart et al. 2026; earlier 4U 0142+61, Taverna et al. 2022 | at 10¹⁰ T the board is a crystal: the same cubic reactance, read as a refractive index |
| the breakdown field is not yet reached | E\_S = mₑ²c³/eħ = 1.32 × 10¹⁸ V/m; critical field 4.41 × 10⁹ T | Schwinger 1951 | the board's dielectric strength |

**The electron is the board held above its gap.**

- **The pair.** A photon or a collision above the gap lifts one excitation out and leaves one hole: the electron and the positron, each carrying half the gap, 511 keV, as its held leg.
- **The charge.** It is the U(1) phase coupling that the Hopf projection hides (section 14.2), and e² = 2αh/Z₀ = 2.5670 × 10⁻³⁸ C², checked.
- **The spin.** Spin ½ is the half-turn: one full turn of the electron's phase is two turns of the field's, the double cover whose parity is the start rule (section 7.3).
- **The moment and α.** The measured g/2 = 1.00115965218059(13), to 0.13 parts per trillion, is the most precisely known property of any particle (Fan et al. 2023). With QED's series it reads α⁻¹ = 137.035999166(15). That sits 2.2σ from rubidium's 137.035999206(11) (Morel et al. 2020) and 3.9σ from caesium's 137.035999046(27) (Parker et al. 2018), and the two atomic values disagree with each other at 5.5σ: the board's one number read three ways, with the electron's in the middle.
- **No size.** The electron has no measured size. Its structure is absent down to 10⁻¹⁸ m, and its electric dipole moment is below 4.1 × 10⁻³⁰ e·cm (Roussy et al. 2023), round to the limit of the measurement.
- **Three lengths, one flip.** It has three lengths, and they are one flip (section 5.1):
  - the classical radius, where the board's electric store around the charge equals the rest energy;
  - the reduced Compton wavelength, below which the board answers a probe with pairs;
  - the Bohr radius, where the atom's orbit sits.

**The muon.** It is the same reaction 206.768 times heavier, and its load shows the board reacting with its hadrons: about 60 parts per million of its anomaly is the board's hadronic polarisation (58.7 ppm with the 2020 data-driven value, 60.4 ppm with the 2025 lattice value).

- **The measurement.** The final Fermilab value is a\_μ = 116 592 070.5 ± 14.8 × 10⁻¹¹ (127 ppb; Muon g−2 Collaboration 2025).
- **The predictions.** It agrees with the 2025 lattice-based prediction, 116 592 033 ± 62 × 10⁻¹¹, at 0.6σ, while the 2020 prediction built on e⁺e⁻ data sits 5.7σ below it (Muon g−2 Theory Initiative 2025).

The open discrepancy is between two readings of the board's hadronic reaction, not between the muon and QED.

**Table 26. The virtual particle, row by row.** Nothing here changes a number of QED's. What the calculator owns is the vocabulary, and the claim that the reactance which stores the Coulomb field is the inductance that loads the mass tables (sections 7.2 and 9.1).

| QED's name | What is measured | The circuit reading |

| zero-point energy | its naive sum is 10¹²⁰ times the dark energy; the measured residual is 2.24 meV | the stored energy of the gapped reactions, zero for the massless photon, cancelled against the condensate's; the residual, measured at 2.24 meV, is read as v²/M₂₃ = 2.02 ± 0.20 meV (section 13) |
| Hawking radiation | unobserved; its analogue at a sonic horizon in a condensate seen, thermal at 0.35 nK (Steinhauer 2016; Muñoz de Nova et al. 2019; section 4.7.3) | the board's reaction at a horizon |

**The atom is the board's resonance with a nucleus.** Every length and energy in hydrogen is one rung of the ladder of α:

- **Lengths.** rₑ, ƛₑ and a₀.
- **Energies:**
  - mₑc² = 511.0 keV;
  - α·mₑc² = 3.729 keV, the Bohr orbit's momentum times c;
  - α²·mₑc² = 27.211 eV, the Hartree, of which the binding is half, 13.606 eV, by the virial theorem;
  - the fine structure, α⁴·mₑc²/32 = 10.95 GHz, against the measured 10.97 GHz for the n = 2 doublet;
  - the hyperfine line, α⁴·mₑc²·(mₑ/mₚ)·(4/3)gₚ = 1421.2 MHz at leading order and 1418.8 MHz with the reduced-mass factor, against the measured 1420.405751768 MHz (Hellwig et al. 1970; Essen et al. 1971);
  - the Lamb shift, at α⁵·mₑc² times a logarithm.

Each power of α is one more turn of the per-cycle coupling. The whole spectrum is measured to parts in 10¹⁵: the 1S–2S interval is 2,466,061,413,187,035 ± 10 Hz (Parthey et al. 2011). The hidden symmetry that makes the shells squares is Fock's four-dimensional sphere, in Baez's sharper form of 2025 (section 14.5): the atom is a field on the 3-sphere, the held torus and the Hopf map in one more form, and the periodic table is its spectrum.

**Positronium, the board's own exciton.** It is the same spectrum at half the Rydberg, because its two halves weigh the same: binding 6.803 eV, and its 1S–2P line at 243.0 nm, twice Lyman-α.

- **Cooling.** It was laser-cooled from 610 K to 1 K along the beam in 2024 with a chirped pulse train on that line (Shu et al. 2024), after AEgIS reached 170 K (Glöggler et al. 2024).
- **CPT.** Its decays test CPT, with no violation found at a statistical precision of 10⁻⁴ (Moskal et al. 2021).
- **The glow.** It ends as two 511 keV photons, the board's glow.

**The Lamb shift, worked.**

- **Welton's picture.** It is the circuit's: the board's zero-point ringing shakes the electron, with mean-square displacement ⟨(δr)²⟩ = (2α/π)ƛ² ln(1/α). A shaken electron feels the Coulomb cusp smoothed by (1/6)⟨(δr)²⟩∇²V, which for the 2S state gives ΔE = α⁵mₑc² ln(1/α)/(6π) = 667 MHz.
- **The full value.** Bethe's calculation with the proper logarithm gives 1,040 MHz, and the measured n = 2 interval is 1,057.8 MHz, to 3 kHz. The same measurement gives rₚ = 0.833 ± 0.010 fm (Bezginov et al. 2019).
- **The polarisation term.** About −27 MHz of the measured shift is the board's polarisation, the Uehling term.
- **Muonic hydrogen.** There the orbit sits 207 times closer, and the polarisation is the main term: the measured shift is 202.37 meV, the one-loop vacuum polarisation alone is 205.03 meV, and the proton's size subtracts 3.7 meV (Antognini et al. 2013).
- **The running of α.** From 1/137.036 at rest to about 1/129 at the Z mass, it is the same polarisation read as dispersion.

**The turn and the hold.** Everything in the calculator that moves does so in one of two ways, the odd and even loops of section 7.3:

- **The odd ring.** An odd ring of inversions cycles: the clock, the start, the turn, the π side of the pair.
- **The even ring.** An even ring holds: the latch, the √4 level, the store. When the store is space itself, its expansion is exponential, the e side.

The two are joined by the quarter turn, V = L dI/dt: a turning phase feeds a store and a store feeds a turn, and the √2 → √4 swing is the one cycle. A held state driven by a turning one answers at twice the frequency, because it couples to the square, cos²ωt = (1 + cos 2ωt)/2. That covers single-phase power, ball lightning's 99.4 Hz, the inflaton-driven curvature R ∝ ½ + (3/2) cos 2mt (section 11.3), and the pulse of any store that is squeezed and released.

The expansion itself does not oscillate (section 13): the store holds and the phases turn. A biological pulse, a pacemaker cell firing when its stored charge crosses a threshold (van der Pol and van der Mark 1928), is the same logic in a membrane. The calculator gives it the mechanism, not a number.

The reactive-board circuit is the author's own electrical reading, inductance included. It is built on Steven D. Bass's emergent-vacuum work, which supplies the subcomponent dynamics and not the circuit. Section 5.3 quotes his statements; Table 27 sets each piece of the circuit beside its counterpart.

In circuit terms the W mass is the vacuum's kinetic inductance: screening depth ħc/m\_W = 2.46 × 10⁻¹⁸ m, cutoff m\_Wc²/h = 1.94 × 10²⁵ Hz | Bass 2026; the circuit numbers are the author's |

**What the circuit adds.** It adds explicit electrical numbers that Bass's papers do not state:

- the base ohms, Z₀ = 376.73 Ω;
- the reactance split;
- the three-phase balance;
- the cube geometry of the √2 → √4 torus swing.

**The emergence scale, with the factor in front taken as 1.**

- **The scale the hum needs.** v²/μ\_vac = 2.7 × 10¹⁶ GeV.
- **The crossings.** The forces cross only in pairs, in the two-loop running with PDG 2026 inputs:
  - α₁ = α₂ at 1.1 × 10¹³ GeV;
  - α₁ = α₃ at 1.7 × 10¹⁴ GeV;
  - α₂ = α₃ at 3.0 × 10¹⁶ GeV.
- **Which crossing.** The author's rule says action starts where phases overlap, and only two can overlap at once. Coming down from where nothing has shifted, the first overlap is weak = strong. Taking that crossing as Bass's M gives the hum within 1.1σ (section 13).
- **What makes it exact.** α\_s = 0.1171, −1.0σ from PDG.
- **The neutrinos.** If the lightest neutrino is also v²/M, it is 2.0 meV and the sum is 61 meV, under the cap of about 64 meV from DESI's second release with the microwave background. This is untested. The measured splittings need factors of 4 to 25 in front, so only the lightest neutrino could sit at v²/M.
- **The same lever as the balance test.** At α\_s = 0.1171 the three-phase rule still holds:
  - a top of 172.21 ± 0.38, −0.15σ against the pole mass;
  - a Higgs of 125.91 ± 0.93, −0.8σ.

  Both leads hinge on α\_s and the top mass, so α\_s to ±0.0002 and a top threshold scan test both.
- **Near the edge.** Bass puts the vacuum “within about 1.3σ of being stable”. With PDG 2026 inputs the calculator gets 1.6σ with the pole mass and 3.6σ with the direct mass (section 9.6). Bass explains why the Higgs sits near the edge, a critical point where the symmetries emerge. The author's rule says how far past it: the 0.955% three-phase offset.

## 6. Spacetime: the two tables, light and the views

On the calculator, spacetime is the pair of tables. The sum table is energy and momentum, E² = (pc)² + (mc²)²; the difference table is the interval, s² = (ct)² − x²; and zero difference, the 45° line, is light. Relativity's three curves, the circle, the hyperbola and the line where they touch, are the three behaviours of one complex square (section 3.4.2). Every view of the cube loses one direction, and a straight step at an angle costs a staircase of legs. Every number in this section is the standard one. What the calculator adds is the single figure that holds them.

### 6.1 Energy and momentum: the sum table

Write the energy as the hypotenuse of a right triangle whose legs are pc and mc². At a fixed energy the states lie on a circle in the (pc, mc²) plane; a particle at rest sits on the mass axis, and light sits on the momentum axis. The rest mass comes back from the difference table: (mc²)² = E² − (pc)², so every rest mass in the particle tables is a cell of the difference table.

Table 29 sets the three behaviours of one square, the circle, the hyperbola and the 45° line, beside the physics each carries and the measurement that checks it.

**Table 29. The three behaviours of one square, in physics.**

| Table | Curve | In physics | Check |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| sum, a² + b² = c² | a circle at fixed c | E² = (pc)² + (mc²)² at fixed energy; bound orbits | the kinematics of every collision |
| difference, a² − b² = d² | a hyperbola at fixed d | the interval s² = (ct)² − x² under boosts; the rest mass from E and p | time dilation at γ = 29.33, to 0.2% (Bailey et al. 1977) |
| zero difference, a = b | the 45° line | light: s = 0 and m = 0 | the photon's mass below 10⁻¹⁸ eV (PDG 2026) |

**Worked: a muon in a storage ring.** At γ = 29.33 a muon of 105.658 MeV has E = γmc² = 3,098.96 MeV and pc = √(E² − (mc²)²) = 3,097.16 MeV. Its clock runs on the difference: proper time is dτ = dt√(1 − β²) = dt/γ, so the laboratory lifetime is γτ₀ = 29.33 × 2.19698 μs = 64.44 μs. The CERN storage ring measured that dilation to 0.2% (Bailey et al. 1977).

**Standing.** **M**: special relativity (Einstein 1905; Minkowski 1908). **N**: reading E, pc and mc² as the cells of the author's two tables (section 3.4).

### 6.2 Light: the 45° line

Zero difference is where the circle and the hyperbola touch: equal legs, s = 0, no mass. It is also the line of equal shares (section 7.2): a light wave carries equal electric and magnetic energy at every instant (section 5.1).

- **No third leg.** The photon's mass is below 10⁻¹⁸ eV (PDG 2026).
- **The same line for gravity.** Gravitational waves and light from GW170817 arrived together, so their speeds agree to about 3 parts in 10¹⁵ (LIGO Scientific and Virgo Collaborations 2017).
- **No preferred frame.** The line has no ether wind (Michelson and Morley 1887). A board with physical properties but no state of motion is Einstein's own description of space after relativity (Einstein 1920), and it is the board of section 5.

**Standing.** **M**: every statement. **N**: light as the zero-difference line of the author's tables, shared with the 45° balance of section 7.2.

### 6.3 Time as the held axis

The calculator holds time as the fourth axis, and a rate is a quarter turn into it (section 4.4). The interval factors, (ct)² − x² = (ct + x)(ct − x): a boost stretches one factor and shrinks the other by the same amount, so the product holds (at ct = 5, x = 3 and β = 0.6, s² = 16 before and after; section 4.4.1). Proper time is the root of the difference. Four dimensions is also where the unit diagonal reaches exactly 2, the held level √4 (section 3.7.2), and gravitational waves are damped as they would be in four dimensions (section 4.7.2).

**Standing.** **M**: the algebra and the measurements. **N**: time as the held axis of the author's fixed cube, reached by a quarter turn.

### 6.4 The view down a diagonal, and the staircase

**A view loses one direction.** A projection from three dimensions to two has a one-dimensional kernel, its line of sight; for the Clarke matrix, K·(1, 1, 1) = 0.

- **Thirteen lines of sight.** The cube's 26 non-centre points lie on 13 lines through the centre: 3 face axes, 6 edge axes and 4 corner axes.
- **Shadows.** Down a unit direction n the unit cube casts a shadow of area |n\_x| + |n\_y| + |n\_z|, which is 1, √2 and √3 on the three kinds of axis: the root ladder as shadow areas. The mean over all directions is 3/2 (Cauchy 1841; Monte Carlo 1.49993).
- **Corner-on.** The view down a corner axis is the Clarke frame and the tribimaximal matrix (section 10.1).

**The sequence tester reads the tilt in volts.** Two equal loads and a capacitor with X\_C = R, in star with the star point floating, put (3 ± √3)/√10 of the phase voltage across the two loads. Their ratio is 2 + √3 = 3.732 = cot 15°, and which load is bright tells ABC from ACB, the order no single phase carries.

**The staircase.** A straight unit step at angle θ costs |cos θ| + |sin θ| in legs, on average 4/π in two dimensions and 3/2 in three. A staircase of s legs on a 45° path changes direction between segments by (2/√π)/√s radians on average, 64.65°/√s. Hill's 17.3° for lightning (Hill 1968) corresponds to s ≈ 14 legs per segment; s is fitted, not derived.

**Standing.**

- **M.** Cauchy's mean-projection theorem, Fortescue's sequences and the lamp-and-capacitor tester are standard.
- **N.** The 13 lines of sight with shadows 1, √2 and √3, and the tester's ratio as cot 15°, were not found.

### 6.5 Beside the standard theories

On this layer too the standard theories give every number, and the calculator gives the same ones. Its additions are the single figure and one identification: the tester's ratio as the cotangent of the mixing tilt.

**Table 30. Spacetime on one yardstick.**

**Origination.** **M** for every number. **N** for the thirteen lines of sight with shadows 1, √2 and √3, the tester's ratio as cot 15°, and the segment law in closed form.

## 7. Balance and three phases

Two rules run the calculator's physics. **Balance:** a state is balanced when its two shares are equal, the 45° line, and a state in motion sits slightly past the line, held there by a load of one coupling squared over 8π² per loading phase, per action cycle. **Three phases:** three equal phases 120° apart sum to zero, and a start cannot use all three; the third rides the first two. Computed, the first rule gives the escape speed as √2 times the orbital speed, flat three-phase power and, at its first order, the electron's anomalous moment α/2π. The second gives the forces crossing in pairs, at 1.1 × 10¹³, 1.7 × 10¹⁴ and 3.0 × 10¹⁶ GeV, never at one point. This section states the cube's levels, moves and grids that the rules act on, then the two rules.

### 7.1 Levels, moves and grids

**The count.** A point with k non-zero coordinates chooses which k of the three axes are active, in C(3, k) ways, and a sign for each, in 2ᵏ ways: C(3, k)·2ᵏ = 1, 6, 12 and 8, summing to 27 = 3³. Distance squared is the number of unit coordinates, so the level counts the active axes, which is all that “levels count phases” says. The tesseract's corner, at 2, is the held fourth level.

**The moves are the roots of 1** (checked by enumeration):

- inside-out, p → −p, is −1;
- a third of a turn about a body diagonal, (x, y, z) → (z, x, y), is ω;
- a quarter turn about a face axis is i.

Together they generate the cube's 48 symmetries, every permutation of the axes times every sign pattern, which crystallographers call m3m.

**The moves are bit operations.**

- Inside-out, p → −p, is NOT on all three bits.
- The 120° turn about a body diagonal is a cyclic shift of the bits.
- The quarter turn about a face axis is a swap with one NOT.

The count of 48 is forced. A symmetry of the cube sends face centres to face centres, so it sends each axis to plus or minus some axis: a permutation of the axes (6) times a sign on each (8). Every signed permutation is a symmetry, so there are no others, and enumeration confirms that the three moves generate all 48. Each move is the smallest operation of its order that the cube allows: (−1)² = ω³ = i⁴ = 1.

**Levels and chords.** The levels are the squared chords of the moves: |1 − e^(iθ)|² = 2(1 − cos θ) = 1, 2, 3, 4 at θ = 60°, 90°, 120°, 180°.

**Grids.** The flip r → 1/r makes the grids 1, ½, ⅓ and ¼ (section 3.8). The share one leg carries of the k-cube's diagonal is cos²θ = 1/k, and the corners project onto the diagonal at the fractions j/k with binomial counts: (1, 2, 1), (1, 3, 3, 1) and (1, 4, 6, 4, 1).

**The unit cube inside the side-2 cube.** Put the side-2 cube on \[0, 2\]³, with its centre at (1, 1, 1). The unit cube's corners then sit as follows:

- the corner with no coordinate equal to 1 is a corner of the big cube;
- the three with one 1 are its edge midpoints (level √2);
- the three with two 1s are its face centres (level 1, the four-fold symmetry points);
- (1, 1, 1) is its centre.

So k ones sit at level √(3 − k): the grading (1, 3, 3, 1) read backwards, the same complement k → 3 − k as the inside-out of section 8.1.

**The tetrahedron as a third.** Cutting a corner of the side-2 cube along the three face diagonals that meet at the neighbouring corners removes a tetrahedron with three perpendicular edges of length 2, of volume (1/6) · 2 · 2 · 2 = 4/3. Four alternate corners remove 16/3 of the cube's 8 and leave 8/3, exactly ⅓ of the cube: a regular tetrahedron of edge 2√2.

**Three curves of physics.** The sum, the difference and the 45° line are E² = (pc)² + (mc²)² (a circle in (pc, mc²) at fixed E), the interval s² = (ct)² − x² (a hyperbola under boosts), and the light cone s = 0.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The symmetry group is textbook crystallography, and the gradings (1, 3, 3, 1) and (1, 4, 6, 4, 1) are those of the Clifford algebras of three- and four-dimensional space. Gauss (1831) called +1, −1 and √−1 the direct, inverse and lateral units.
- **N.** Reading levels as active phases, and the moves as roots of 1 on the cube's own axes, were not found.

### 7.2 Balance and the load

**Equal shares, with their proofs.** A light wave carries u\_E = u\_B at every instant (section 5.1), and three more cases carry equal shares:

- **Escape is √2 × orbit.** On a circular orbit GMm/r² = mv\_c²/r, so v\_c² = GM/r. Escape needs kinetic energy equal to the well's depth, ½mv\_e² = GMm/r, so v\_e² = 2GM/r. On the circle the kinetic energy is half the potential's size and at escape they are equal; the speed ratio is √2 because energy goes as the square of speed. This is the virial theorem (Clausius 1870).
- **Three-phase power is flat.** With voltages cos(θ − 2πn/3) and currents cos(θ − 2πn/3 − φ), each phase's power is ½\[cos φ + cos(2θ − 4πn/3 − φ)\]. Summed over n = 0, 1, 2 the three 2θ terms are 120° apart and cancel, 1 + ω + ω² = 0, leaving (3/2) cos φ. With one phase nothing cancels the cos 2θ term, and cos²θ = (1 + cos 2θ)/2 peaks at twice its average: single-phase power pulses at 2f.
- **The Koide identity.** Put √mₖ = μ\[1 + a cos(δ + 2πk/3)\]. Over the three phases Σ cos(δ + 2πk/3) = 0 and Σ cos²(δ + 2πk/3) = 3/2 for every δ, so Σ√m = 3μ, Σm = μ²(3 + 3a²/2) and Q = (1 + a²/2)/3.
  - The waveform's DC power is 1 and its AC power a²/2, so a = √2 is DC = AC.
  - The angle between (√m₀, √m₁, √m₂) and (1, 1, 1) has cos θ = 1/√(3Q), so Q = ⅔ is θ = 45°.

  One ratio is read three ways: as a power split, as a quadratic form and as an angle.

**Slightly past, and held.** Nature sits just past the line and is held there:

- static friction exceeds sliding friction (Amontons 1699; Coulomb 1785);
- a balanced rock outlasts the earthquake (Housner 1963; Brune 1996);
- stick-slip governs faults (Brace and Byerlee 1966);
- the Higgs vacuum's quartic coupling dips just below zero, with a lifetime of order 10¹³⁹ years (Andreassen, Frost and Schwartz 2018).

**The load.** The size of “slightly” is the load per loading phase,

```latex
\delta=\frac{g_i^{2}}{4\pi\cdot 2\pi}=\frac{\alpha_i}{2\pi}
```

the coupling squared over the face sphere's solid angle 4π and one full turn 2π. For the electron it is 0.00116141, the first term of its anomaly (Schwinger 1948).

The couplings run by the same unit per e-fold of scale: 1/αᵢ(μ) = 1/αᵢ(m\_Z) − (bᵢ/2π) ln(μ/m\_Z), with b = 41/10, −19/6 and −7 read off the register of section 8.1.

**Why the denominator is 8π².**

- **The geometry.** The unit four-dimensional ball has volume π²/2, so the unit 3-sphere has area 2π², its derivative at r = 1.
- **The loop.** A one-loop integral in four dimensions carries ∫d⁴k/(2π)⁴, whose angular part is 2π²/(2π)⁴ = 1/8π². The first correction to any one-phase quantity is one closed loop of that phase, and in four dimensions a closed loop costs exactly this angular share; what stands in front is the problem's own factor, which for the electron's moment is 1.
- **In SI.** α = e²/(4πε₀ħc) and h = 2πħ, so α/2π = e²/(4πε₀hc) = 0.00116141, checked to twelve digits: charge squared, per sphere, per quantum of action per cycle (h, not ħ; the 2π between them is the turn).
- **Schwinger's series.** aₑ = α/2π − 0.328479(α/π)² + 1.181241(α/π)³ − 1.9122(α/π)⁴ + … = 0.00116141 − 0.00000177 + 0.00000001 − … = 0.001159652, against the measured 0.00115965218. The first term is one loop of the one loading phase, the calculator's unit; the rest is QED's series.
- **e and π as a pair.** The exponential is the function equal to its own rate, so for a phasor d/dθ e^(iθ) = i e^(iθ): the rate is the phasor turned a quarter turn, which is the inductive law V = L dI/dt. At θ = π the phasor is −1, so iπ = ln(−1): the half-turn is π on the angle side and is reached through e on the rate side, as √2 and 1/√2 are exchanged by the flip.

**Where the load applies.** The unit sphere touches the cube at the six face points, the level the flip fixes, so a load is a property of one phase, and a state with several phases combines one-phase loads at 120° (section 9.1).

- **The same law in a circuit.** A current already flowing resists change, V = L dI/dt, 90° ahead. Inertia is F = m dv/dt, with momentum as flux and ½mv² as ½LI².
- **Linked loops.** Two linked flux loops share one flux: Gauss's linking integral and mutual inductance. The cube's two √2 circles linked once on the √4 sphere (section 4.6) are that geometry.

**Where it stays slight.** Only where αᵢ/2π times its count is small: 0.00116 for the electron and about 1% for the top. At the strong scale it is not small, and that is where the proton's statics sit far past the grid (section 12.1).

**Standing.**

- **M.** The equal-share half is the virial theorem, equipartition and resonance. The load's size is QED's first term, and the running is the renormalisation group.
- **N.** The claim that the same per-cycle unit sets the mass tables, where standard physics has no rule, was not found. It is tested in section 9.1 and section 17.

### 7.3 Three phases and the start rule

**On the cube.** Down the body diagonal from ’ to ×, the other six corners form a hexagon. It is the six-step sequence of a variable-frequency drive, with ’ and × as the zero vectors.

- **The Clarke transform** (Clarke 1943) is the cube seen down its body diagonal, with its zero row (1, 1, 1)/√3.
- **As logic.** Three inverters in a ring cannot settle and oscillate, with their nodes 120° apart; two in a ring hold. The start rule (the third phase rides the first two) is that contradiction read as a rule.

**One phase rotation.** A 120° turn about that axis is one phase rotation, and three phases on three axes keep |v| = √(3/2)·V\_peak constant (section 4.9.1).

**The line voltage.** It is √3 times the phase voltage: 1 − e^(−i2π/3) = 3/2 + i√3/2 has squared modulus 9/4 + 3/4 = 3, and the two phasors with their difference form the 30-60-90 triangle.

**The ring oscillator, timed.** Three inverters in a ring each have delay t\_d:

- a rising edge at node 0 becomes a falling edge at node 1 after t\_d, a rising edge at node 2 after 2t\_d, and a falling edge back at node 0 after 3t\_d;
- so node 0 is inverted every 3t\_d and the period is 6t\_d;
- node k lags by k·t\_d, which is k × 60° of the period, and is inverted k times, another k × 180°: in all k × 240° ≡ −k × 120°.

A loop of n inverters returns a node's level to itself after n inversions. With n even the level agrees with itself and the loop can rest; with n odd it contradicts itself and must keep changing.

**The start rule as a theorem.** In space, turns do not commute. A quarter turn about x followed by a quarter turn about y has trace 0, so it is a 120° turn, about the body diagonal (1, 1, −1). In quaternions (Hamilton 1843),

```latex
\frac{1+i}{\sqrt2}\cdot\frac{1+j}{\sqrt2}=\frac{1+i+j+k}{2}=\cos 60^{\circ}+\sin 60^{\circ}\,\frac{i+j+k}{\sqrt3}
```

The quaternion that performs the third turn is the tesseract's corner (1, 1, 1, 1)/2 placed on the unit sphere. Once two perpendicular quarter turns have happened, the third phase is their product and is not chosen.

**The covers.** Two 24s and two 48s meet here and are not the same:

- the cube's 24 rotations (S₄) and its 48 symmetries with reflections;
- the 24 Hurwitz units, the binary tetrahedral group 2T: ±1, ±i, ±j, ±k and (±1 ± i ± j ± k)/2, the eight axis units with the tesseract's sixteen normalised corners, closed under multiplication, the vertices of the 24-cell;
- the 48 unit quaternions of the binary octahedral group 2O.

The 48 quaternions double-cover only the 24 rotations and cannot represent a reflection. The 24 Hurwitz units double-cover the 12 rotations that keep the two inscribed tetrahedra in place. (1 + i)/√2 and (1 + j)/√2 are quarter turns, outside 2T because each swaps the two tetrahedra, and their product (1 + i + j + k)/2 is a third turn inside it. Odd times odd is even, and 2O/2T ≅ Z₂ is the rule in one line.

**The couplings cross in pairs.** To one loop each 1/αᵢ is a straight line in ln μ with slope −bᵢ/2π, starting at m\_Z from 1/α₁ = 59.0 (in the √(5/3) normalisation), 1/α₂ = 29.6 and 1/α₃ = 8.45. Three lines meet at one point only if the intercept gaps are proportional to the slope gaps, and the Standard Model's numbers give two different ratios:

- (1/α₁ − 1/α₂)/(b₁ − b₂) = 4.05;
- (1/α₂ − 1/α₃)/(b₂ − b₃) = 5.51.

At one loop the pairs cross at 1.0 × 10¹³, 2.4 × 10¹⁴ and 1.0 × 10¹⁷ GeV; at two loops, with PDG 2026 inputs:

| Pair | Crossing scale | 1/α there |
| --- | --- | --- |
| hypercharge = weak | 1.1 × 10¹³ GeV | 42.3 |
| hypercharge = strong | 1.7 × 10¹⁴ GeV | 40.4 |
| weak = strong | 3.0 × 10¹⁶ GeV | 46.2 |

The three come within 3.2 in 1/α of each other, at 1.7 × 10¹⁴ GeV. Supersymmetric models add particles precisely to change the slopes until the two ratios match (Amaldi, de Boer and Fürstenau 1991).

**sin²θ\_W = 3/8 where the first two phases cross.**

- sin²θ\_W = g′²/(g² + g′²), and hypercharge is normalised as g₁² = (5/3)g′², the 5/3 being Tr Y²/Tr T₃² = (10/3)/2.
- Where α₁ = α₂, g² = (5/3)g′², so sin²θ\_W = 1/(8/3) = 3/8: the fraction of the generation's charge squared that sits in the weak slots, 2/(2 + 10/3).

It runs from there to 0.23122 at the Z.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The power engineering is Steinmetz (1893), Fortescue (1918), Park (1929) and Clarke (1943). The non-commuting turns and Hamilton's rule i·j = k are classical. The running and the pairwise crossing are the Standard Model's: grand unification needs new particles to make the couplings meet (Georgi and Glashow 1974; Amaldi, de Boer and Fürstenau 1991).
- **N.** Reading the pair crossings as the start rule, and the start rule as the parity of the double cover, were not found.

### 7.4 Beside the standard theories

Every number the two rules produce on this layer is a standard number. The calculator's own claims here are two: that the load which gives the electron its first anomalous term also places masses (tested in section 9.4), and that the pairwise crossing of the couplings is the start rule rather than a defect to be repaired.

| three-phase power | constant, since 1 + ω + ω² = 0 (Steinmetz 1893) | the balanced hexagon of the cube's corners | every three-phase grid | same |
