**Origination.** **M** for every number. **N** for the load applied to the mass tables, the three-phase start as the reason the couplings cross in pairs, and the start rule as the parity of the binary octahedral cover.

## 8. Matter: one generation as a 16-state register

One generation of matter, computed on the tesseract, is a 16-state register: five slots, three colour slots worth −⅓ each and two weak slots worth +½ each, with an even number filled. Charge comes in thirds and halves because the grids are the inverse levels squared; every anomaly sum vanishes because the sums are the parity checks of the register's code; and hypercharge's normalisation √(5/3), with sin²θ\_W = 3/8 where the first two couplings meet, is read off the same table. Antimatter is the register turned inside out in all five slots. These are the Standard Model's own representations, reached by a different road; the calculator adds the reasons and no new state.

### 8.1 The register

**The register.** One generation is five slots: three colour slots worth −⅓ each and two weak slots worth +½ each, with an even number filled. That leaves four free slots and 2⁴ = 16 states, the tesseract's corners.

- **The charges.** Y = ½(weak slots filled) − ⅓(colour slots filled), T₃ = ½(first weak slot − second), and Q = Y + T₃.
- **The states.** The 16 are: νᶜ (Y = 0); e⁺ (Y = 1); the quark doublet, three colours each of d (Y = 1/6, Q = −⅓) and u (Q = ⅔); ū (three, Y = −⅔); d̄ (three, Y = ⅓); and the lepton doublet e (Q = −1) and ν (Q = 0), both with Y = −½.

**What the table gives.**

1. **Why sixteen.** Five slots filled or empty give 2⁵ = 32 words, and the parity condition halves them to 16. Without it the 32 would hold the generation and its mirror, the 16-bar of odd parity.
2. **Charge in thirds and halves.** With n\_c colour slots filled and weak slots w₁ and w₂:
   - Y = −n\_c/3 + (w₁ + w₂)/2;
   - T₃ = (w₁ − w₂)/2;
   - Q = −n\_c/3 + w₁.

   Every charge is a count of weak slots minus a third of the colour count, so colour triplets carry ⅓ or ⅔ and singlets 0 or 1. The positive charges on the cube's eight corners, 0, ⅓, ⅔ and 1, are the T and V slots of Harari and Shupe (1979).
3. **Eight bits in both directions.** Complementing the four free slots sends n\_c → 3 − n\_c and w₁ → 1 − w₁, so Q → n\_c/3 − w₁ = −Q for all 16, while Y and T₃ are not negated: e⁺ (Y = 1, T₃ = 0) goes to the e of the doublet (Y = −½, T₃ = −½).
   - **Inside the generation.** This is the tesseract's p → −p on its four free coordinates, determinant +1, a rotation inside the generation.
   - **Charge conjugation proper.** Y → −Y and T₃ → −T₃ is the inside-out of all five slots. It flips the parity and lands in the 16-bar, a reflection.
   - **Gravity.** Neither map touches the three-dimensional cube's face sphere, which is the calculator's reading of why antimatter falls as matter does (section 11.2).
4. **Every balance sum vanishes.**
   - ΣY³ = 6(1/6)³ + 3(−2/3)³ + 3(1/3)³ + 2(−1/2)³ + 1³ = 1/36 − 8/9 + 1/9 − 1/4 + 1 = 0.
   - The SU(2)² sum over doublet members is 6(1/6) + 2(−1/2) = 0.
   - The SU(3)² sum over triplets and anti-triplets is 6(1/6) + 3(−2/3) + 3(1/3) = 0.

   Each is a one-loop triangle with three gauge phases at its corners, whose non-zero value would break a conservation law. Here they are the parity checks of the even-parity code of length 5 (minimum distance 2): one flipped bit leaves the generation.
5. **The hypercharge root.** Tr Y² = 6(1/36) + 3(4/9) + 3(1/9) + 2(1/4) + 1 = 10/3, and Tr T₃² = 8 × (½)² = 2.
6. **The running counts.** For a gauge phase with group G the one-loop coefficient is

   ```latex
   b=-\frac{11}{3}C_2(G)+\frac{2}{3}\sum_{\rm Weyl}T(R)+\frac{1}{3}\sum_{\rm scalar}T(R)
   ```

   for the phase loading itself, the moving spin-½ states it loads, and the held scalar.
   - **U(1)**, in the √(5/3) normalisation: (3/5)\[(2/3) · 3 · (10/3) + (1/3) · 2 · (1/4)\] = 4 + 1/10 = 41/10.
   - **SU(2):** −22/3 + (2/3) · 12 · (1/2) + (1/3) · (1/2) = −19/6.
   - **SU(3):** −11 + (2/3) · 12 · (1/2) = −7.

   A spin-½ state in motion has two helicities and loads a phase with weight ⅔ per Weyl state, a held scalar loads with ⅓, and a non-abelian phase loads itself with −11/3 because its own charge circulates with the opposite sign: asymptotic freedom.
7. **The missing leg.** The W and Z carry a third, longitudinal polarisation that the photon does not (section 3.7.1). It is seen directly: ATLAS observed WZ events with both bosons longitudinally polarised at 7.1σ (ATLAS Collaboration 2023).

**A computer, in the exact sense.**

- **Register.** A corner is a 3-bit word and a tesseract corner a 4-bit word.
- **Instruction set.** The 48 operations are every bit permutation times every bit complement, all reversible (Bennett 1973; Fredkin and Toffoli 1982).
- **Code and clock.** The register is a parity code, and the ring oscillator is its clock.
- **Clock rate.** The fastest orthogonal step takes h/4E (Margolus and Levitin 1998).

**The lineage of the computer reading.** Zuse (1969), Feynman (1982), Fredkin and Toffoli (1982), Wheeler's it-from-bit (1989), Lloyd (2000, 2002) and 't Hooft (2016). What the cube adds is the specific machine:

- the register;
- the instruction set;
- the code;
- the clock;
- a tri-state bus (above, on or below the line: 1, Z, 0);
- the substrate, a semiconductor with a 2mc² gap and Dirac's holes as positive carriers.

**Standing.**

- **M.** This is the Standard Model's generation exactly, and the slot-and-parity construction is the binary code of the SO(10) spinor: Fritzsch and Minkowski (1975), Wilczek and Zee (1982), and the five-slot exposition of Baez and Huerta (2010).
- **N.** The calculator reaches it by another road and adds no state. Its additions are readings: charges as the grids of phase counts, the anomaly conditions as a code's parity checks, antimatter as inside-out, and the register as a reversible machine.

### 8.2 Worked example: a generation step

1. **Frame.** The lepton layer puts √mₑ, √mμ and √mτ on x, y and z. The zero axis ’–× is their common level, and the roots sit on a 45° cone about it.
2. **Motion, not a turn of the cube.** The data point moves a third of a turn round the fixed zero axis, (x, y, z) → (z, x, y), which advances the phase δ + 2πk/3 while the cube stays. One phasor taken three times,

   ```latex
   \sqrt{m_k}=\mu\left[1+\sqrt{2}\cos\!\left(\delta+\tfrac{2\pi k}{3}\right)\right],\qquad \delta=\tfrac{2}{9}
   ```

   gives 1 : 206.77 : 3477.5 against the measured 1 : 206.768 : 3477.37.
3. **Inverse.** The half turn negates all three roots, and the masses and Koide's Q are unchanged. Antileptons have their leptons' masses: e± within 8 × 10⁻⁹ and τ± within 2.8 × 10⁻⁴ (Abe et al. 2007).
4. **Mirror.** The neutrino triplet comes from flipping one root (section 9.1). With the cube fixed, the flip is a half turn in the plane of that root and the held axis, so the neutrinos are reached by turning through the fourth dimension.

### 8.3 The inverse cell: particle against antiparticle

One half turn is CPT, which relativistic quantum field theory guarantees, so the inverse cell must hold every particle's image exactly. Every comparison found agrees within 1.5σ:

- **The positron.** Its g-factor matches the electron's within (−0.5 ± 2.1) × 10⁻¹² (−0.2σ; Van Dyck, Schwinberg and Dehmelt 1987).
- **The antimuon.** Its g-factor matches within −0.9σ and its lifetime within +0.3σ (PDG muon listing).
- **The antiproton.** Its moment is −2.7928473441(42) μ\_N (Smorra et al. 2017), −0.1σ from the proton's; its charge-to-mass ratio matches within 16 parts per trillion (Borchert et al. 2022).
- **The antitop.** The top–antitop mass difference is −0.15 ± 0.21 GeV (CMS Collaboration 2017), −0.7σ.
- **The antitau.** Its mass matches within 2.8 × 10⁻⁴ (Abe et al. 2007).
- **Antihydrogen.** Its 1S–2S line matches hydrogen's to a couple of parts in 10¹² (Ahmadi et al. 2018), its hyperfine splitting gives 1,420.4 ± 0.5 MHz (Ahmadi et al. 2017), and its Lamb shift agrees within 11% (ALPHA Collaboration 2020). It falls down (−1.2σ; Anderson et al. 2023).
- **Neutrino against antineutrino.** Solar neutrinos give Δm²₂₁ = 6.10 +0.95/−0.81 × 10⁻⁵ eV² (Super-Kamiokande Collaboration 2024), against the reactor antineutrinos' (7.50 ± 0.12) × 10⁻⁵ eV² (JUNO Collaboration 2025): −1.5σ. The calculator and CPT both require equality, so this is the one inverse-cell tension to watch.

### 8.4 Beside the standard theories

The register is the Standard Model's generation exactly, including the right-handed neutrino of the SO(10) spinor, so on this layer the two give the same states and the same numbers. The difference is in what each takes as given: the Standard Model takes the hypercharges as inputs and requires the anomalies to cancel, while the calculator derives both from the slot rule.

**Table 32. Matter on one yardstick.**

| Quantity | Standard theory | The calculator | Measured | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| the states of one generation | 15 Weyl states, 16 with a right-handed neutrino (the 16 of SO(10)) | the 16 corners of the tesseract: five slots, even parity | 15 seen; the right-handed neutrino unobserved | same as SO(10); both carry the right-handed neutrino |
| charges in thirds and halves | the hypercharges, as inputs | the grids of the levels √3 and √2 | every quark and lepton charge | exact |
| anomaly cancellation | required for consistency, arranged by the hypercharges | the parity checks of the register's code | the theory's consistency | exact |
| hypercharge's normalisation | Tr Y² ÷ Tr T₃² = (10/3) ÷ 2 = 5/3, a unification input | read off the register | sin²θ\_W runs from 3/8 to 0.23122 at the Z | exact |
| antimatter | the CPT conjugate | the five-slot inside-out, the mirror 16 | the positron's g within 2 × 10⁻¹², the antiproton's moment at −0.1σ, antihydrogen's 1S–2S line within a few parts in 10¹² | same |
| particle against antiparticle | CPT requires equality | the half turn requires equality | solar against reactor Δm²₂₁ at −1.5σ | same; the one tension to watch |
| how many generations | three, an input | three phases, with the fourth level held | N\_ν = 2.9963 ± 0.0074 from the Z width | reading |
| the lepton mass ratios | free Yukawa couplings | one phasor taken three times: 1 : 206.77 : 3,477.5 | 1 : 206.768 : 3,477.37 | new number (section 9.1) |

**Origination.** **M**: the generation is the Standard Model's (Georgi and Glashow 1974), the 16 of SO(10) (Fritzsch and Minkowski 1975; Wilczek and Zee 1982), and the five-slot construction is Baez and Huerta's (2010). **N**: the anomaly sums as the parity checks of an even-parity code, antimatter as the five-slot inside-out, and the running counts read off the register.

## 9. Mass: the balance line and the vacuum's edge

The calculator computes masses with its balance rule: a family of three masses is three phases, balanced when its DC power equals its AC power. The charged leptons sit on that line to a few parts in a million: Q = 0.6666645 ± 0.0000051 against ⅔, an angle of 44.99991° ± 0.00022° to the diagonal, with the τ predicted from the electron and muon at 1,776.969 MeV against 1,776.93 ± 0.09. The same equation, with one root on the flipped branch, gives the neutrino masses: 0.364, 8.66 and 50.13 meV, normal ordering. The Higgs vacuum sits just past its own edge, held by the three-phase load δ₃ = 0.955%, which places the top at 172.57 ± 0.38 GeV and the Higgs at 125.20 ± 0.92 GeV. The Standard Model has no mass relation: every one of these masses is, for it, an input. The lepton relation and its phase are inherited from Koide, Foot and Brannen; the reason, the neutrino reading and the load are the calculator's.

### 9.1 The balance line in the lepton masses

**The charged leptons.** The PDG 2026 masses give Q = 0.6666645 ± 0.0000051, an angle of 44.99991° ± 0.00022° to the diagonal. From the electron and muon alone, Q = ⅔ is a quadratic in √mτ whose heavy root gives mτ = 1776.969 MeV, against 1776.93 ± 0.09. Measured from all three masses, the phase is δ = 0.2222248 ± 0.0000063 rad, 2/9 at 0.4σ. With Q = ⅔ exact the electron and muon fix it: δ = 0.2222220471 ± 0.0000000004 rad, 2/9 to 0.8 parts per million but not on it. Q = ⅔ and δ = 2/9 together would put mμ/mₑ at 206.770316, 442σ from its measured 206.7682827 ± 0.0000046 (section 15.3), so the phase is an output of the balance, not a second rule.

**The charged leptons, step by step.**

- **The family.** The square roots of the PDG 2026 masses are 0.714842, 10.279026 and 42.153648. Their sum is S = 53.147515, so μ = S/3 = 17.715838 √MeV, and Σm = 1883.0994, so Q = 1883.0994/53.147515² = 0.6666645. From Q, a = √(2(3Q − 1)) = 1.414209, and cos θ = 1/√(3Q) gives θ = 44.99991°.
- **The phase.** (√mₑ/μ − 1)/a = (0.040350 − 1)/1.414209 = −0.678577, whose arccosine is 132.73253°. The electron is one phase step from the reference, so δ = 132.73253° − 120° = 12.73253° = 0.2222248 rad.
- **The errors.** They come almost entirely from m\_τ (±0.09 MeV), propagated by Monte Carlo: Q ± 0.0000051, θ ± 0.00022° and δ ± 0.000006 rad.

**The τ from e and μ alone.** Write s = √mₑ + √m\_μ = 10.993868, t = mₑ + m\_μ = 106.169374 and x = √m\_τ.

- **The quadratic.** Q = ⅔ means (s + x)² = (3/2)(t + x²), that is ½x² − 2sx + (3t/2 − s²) = 0, so x = 2s ± √(6s² − 3t) = 21.98774 ± 20.16637.
- **The heavy root.** It gives m\_τ = 1776.969 MeV, against 1776.93 ± 0.09 (−0.4σ), and lies between the two precise methods (Table 75).
- **The light root.** At 3.32 MeV it is the other place a third phase could sit, and nothing does: the balance equation is quadratic in the third root.

**The domain.** The balance rule's quantity is the pole mass of a free excitation of the board that is a Yukawa leg of v. That covers the leptons and the Higgs sector. Quarks, whose masses run, and hadrons, whose mass is 99% field energy, are outside it: at the conventional scales (PDG 2026) the down and up families give Q = 0.732 and 0.845, and the heavy triple c, b, t gives 0.663, values that move with the scale.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The relation is Koide's (1982). The 45° reading is Foot's (1994). The three-phase form with phase near 2/9 is Brannen's (2006), and Żenczykowski (2012) found the phase indistinguishable from 2/9.
- **History.** Koide's relation of 1982 gave 1,777 MeV for the τ when the measured mass was 1,784 ± 3 MeV. The measurement moved to him.
- **N.** The reason, balance read as DC power equal to AC power by the same rule as light and the orbit, was not found. Nor was the phase read as the balance ratio shared over three phases, 3δ = Q; it holds to 0.8 parts per million but cannot hold exactly beside Q = ⅔, so it is withdrawn as a rule (section 15.3).

### 9.2 The neutrino masses

**The neutrinos.** With all three roots positive, the equation cannot reach 45°. With the lightest root on the flipped branch, it crosses Q = ⅔ once:

- **normal ordering.** m₁, m₂, m₃ = 0.364, 8.66 and 50.13 meV, summing to 59.2 meV;
- **inverted ordering.** The solution sums to 102.6 meV, excluded by the 64 meV cap from DESI's second data release with the microwave background (DESI Collaboration 2025).

**The neutrinos, both orderings.** Only the splittings are measured: Δm²₂₁ = 7.49 × 10⁻⁵ eV², and Δm²₃₁ = 2.513 × 10⁻³ eV² for the normal ordering (|Δm²₃₂| the same for the inverted).

- **All roots positive.** Q(m₁) falls from 0.5851 at m₁ → 0 to ⅓ as the three masses become degenerate, and never reaches ⅔.
- **Why a root may flip.** Every root has a ± partner, because the equation is in √m, the physical mass is the square, and the sign is the relative phase of the state's amplitude: exactly the freedom the flip gives every root.
- **The normal ordering.** With the lightest root on the flipped branch, Σ√m falls and Q rises, crossing ⅔ once at m₁ = 0.3642 meV: 0.364, 8.662 and 50.131 meV, sum 59.157 meV.
- **The inverted ordering.** The same branch crosses ⅔ at m₃ = 2.934 meV: 49.464, 50.216 and 2.934 meV, sum 102.61 meV.
- **The other sign patterns.** They never cross ⅔: with √m₂ flipped Q stays between 1.66 and 3.38, and with √m₃ flipped it stays above 3.

Cosmology caps the sum at 64 meV (DESI DR2 with the microwave background, 95%), which excludes the inverted solution and leaves the normal one, 5 meV under the cap and 0.4 meV above the normal ordering's floor of 58.8 meV.

**What can and cannot see these masses.**

- **Too close to the floor.** The sum sits so close to the floor that no planned cosmological or β-decay measurement can tell this solution from m₁ = 0.
- **Double beta decay.** Its effective Majorana mass, m\_ββ = 1.3 to 4.1 meV depending on the Majorana phases, is below the reach of the planned searches:
  - KamLAND-Zen's complete data cap m\_ββ at 28 to 122 meV, with T½ > 3.8 × 10²⁶ yr (KamLAND-Zen Collaboration 2024);
  - GERDA, MAJORANA and LEGEND-200 combined give T½ > 1.9 × 10²⁶ yr (LEGEND Collaboration 2025);
  - LEGEND-1000 is designed to reach about 10 meV.
- **Direct mass.** The β-decay cap, mν < 0.45 eV (KATRIN Collaboration 2025), is a thousand times above the calculator's masses.

What the data can decide is the ordering and the cap.

**The cap is pressing.** DESI's own analysis of its DR2 data lets the cosmological mass run negative as a bookkeeping device, and finds Σmν,eff = −0.101 +0.047/−0.056 eV in ΛCDM, 3.0σ below the normal-ordering floor (Elbers et al. 2025).

- **The limit.** Its profile likelihood's 95% limit, 0.053 eV, is under the floor.
- **What would remove the tension.** It goes away only if w drifts (−0.044 ± 0.084 eV with DESY5 in w₀wₐCDM) or if the optical depth is higher than Planck's (1.7σ at τ = 0.074).
- **How it is read.** DESI reads a negative value as a systematic or as new physics unrelated to neutrinos, and Craig, Green, Meyers and Rajendran (2024) read it the same way.

The calculator has committed to both ends, w = −1 and the floor, so this is one test with two faces. If the preference for a vanishing mass is a systematic, both stand; if it is physics, one of the two gives, since the neutrino masses carry no free parameter.

**Standing.** **M**: the solution with one flipped root is Brannen's (2006) and Rodejohann and Zhang's (2011), recomputed here with the current splittings. **N**: the flip as the reason the solution exists.

### 9.3 The Higgs on the board

This section gives the top and the Higgs from the vacuum's edge and one load in full; the scorecard of section 16.1 carries their rows.

**Inputs.** All numbers use PDG 2026:

- top 172.60 ± 0.27 GeV (direct) and 172.1 ± 0.6 GeV (pole, from cross sections);
- Higgs 125.13 ± 0.11 GeV;
- W 80.3625 ± 0.0077 GeV;
- α\_s(m\_Z) = 0.1180 ± 0.0009.

**Code.** The running is two-loop in all couplings (Luo, Wang and Xiao 2003) with three-loop QCD (Chetyrkin and Zoller 2012). It starts from the MS-bar couplings at μ = M\_t, matched as in Buttazzo et al. (2013), with their theory errors on the matching: ±0.0003 in λ(M\_t) and ±0.0005 in y\_t(M\_t). At M\_t = 172.60 GeV the matched couplings are:

- g₁ = 0.46285 (GUT-normalised);
- g = 0.64759;
- g₃ = 1.16515;
- y\_t = 0.93303;
- λ = 0.12603.

The scripts are listed in section 21.1.

**The bias and the grips.**

- **The bias.** The Higgs field's value v = (√2 G\_F)^(−1/2) = 246.22 GeV is the board's bias. It sets every reaction's gap.
- **The grips.** A particle's grip on the Higgs is its mass over the bias, y = √2·m/v: 0.000607 for the muon, 0.0102 for the τ and 0.991 for the top at its pole mass.
- **The Higgs itself.** The Higgs boson is the board's own amplitude reaction, with mass √(2λ)·v. At tree level λ = m\_H²/2v² = 0.12914.

**Table 33. Rules for the Higgs mass, each fixed before computing.**

| Rule | Says | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Balance (the author's) | the Higgs sits just below its exact edge, 128.55 ± 0.96 GeV | 125.13 is on the predicted side, at 45.77°; a side, not a value |
| Held below its W-pair gap | m\_H < 2m\_W = 160.73 GeV | yes; this is why one W is always virtual in H → WW\* |
| Held at √4, λ = 1/8 (a reading tested here, not the author's) | m\_H = v/2 = 123.11 GeV | a miss: the measured mass is 1.64% higher, 18σ |
| The three-phase load δ₃ (section 9.4) | from the measured top, m\_H = 125.20 ± 0.92 GeV | −0.1σ (section 9.4) |

The board sets every other gap through v. The Higgs's own gap still needs λ, and of the rules in Table 33 only the δ₃ rule of section 9.4 supplies a number for it.

**The decays, as reactions of the board.** Each channel is classed by how it opens:

- **Open.** The pair lies below m\_H, so both partners are real.
- **Held.** The pair gap lies above m\_H, so one partner is virtual.
- **Sub-reaction.** There is no tree-level coupling, so the decay runs through a loop.

**Table 34. The Higgs decays computed as reactions, at m\_H = 125.00 GeV, beside the standard values.**

| Decay | Kind | Computed | Standard |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| b b̄ | open (pair gap 8.4 GeV) | 59.1% | 58.2% |
| W W\* | held: gap 160.7 GeV, one W virtual | 20.9% | 21.4% |
| g g | sub-reaction: top and bottom loops | 7.78% | 8.18% |
| τ τ | open (gap 3.6 GeV) | 6.41% | 6.27% |
| c c̄ | open (gap 2.5 GeV) | 2.93% | 2.89% |
| Z Z\* | held: gap 182.4 GeV, one Z virtual | 2.60% | 2.62% |
| γ γ | sub-reaction: W and top loops | 0.228% | 0.227% |
| μ μ | open (gap 0.21 GeV) | 0.0227% | 0.0218% |
| total width |  | 4.04 MeV | 4.07 MeV |

**How it was computed.** The formulas behind Table 34 are the standard ones (Djouadi 2008). In order:

- **The quark channels.** They use MS-bar masses run to m\_H at three loops, m\_b(m\_H) = 2.79 GeV and m\_c(m\_H) = 0.62 GeV, with the QCD correction to third order in α\_s.
- **The held channels.** Both vector bosons are integrated over their Breit–Wigner shapes.
- **The loops.** They use the spin-½ and spin-1 loop functions A\_{1/2} and A\_1, with a QCD factor of 1.81 for g g.
- **The standard column.** It is the LHC Higgs Working Group's (de Florian et al. 2016).
- **The fit.** Every channel lands within 5%. The rest is the electroweak and higher-order QCD corrections left out, plus Z γ (0.15%), which is not computed.

**Measured.**

- **The rates.** The overall rate is 1.002 ± 0.057 of the prediction (CMS Collaboration 2022) and 1.05 ± 0.06 (ATLAS Collaboration 2022).
- **The width.** From off-shell production, 3.2 +2.4/−1.7 MeV (CMS Collaboration 2022, Nature Physics) and 4.5 +3.3/−2.5 MeV (ATLAS Collaboration 2023).
- **The couplings.** They follow the masses from the muon to the top.

The board reading of the decays is the Standard Model's, renamed. It owns the vocabulary of open, held and sub-reaction channels, and no number.

#### 9.3.1 Why λ runs

Quantum effects make couplings depend on the energy μ at which they are probed. At one loop:

```latex
16\pi^2\,\frac{d\lambda}{d\ln\mu}=-6y_t^{4}+\tfrac{3}{8}\left[2g^{4}+\left(g^{2}+g'^{2}\right)^{2}\right]+24\lambda^{2}+\lambda\left(12y_t^{2}-9g^{2}-3g'^{2}\right)
```

The terms are a tug-of-war:

- the top pulls λ down (−6y\_t⁴);
- the force carriers push it up;
- λ's own term and the mixing terms follow λ.

**Table 35. The one-loop pieces, with the couplings run at two loops.** The first three columns are in units of 1/16π². The last is their sum over 16π², the change in λ per e-fold of energy. From the top mass to the Planck mass is 38.80 e-folds.

| Energy | Top pulls | Carriers push | Self and mixing | Net per e-fold |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10³ GeV | −3.153 | +0.234 | +0.671 | −0.01424 |
| 10¹⁰ GeV | −0.484 | +0.167 | +0.000 | −0.00201 |
| 10¹⁸ GeV | −0.143 | +0.137 | +0.015 | +0.00006 |

In Table 35, the top's pull weakens as y\_t runs down, from 0.851 at 10³ GeV to 0.393 at 10¹⁸ GeV. Near the Planck scale the pull and the push nearly cancel, which is the near-criticality of section 9.4.

**Why it falls.** At the top mass λ(M\_t) = 0.126 and y\_t(M\_t) = 0.933 in the matching of Buttazzo et al. (2013); at tree level λ = m\_H²/2v² = 0.12914. So the −6y\_t⁴ term (−4.5) outweighs 24λ² (+0.4) and λ falls.

#### 9.3.2 What the running shows

**Table 36. λ against energy, two-loop running with three-loop QCD.**

| Energy (GeV) | 10³ | 10⁵ | 10⁷ | 10⁹ | 10¹¹ | 10¹³ | 10¹⁵ | 10¹⁷ | 10¹⁹ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| measured masses (top 172.60, Higgs 125.13) | 0.0965 | 0.0493 | 0.0233 | 0.0078 | −0.0015 | −0.0069 | −0.0097 | −0.0107 | −0.0105 |
| exact edge (top 170.935, Higgs 125.13) | 0.0980 | 0.0542 | 0.0305 | 0.0166 | 0.0083 | 0.0035 | 0.0010 | 0.0001 | 0.0002 |

- **Where it goes.** With the measured masses λ crosses zero at 3.9 × 10¹⁰ GeV and reaches its minimum, −0.01075, at 3.3 × 10¹⁷ GeV. At 1.22 × 10¹⁹ GeV it is −0.0104.
- **Metastable.** The vacuum is metastable. The complete calculation of its lifetime, with the scale-invariant instantons treated in full, gives 10¹³⁹ years for the masses of 2017 (Andreassen, Frost and Schwartz 2018).
- **Code check.** At the inputs of Buttazzo et al. (2013), with top 173.34, Higgs 125.15 and α\_s = 0.1184:
  - this code gives λ(M\_Pl) = −0.0141, against their −0.0143;
  - it puts absolute stability at m\_H > 129.77 GeV, against their 129.6 ± 1.5 GeV.

### 9.4 The edge and the balance rule

**The top and the Higgs.** The Higgs quartic coupling runs negative near 10¹⁰ GeV, so the vacuum is metastable (Degrassi et al. 2012; Buttazzo et al. 2013). The edge, where its minimum is exactly zero, sits at m\_t = 170.93 ± 0.38 GeV for m\_H = 125.13. Two candidate rules are tested against it:

- **Slightly past, held.** The calculator puts nature past the edge by the three-phase load of the top's two phases, its Yukawa coupling and the weak coupling, combined at 120°:

  ```latex
  \delta_3=\frac{\left|\,y_t^{2}+g^{2}e^{i120^{\circ}}\right|}{8\pi^{2}}=0.955\%
  ```

  This gives a top of 172.57 ± 0.38 GeV against 172.60 ± 0.27 from direct measurements and 172.1 ± 0.6 as a pole mass, and a Higgs of 125.20 ± 0.92 GeV against 125.13 ± 0.11.
- **Exactly on the edge.** The proposal that nature sits exactly on the edge (Froggatt and Nielsen 1996; Shaposhnikov and Wetterich 2010), which is not the author's, gives a Higgs of 128.55 ± 0.96 GeV, 3.5σ above the measured 125.13, and a top of 170.94 ± 0.38 GeV, 3.6σ below the direct mass and 1.6σ below the pole mass from cross sections.

**The edge.** The edge is the set of pairs (m\_t, m\_H) for which the minimum of λ over all energies is exactly zero. On the stable side λ never reaches zero; on the metastable side it crosses zero twice, as in Table 36.

- **For the measured Higgs.** The edge top is m\_t = 170.935 ± 0.375 GeV. Its error budget:
  - α\_s, ±0.354;
  - the matching, ±0.113 (λ ±0.072, y\_t ±0.087);
  - the Higgs mass, ±0.055.
- **For the measured top.** The edge Higgs is m\_H = 128.548 ± 0.964 GeV. Its error budget:
  - α\_s, ±0.746;
  - the top mass, ±0.564;
  - the matching, ±0.228.
- **The slope.** Along the edge the Higgs moves 2.02 GeV per GeV of top at m\_t = 171.0, and 2.10 at m\_t = 172.6.

**The balance rule, the author's.** At exactly 45° a balanced block is poised. It takes slightly more to break static balance into a kinetic turn, the way a balancing rock stands until it is pushed a little past its tipping point. The everyday physics behind the rule:

- static friction exceeds sliding friction, which is why things stick and then slip (Amontons 1699; Coulomb 1785);
- earthquakes are this stick-slip on faults (Brace and Byerlee 1966);
- precariously balanced rocks in California have stood through thousands of years of earthquakes, because a rocking block needs more than its static tipping push to fall (Housner 1963; Brune 1996).

For the vacuum, the rule says the measured pair sits just past the edge on the metastable side: a top above 170.94 and a Higgs below 128.55. Both measurements fall on those sides.

- **As angles.** tan θ = 172.60/170.935 gives 45.28° read on the top, and tan θ = 128.548/125.13 gives 45.77° read on the Higgs. They are one offset in two units, because the edge moves about 2 GeV of Higgs per GeV of top.
- **As λ.** λ dips to −0.0108 against 0.126 at the top mass: the balancing rock that has not tipped.

**How far past 45°, from measured contacts.** At 45° the push equals the running resistance. Starting needs the static resistance instead, so the start angle is tan θ = μ\_static/μ\_kinetic.

**Table 37. Start angles from measured friction (OpenStax College Physics, Table 5.1).**

| Contact | Start / run | Start angle |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Teflon on steel | 0.04 / 0.04 = 1.00 | 45.0° |
| bone with joint fluid | 0.016 / 0.015 = 1.07 | 46.8° |
| shoes on wood | 0.9 / 0.7 = 1.29 | 52.1° |
| rubber on wet concrete; waxed wood on wet snow | 1.40 | 54.5° |
| rubber on dry concrete | 1.0 / 0.7 = 1.43 | 55.0° |
| wood on wood; metal on wood; oiled steel | 1.67 | 59.0° |
| dry steel; shoes on ice | 2.00 | 63.4° |
| ice on ice | 3.33 | 73.3° |
| steel on ice | 20 | 87.1° |
| the Higgs vacuum | top 1.0097; Higgs 1.0273 | 45.3° to 45.8° |

- **Always past.** Every measured contact in Table 37 starts at or above 45°, as the rule says. How far past depends on the surfaces, from 0° to 42° past.
- **The vacuum.** It is the slightest of all, 0.3° to 0.8° past, in the class of the slickest contacts.

**The load.** The rule that gives a size reads the offset as a load. The anchor was fixed before computing: the electron's magnet sits past its balance, g = 2, by its first sub-reaction, α/2π = 0.00116141 (measured 0.00115965). Here α is the board's conductance, the base ohms times the conductance quantum over 4 = 1/137.036 (section 7.2).

- **The perpendicular-conductivity rule.** A held system's offset is the coupling of the reaction carrying its perpendicular current, at its own mass scale, over 2π, carried by that reaction's mass.
- **The vacuum's conductor.** For the vacuum the conductor is the top.
- **The overlap version.** The start cannot use all three phases, because three phases add to zero. So the start rides the sum of the two previous phases, 120° apart:
  - the conductor (the top, y\_t² = 0.8705);
  - the magnetic spin (the weak field, g² = 0.4194).
  - Gravity's part, (m\_t/M̄\_Pl)² = 5.0 × 10⁻³³ at the top's mass, is the tiny third.

**The load, worked.** With y\_t = 0.9330 and g = 0.6476 at M\_t = 172.60 GeV, |0.8705 + 0.4194(−½ + i√3/2)| = |0.6608 + 0.3632i| = 0.7540, over 8π² = 78.957, so δ₃ = 0.955%.

- **The phase read from the masses.** Solved for the angle that lands the top exactly, the phase is 117.9°, with a 1σ range of 87° to 149°. Three-phase's 120° sits in the middle of it.
- **Against the pole mass.** The rule's 172.57 is 0.7σ above the pole mass from cross sections, 172.1 ± 0.6.
- **The honest count.** δ₃ is about the twentieth candidate load tried, and while the edge is known to ±0.38 GeV any top load from 0.70% to 1.24% passes at 1σ. So the closeness is a lead, not evidence, and section 17 scores it that way.

**What decides it.** The top's direct mass carries a scheme ambiguity of about 0.5 GeV, since it is a quark measured through simulations (Hoang 2020). An e⁺e⁻ threshold scan measures a mass with a clean definition, to about ±0.035 GeV (a 2025 study of the scan; arXiv:2503.18713).

- **The edge then.** With α\_s known to ±0.0002 and the Higgs to ±0.05 GeV, the rule's top prediction tightens to ±0.14 GeV, with the matching's ±0.11 then the largest part.
- **Telling the loads apart.** Three-phase (172.57) and the nearest rival load, the top's coupling alone, y\_t²/8π² (172.82), are 0.25 GeV apart, 1.7σ at that precision.
- **The rule's failure.** A pole mass at or below the edge would put the vacuum on the stable side and break the balance rule outright.

**Standing.** **N**: the balance reading, slightly past the edge and held, is the author's; the δ₃ load at 120° is new, but it was about the twentieth candidate tried, so it is a lead and not evidence (section 17). **D**: “slightly past the edge” diverges from the proposals that put nature exactly on it (Froggatt and Nielsen 1996; Shaposhnikov and Wetterich 2010), which miss today by 3.5σ on the Higgs.

### 9.5 Gravity at the boundary

**Gravity sets a boundary.** Above the scale where gravity takes over, it adds a term A·λ to λ's running, and the prediction needs A > 0 (Shaposhnikov and Wetterich 2010):

```latex
\frac{d\lambda}{d\ln\mu}=A\,\lambda+B
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\lambda(t)=-\frac{B}{A}+\left(\lambda_0+\frac{B}{A}\right)e^{A(t-T)}
```

Flowing down from any starting value λ₀ at a high scale T, with A > 0, gives λ = −B/A at the boundary. Here B is the Standard Model running at λ = 0 at the reduced Planck mass, 2.435 × 10¹⁸ GeV. It is B = +4.17 × 10⁻⁶ per e-fold: tiny, because the Standard Model is near-critical there.

**Table 38. The boundary for several strengths of gravity.**

| Gravity's strength A (per e-fold) | λ at the boundary | Higgs it predicts |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1.0 | −4.2 × 10⁻⁶ | 128.55 GeV |
| 0.3 | −1.4 × 10⁻⁵ | 128.54 GeV |
| 0.1 | −4.2 × 10⁻⁵ | 128.54 GeV |
| 0.03 | −1.4 × 10⁻⁴ | 128.51 GeV |
| needed for 125.13 | −0.0107 | A = 3.9 × 10⁻⁴ |

- **The prediction.** The gravity boundary of Table 38 gives m\_H = 128.55 ± 0.96 GeV, 3.5σ high: the exact edge to 0.01 GeV, because λ's minimum sits near the Planck scale.
- **Validation.** Shaposhnikov and Wetterich used m\_t = 171.3 GeV and predicted 126 GeV, “with only a few GeV uncertainty”. This code at m\_t = 171.3 gives 125.86 GeV.
- **What would land it.** The prediction moves 2.09 GeV per GeV of top. The top that lands it on 125.13 is 170.945 GeV.
- **The double root.** A double root at the Planck mass, λ = 0 and β\_λ = 0 together (the multiple-point principle of Froggatt and Nielsen 1996), fixes both masses:
  - m\_t = 174.29 ± 0.57 GeV, −2.7σ;
  - m\_H = 132.13 ± 0.47 GeV, −14.5σ.
  - At 1995 precision the same principle gave 173 ± 5 and 135 ± 9 GeV, which today sit at −0.1σ and −1.1σ.

**Gravity and the decay of the vacuum.** The flat-space bounce action is S₀ = 8π²/(3|λ|). Gravity's leading correction is ΔS = 256π³/\[45(R M̄\_Pl λ)²\], with bounce size R = 1/μ (Isidori, Rychkov, Strumia and Tetradis 2008). Each is minimised against the survival requirement for 13.8 billion years, 4 ln(τ\_U μ).

**Table 39. The bounce at leading order.**

|  | Action | Its scale | Survival margin | Lifetime at leading order | Already decayed if |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| flat space | 2,448 | 4.6 × 10¹⁷ GeV | 1,900 | about 10²¹⁶ years | m\_t > 178.11 GeV |
| with gravity | 2,559 | 1.0 × 10¹⁶ GeV | 2,027 | about 10²³⁰ years | m\_t > 178.31 GeV |

Table 39 shows that gravity makes the vacuum slightly more stable and moves the cheapest bubble to a larger size. Isidori et al. reach the same conclusion: gravitational corrections “only induce a minor shift” on the instability border. The leading-order lifetimes compare the two columns; they are not the lifetime. The complete calculation, with the fluctuation determinant and the scale-invariant instantons, gives 10¹³⁹ years for the masses of 2017 (Andreassen, Frost and Schwartz 2018).

The load times the bounce is exactly ⅓: (|λ|/8π²) × 8π²/(3|λ|) = 1/3, an identity of the formula and not a result (section 4.7.3).

**Gravity's direct effect.** At collider energies gravity's direct effect is (m\_H/M̄\_Pl)² = (125.13/2.435 × 10¹⁸)² = 2.6 × 10⁻³³. Below the Planck scale it changes nothing measurable in the Higgs, and acts only through the boundary. The Higgs vacuum's own energy, and the 10^54.7 by which it overshoots the dark energy, are in section 13.

**Table 40. What the Standard Model itself predicts, against measurement.** Where the calculator restates the Standard Model, it predicts exactly this well, and no better.

| Prediction | Predicted | Measured | Pull |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| W mass, global electroweak fit | 80.356 ± 0.005 GeV | 80.3625 ± 0.0077 GeV | +0.7σ |
| top mass, global electroweak fit | 172.85 ± 0.55 GeV | 172.60 ± 0.27 GeV | −0.4σ |
| top mass before its discovery, 1994 fits | about 145 to 185 GeV | 176 ± 13 GeV (CDF 1995); 199 ± 30 GeV (DØ 1995) | inside |
| Higgs mass before its discovery, 2011 fit without direct searches | 91 +30/−23 GeV | 125.13 ± 0.11 GeV | +1.1σ |
| Higgs mass ceiling from unitarity, √(16π/3)·v | below 1,007.9 GeV | 125.13 GeV | passes |
| Higgs couplings to W and Z scale with mass | κ\_V = 1 | 1.023 ± 0.026 | +0.9σ |
| Higgs couplings to fermions scale with mass | κ\_F = 1 | 0.94 ± 0.05 | −1.2σ |
| Higgs self-coupling, trilinear 3m\_H²/v = 190.8 GeV | κ\_λ = 1 | −1.24 to 6.49 at 95% (CMS 2022) | inside |

Sources for Table 40: the fit values from PDG's electroweak review (2024); Gfitter (Baak et al. 2012); Lee, Quigg and Thacker (1977); CDF Collaboration (1995) and DØ Collaboration (1995); the coupling modifiers from PDG (2024). The W and top fit values are relations among many measurements rather than independent forecasts.

### 9.6 The top-mass measurements

There are two kinds of top-mass measurement:

- **Direct.** The mass is fitted to simulations, and it is the simulation's mass setting.
- **Pole.** The mass is extracted from a rate computed with a cleanly defined mass.

The two may differ by about 0.5 GeV (Hoang 2020).

**A pole mass worked out from the cross section.** ATLAS's Run 2 review gives the inputs (ATLAS Collaboration 2025, Physics Reports):

- **The prediction.** 834 +21/−30 (scale) ± 21 (PDF and α\_s) pb at m\_t = 172.5 GeV, falling 23 pb per GeV of mass.
- **The measurement.** 829.0 ± 1.0 ± 13.2 ± 8.0 pb with 140 fb⁻¹.
- **The mass.** m\_t(pole) = 172.5 + (834 − 829)/23 = 172.72 +1.46/−1.73 GeV, with the experiment ±0.67 and the theory +1.29/−1.59.
- **What it would take.** Reaching ±0.5 GeV needs the prediction to ±12 pb, 1.4%.

Table 41 sets every top-mass result, direct and pole, against the rule and against the exact edge.

**Table 41. Top-mass results against the rule (172.57 ± 0.38) and the exact edge (170.94 ± 0.38).**

| Result | Kind | Value (GeV) | Against the rule | Against the edge |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PDG 2026 average | direct | 172.60 ± 0.27 | +0.1σ | +3.6σ |
| PDG 2026 average | pole | 172.1 ± 0.6 | −0.7σ | +1.6σ |
| ATLAS Run 1 combination | direct | 172.69 ± 0.48 | +0.2σ | +2.9σ |
| ATLAS and CMS Run 1 combination | direct | 172.52 ± 0.33 | −0.1σ | +3.2σ |
| ATLAS Run 2, soft muon, 36 fb⁻¹ | direct | 174.41 ± 0.81 | +2.1σ | +3.9σ |
| ATLAS Run 2, dilepton, 140 fb⁻¹ (preliminary) | direct | 172.21 ± 0.80 | −0.4σ | +1.4σ |
| ATLAS cross section, 36 fb⁻¹ | pole | 173.1 +2.0/−2.1 | +0.3σ | +1.0σ |
| ATLAS cross section, 140 fb⁻¹ (worked out above) | pole | 172.72 +1.46/−1.73 | +0.1σ | +1.0σ |
| ATLAS, top pair plus one jet, 13 TeV (2025) | pole | 170.7 ± 1.5 | −1.2σ | −0.2σ |

Sources: the averages, PDG 2026; the ATLAS Run 1 combination, ATLAS Collaboration (2019, Eur. Phys. J. C 79, 290); the ATLAS and CMS Run 1 combination, ATLAS and CMS Collaborations (2024, Phys. Rev. Lett. 132, 261902); the soft-muon result, ATLAS Collaboration (2023, JHEP 06, 019); the cross section at 36 fb⁻¹, ATLAS Collaboration (2020, Eur. Phys. J. C 80, 528); the 140 fb⁻¹ cross section, the Run 2 review (ATLAS Collaboration 2025, Physics Reports); the top pair plus one jet, ATLAS Collaboration (2025, JHEP 12, 023). The preliminary dilepton result could not be traced to a public record and is kept as reported.

- **The direct results** sit up to 3.9σ above the edge and within about 2σ of the rule.
- **The pole results** cannot yet tell the two apart.
- **The 2012 bound.** Alekhin, Djouadi and Moch (2012) found a pole mass of 173.3 ± 2.8 GeV from the cross section, and said full stability needs m\_H ≥ 129.8 ± 5.6 GeV. This code at 173.3 GeV gives 130.02 GeV.

**The peaks rebuilt from public collision data.** ATLAS released 10 fb⁻¹ of its 2016 proton collisions at 13 TeV for education, with matching simulations (ATLAS Collaboration 2020, open data).

- **The method.** Heavy particles fall apart before reaching the detector, so the pieces are added up, m² = E² − p², and real decays pile up into a peak on a smooth background.
- **The calibration.** Measured mass = the simulation's true mass + (data peak − simulation peak).

**Table 42. The Higgs peak.**

| Channel | Selection | Events | Simulation shift | Calibrated mass | Signal |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| two photons | two tight, isolated photons; p\_T above 35% and 25% of the mass; 105 to 160 GeV | 76,562 | +0.05 GeV | 124.43 +1.00/−1.05 GeV | about 357 events, 2.5σ local |
| four leptons | four tight, isolated electrons or muons; total charge 0; same-flavour pairs; p\_T above 25, 15 and 10 GeV | 228 | +0.96 GeV | 124.06 +0.76/−0.80 GeV | about 13 events, 4.1σ against flat |

- **Combined.** Weighted by uncertainty, the two channels of Table 42 give 124.20 ± 0.62 GeV, statistical only.
- **The energy scale.** The Z checks it: data minus simulation is +3 ± 8 MeV in 867,054 electron pairs and −26 ± 6 MeV in 1,068,835 muon pairs. These are offsets of +0.003% and −0.029%, worth at most 0.04 GeV at 125 GeV. Photons are assumed to follow electrons.

**The top peak.**

- **The selection.**
  - One tight, isolated electron or muon with p\_T > 30 GeV.
  - Missing momentum above 30 GeV and a transverse W mass above 30 GeV.
  - At least four jets with p\_T > 30 GeV and |η| < 2.5, exactly two of them b-tagged.
- **The W candidate** is the light-jet pair with mass closest to 80.4 GeV. The top candidate is that pair plus the b-jet giving the larger top p\_T.
- **The W constraint.** The two light jets are scaled so that their pair mass is exactly 80.4 GeV.
- **The template.** The simulation (Powheg-Box with Pythia 6, m\_t = 172.5 GeV, single top added) is shifted to match the data, with free normalisation and a straight-line background. Table 43 gives the steps and the result.

**Table 43. The top mass from the open data.**

| Step | Value |
| --- | --- |
| events selected | 138,027 (the simulation expects 133,761 from top pairs and single top) |
| W peak, data − simulation (exactly two light jets) | +0.60 ± 0.16 GeV: jets 0.74 ± 0.20% high in data |
| top peak, W-constrained, data − simulation | +0.53 ± 0.18 GeV (χ²/ndf = 26.1/27) |
| jet-scale correction (b-jets 0.65 GeV per 1%; light jets 0.01 GeV per 1%) | −0.48 GeV |
| top mass | 172.54 ± 0.23 (stat) ± 0.92 (syst) GeV |

**The uncertainties.**

- **The total, 0.94 GeV.**
  - statistics, 0.23;
  - the b-jet scale against light jets, assumed up to 1%, 0.65;
  - signal modelling, 0.64, using the sizes of ATLAS's 140 fb⁻¹ dilepton analysis (matching 0.40, radiation 0.17, colour reconnection 0.27, recoil 0.39);
  - fit method, 0.10;
  - calibration slope, 0.01.
- **The cross-checks.**
  - The four data periods give shifts of +0.12 ± 0.72, +0.46 ± 0.38, +0.78 ± 0.33 and +0.44 ± 0.28 GeV (χ² = 1.0 for 3 degrees of freedom).
  - The raw three-jet mass gives 172.90 ± 0.45 (stat).
  - Leaving the b-jets uncorrected gives 173.03.

**Where the rebuild sits.**

- **The Higgs, 124.20 ± 0.62:**
  - −1.5σ against PDG 2026;
  - −0.9σ against the rule's 125.20 ± 0.92;
  - −3.8σ against the exact edge.
- **The top, 172.54 ± 0.94:**
  - −0.1σ against the direct average;
  - +0.4σ against the pole average;
  - 0.0σ against the rule;
  - +1.6σ against the edge.

**The rebuild's limits.**

- **Not for publication.** The release is educational; CERN says it “is not suited for scientific publications”.
- **One simulation.** There is only one top simulation, so the calibration slope is assumed and the modelling uncertainty is borrowed.
- **The b-jet scale** is assumed to follow the light jets within 1%. It is the largest uncertainty.
- **Direct, not pole.** The top is a direct-type mass.
- **The Higgs errors** are statistical only, and the two-photon bump is 2.5σ.
- **The sample.** The selections are simple cuts on 7% of ATLAS's Run 2 data.

Telling 171.0 from 172.5 at 3σ needs about ±0.5 GeV on a pole-type mass, so the threshold scan stays the decider.

### 9.7 The W–Z triangle and the filled leg

The converter's rule, that going from two dimensions to three needs one leg the two-dimensional data cannot supply (section 3.7.1), is the Higgs mechanism's. The Higgs mass matrix in the (W₃, B) block has rank one, so one combination, the photon, gets no mass, and the Z gets (g² + g′²)v²/4 while the W gets g²v²/4. Hence m\_Z² = m\_W² + (g′v/2)² exactly: a right triangle whose legs are the W mass and g′v/2.

Table 44 reads the W and Z masses as a right triangle: the Z on the hypotenuse, the W on one leg and the hypercharge part on the other.

**Table 44. The W–Z triangle from the PDG 2026 masses.**

| Side | Value | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| hypotenuse, m\_Z | 91.1880 ± 0.0020 GeV | PDG 2026 |
| leg, m\_W | 80.3625 ± 0.0077 GeV | PDG 2026 |
| leg, g′v/2 = √(m\_Z² − m\_W²) | 43.094 GeV | computed |
| angle, θ\_W = arccos(m\_W/m\_Z) | 28.20°, so sin²θ\_W = 0.22334 on shell | computed |

The count closes: four carriers with two legs each and four Higgs components become three carriers with three legs, a photon with two, and one Higgs boson, 12 = 12 (Goldstone's theorem). This is the Standard Model at tree level, read as the converter's filled leg.

**Standing.** **M**: the mass matrix and the count are standard. **N**: the W and Z as the converter's third leg, filled by a phase.

### 9.8 Standing, beside the standard theories

The Standard Model predicts none of the masses on this layer; each is a free parameter. The one standard-adjacent rule that does fix a mass, a vacuum sitting exactly on its edge, misses the Higgs by 3.5σ. The calculator's numbers sit within 0.7σ of their measurements. Two facts temper that, and section 17 weighs both: the lepton relation is inherited, and the δ₃ load was chosen from about twenty candidates.

| the τ from e and μ | no prediction | 1,776.969 MeV | 1,776.93 ± 0.09 MeV | −0.4σ | inherited |
| the lepton phase δ | no prediction | 0.2222220, the output of Q = ⅔; 2/9 exactly is excluded beside it (section 15.3) | 0.2222248 ± 0.0000063 | +0.4σ | inherited (Brannen 2006); the reading 3δ = Q withdrawn |
| the neutrino masses | none in the minimal Standard Model; three free parameters when extended | 0.364, 8.66 and 50.13 meV, Σ = 59.2 meV, normal ordering | Σ < 64 meV (95%); the ΛCDM effective mass −0.101 +0.047/−0.056 eV | inside the cap; −3.4σ against the effective mass | prediction; the exposure is shared with w = −1 |
| the top mass | an input | 172.57 ± 0.38 GeV | 172.60 ± 0.27 direct; 172.1 ± 0.6 pole | +0.1σ; −0.7σ | new number; a lead |

**Origination.** **M**: the running, the matching, the edge, the bounce, the decay widths and the field and eddy-current bounds, each with its source given above. **N**: the balance reading, slightly past the edge and held; the δ₃ load at 120°; the board's vocabulary for the decays. **D**: slightly past the edge, against exactly on it.

## 10. Mixing: the Clarke frame tilted 15°

Neutrinos change flavour because the states with a definite mass are turned against the states with a definite flavour. The calculator computes that turn as the electrician's Clarke frame, the cube seen down its body diagonal, left by one rotation of 15°, the ladder's step from √2 to √3. The frame alone is tribimaximal mixing; the 15° tilt gives sin²θ₁₃ = 0.02233 and sin²θ₁₂ = 0.3181, against 0.02200 ± 0.00056 and 0.3096 +0.0057/−0.0073. Quarks mix by a different rule, by magnitude rather than frame, and the unitarity triangle's angle α is taken as 90°. The Standard Model has none of these angles as predictions; each is an input.

### 10.1 The Clarke frame, tilted

**The frame.** The orthonormal Clarke transform and the tribimaximal matrix are one matrix:

```latex
K=\begin{pmatrix}2/\sqrt6 & -1/\sqrt6 & -1/\sqrt6\\ 0 & 1/\sqrt2 & -1/\sqrt2\\ 1/\sqrt3 & 1/\sqrt3 & 1/\sqrt3\end{pmatrix},\qquad U_{TBM}=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{2/3} & 1/\sqrt3 & 0\\ -1/\sqrt6 & 1/\sqrt3 & -1/\sqrt2\\ -1/\sqrt6 & 1/\sqrt3 & 1/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}
```

- **The match.** The rows of K, the α axis, the β axis and the zero axis (the body diagonal), are the columns of U\_TBM up to sign and order: U\_TBM is Kᵀ with the second and third rows of K swapped and one sign flipped.
- **The angles.** sin²θ₁₂ = |U\_e2|² = ⅓, sin²θ₂₃ = |U\_μ3|²/(1 − |U\_e3|²) = ½ and sin θ₁₃ = |U\_e3| = 0.
- **Why the same frame.** It is the frame adapted to three equal phases 120° apart: one axis along the sum (1, 1, 1)/√3, the other two spanning the plane in which the three-phase vector turns. Power engineering needs it to separate the rotating part from the zero sequence; neutrino physics needs it when the mass states sit symmetrically among three flavours. The cube's rotation group S₄ is the symmetry of that arrangement (Lam 2008).

**The tilt.** Rotate in the plane of the second and third columns, keeping the first column (2, −1, −1)/√6 fixed: U = U\_TBM R₂₃(θ). Then U\_e3 = −sin θ/√3, so sin²θ₁₃ = sin²θ/3, while |U\_e1|² = ⅔ = cos²θ₁₂ cos²θ₁₃ is unchanged:

```latex
\sin^2\theta_{12}=1-\frac{2}{3(1-\sin^2\theta_{13})}=\frac{1-3\sin^2\theta_{13}}{3(1-\sin^2\theta_{13})}
```

- **The measured tilt.** From sin²θ₁₃ = 0.02200 ± 0.00056 it is θ = 14.9° ± 0.2°.
- **The rival, TM2.** Keeping the diagonal fixed instead fixes |U\_e2|² = ⅓ and forces sin²θ₁₂ = 1/(3(1 − sin²θ₁₃)) = 0.341. That is 3.6σ above JUNO's first result and 5.5σ above the NuFIT 6.1 value, so the data pick the α axis: the axis that survives is the one the start fixes first.
- **The ladder's angle.** The step from √2 to √3 is 45° − 30° = 15°, the step between the 45° and 30° lines of the (2, 1) cell. Its nearest strict rival, arctan(1/4) = 14.04°, sits 4σ from the measured tilt. Among the forty strictly defined cube angles, 15° is the only one within 3σ of the measured tilt, a chance of 0.12. Among a generous set of 1,422 angles, 32 lie within 3σ, so a generous reading is not selective. Table 46 sets the tilted frame against the data.

**Table 46. The tilted frame against the data.**

| Quantity | The exact frame | Tilted 15° (the calculator) | Measured | Pull |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| sin²θ₁₃ | 0 | 0.022329 | 0.02200 ± 0.00056 (NuFIT 6.0, as used by Esteban et al. 2026) | −0.6σ |
| sin²θ₁₂ | ⅓ | 0.318107 | 0.3092 ± 0.0087 (JUNO's first 59 days); 0.3096 +0.0057/−0.0073 (NuFIT 6.1); 0.3085 ± 0.0073 (Capozzi et al. 2026) | −1.0σ; −1.5σ; −1.3σ |
| sin²θ₂₃ | ½ | correlated with δ\_CP | 0.561 ± 0.014 (NuFIT); 0.56 +0.03/−0.05 (T2K and NOvA 2025) | not fixed by the frame |
| cos δ\_CP | — | +0.27 in the upper θ₂₃ octant (0.561), −0.27 in the lower (0.44) | best fit −0.47π = −85° (T2K and NOvA 2025) | consistent; DUNE, Hyper-Kamiokande and the ordering decide |

**The CP phase.** In the tilted frame the Dirac phase is tied to the atmospheric angle (Albright and Rodejohann 2009):

```latex
\cos\delta_{CP}=-\frac{1-5\sin^2\theta_{13}}{2\sqrt2\,\sin\theta_{13}\sqrt{1-3\sin^2\theta_{13}}\,\tan2\theta_{23}}
```

- **The octants.** The sign of tan 2θ₂₃ flips across 45°, which is the whole difference between the octants; the frame gives θ₂₃ no value of its own.
- **The data.** The first joint T2K and NOvA fit puts the best-fit phase at −85°, against the frame's ±75° at sin²θ₂₃ = 0.56 and ±90° at maximal mixing (T2K and NOvA Collaborations 2025). The frame says the phase is near-maximal, and the data lean that way, with CP conservation excluded at 3σ only if the ordering is inverted.

**What decides it.** JUNO began taking data in August 2025. Its first 59 days give sin²θ₁₂ = 0.3092 ± 0.0087 and Δm²₂₁ = (7.50 ± 0.12) × 10⁻⁵ eV², already 1.6 times more precise than every earlier measurement combined (JUNO Collaboration 2025).

- **The global fits.** Those that fold JUNO in moved the centre towards the calculator, from 0.307 to 0.309.
- **The first-column pattern stands.** The confrontation of discrete-symmetry patterns with JUNO leaves the first-column-fixed pattern, this one, standing. It projects JUNO's six-year 3σ band at 0.3022 to 0.3118 if the centre holds, which would put 0.318 outside it (Ding, Li, Lu and Petcov 2025).
- **A second rival.** A rigid rotation of the untilted frame about (2, 1, 2)/3 by three quarters of the Cabibbo angle gives sin²θ₁₂ = 0.301 and sin²θ₁₃ = 0.0216 (Sharma and Katoch 2026).
- **The full run.** At ±0.0015:
  - a result at 0.307 excludes the tilted frame at 7σ;
  - a result at 0.318 excludes the rival at about 11σ.
- **The untilted frame is already out.** θ₁₃ = 0 has been excluded since Daya Bay (2012), and is 38σ away today.

**Quark mixing: magnitude, not frame.**

- **Two sectors.** The lepton angles contain no mass: 35.3°, 45° and 0° come from the frame alone, then the tilt. The quark angles contain nothing but mass. The up and down quarks share the frame, it cancels in V = U\_u†U\_d, and what is left is one planar triangle per pair of generations, with legs √m.
- **The Cabibbo angle.** It is the down triangle's: tan θ\_C = √(m\_d/m\_s) (Gatto, Sartori and Tonin 1968; Weinberg 1977). Behind it is a 2 × 2 mass block with a zero in the light slot. The off-diagonal entry is √(m\_d m\_s) = 20.9 MeV and m\_d = (20.9 MeV)²/m\_s: the seesaw shape again.
- **Two perpendicular triangles.** The up triangle's leg is √(m\_u/m\_c) = 0.0449 (2.6°) at 2 GeV. The angle between the two legs, fitted from |V\_us|, is 83.5° to 86° ± 2°, so sin²θ\_C ≈ m\_d/m\_s + m\_u/m\_c (Fritzsch 1977). Fritzsch and Xing (2000) tie that angle to the unitarity triangle's α and predict a right-angled triangle; the calculator takes α = 90° as its value. The legs themselves miss perpendicular by 2.0σ on lattice ratios and 3.1σ with PDG masses, so by the behavior rule they do not supply the right angle, and α = 90° stands as a value (section 15.3). Table 47 sets the two-leg values against the measurements.
- **Where the legs fail.** Every leg that includes the top fails:
  - V\_cb = 0.0407 ± 0.0013 against at best 0.077 from √(m\_s/m\_b) and √(m\_c/m\_t);
  - V\_ub = 0.00389 ± 0.00016 against √(m\_u/m\_t) = 0.0028.
- **Why the top is different.** The top is the one quark whose magnitude is the Higgs scale itself, y\_t = 0.9914 ± 0.0016, held there by the infrared fixed point of its Yukawa coupling (Pendleton and Ross 1981; Hill 1981). Its mass is set by v, not by a texture of legs, so the leg rule holds where the legs are small beside v and stops where the leg is v.

**Table 47. Quark mixing by legs.**

| Quantity | Two-leg value | Measured | Pull | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| sin θ\_C | √(m\_d/m\_s) = 0.2249 ± 0.0014 (PDG 2026 masses); 0.2230 ± 0.0013 (lattice ratios) | \|V\_us\| = 0.2243 ± 0.0009 | −0.4σ; +0.8σ | standard (Gatto, Sartori and Tonin 1968); not counted as the calculator's |
| angle between the up and down legs | 90°, if the legs supplied α | 83.5° ± 2.1° (PDG 2026 masses); 85.9° ± 2.0° (lattice ratios) | −3.1σ; −2.0σ | excluded at 2σ: the legs do not supply the right angle (section 15.3) |
| unitarity-triangle angle α | 90° | 84.1 +3.7/−3.0° (all channels, PDG 2026); 92.6 +4.5/−4.7° (B → ρρ alone, Belle II Collaboration 2025); 180° − β − γ = 91.0 ± 2.8° (HFLAV's γ) or 89.4 ± 4.0° (PDG's γ = 68 ± 4°) | −1.6σ; +0.6σ; +0.4σ; −0.2σ | a prediction; the ρρ channel brackets 90° from above and the all-channel average from below |
| V\_cb, V\_ub | no leg value | 0.0407 ± 0.0013; 0.00389 ± 0.00016 | — | the boundary: every leg that includes the top misses by 1.4 to 1.9 times |

**Standing.**

- **M.** Tribimaximal mixing, the TM1 sum rule, the Gatto–Sartori–Tonin relation and the right-angled unitarity triangle are published.
- **N.** Three things were not found: the identification of the 1943 Clarke transform with the tribimaximal matrix; the 15° tilt taken from the ladder; and the reason the first column survives (the first two phases fix the α axis before the third rides their sum).
- **D.** A rival tilt, a rigid rotation of the untilted frame by three quarters of the Cabibbo angle (Sharma and Katoch 2026), gives 0.301. JUNO's full run separates the two at about 11σ.

### 10.2 Beside the standard theories

The Standard Model takes all four lepton mixing parameters and all four quark mixing parameters as inputs, so on this layer it predicts nothing to compare. The fair comparison is with the other frames that flavour symmetries propose, scored on the same data: the untilted frame is out at 38σ, the frame that keeps the diagonal fixed is out at 5.5σ, and the tilted Clarke frame and one rival remain for JUNO to separate.

## 11. Gravity, held states and black holes

Computed on the calculator, gravity is a pull along the perpendicular, the time leg, and anything that also moves along the space leg feels both. Light, on the 45° line, has equal legs, so it bends twice what the time leg alone gives: 1.7512″ at the Sun's edge. A gyroscope in orbit precesses by the time leg plus half the space leg, 6,604.8 mas/yr, and frame dragging adds 39.1 mas/yr. The black hole is the flip, inversion through a sphere whose fixed sphere is the horizon; what falls in is held, and at most 1 − 1/√2 = 29.3% of a spinning hole's mass can come back. Held states, from positronium to toponium to ball lightning, are states parked at the √4 level. Every classical number here is general relativity's; the calculator's own content is the reading of the interior as the held foundation and of held states as one family.

### 11.1 The two legs of the 45° line, the flip and the held level

**Two legs.** Gravity is read as a pull along the time leg; light, on the 45° line, takes both legs: 1.7512″ of bending at the Sun's edge, 6,604.8 mas/yr of geodetic precession and 39.1 mas/yr of frame dragging for Gravity Probe B, each derived below. Table 49 sets each of gravity's readings beside its route, its prediction and the measurement.

The law of section 4.11 makes the reading exact. Integrated with both legs, Mercury's perihelion advances 42.98″ per century, as measured; with the time leg alone, 14.33″.

**The bending's factor of two.** In the weak field ds² = −(1 + 2Φ/c²)c²dt² + (1 − 2γΦ/c²)dx², with Φ = −GM/r, and a light ray is deflected by (1 + γ)·2GM/(c²b).

- **The two terms.** The first comes from the time part (Newton's particle falling in the potential), the second from the curvature of space.
- **The numbers.** With GM☉ = 1.32712 × 10²⁰ m³/s² and b = 6.957 × 10⁸ m, 2GM/(c²b) = 4.245 × 10⁻⁶ rad = 0.8756″, and with γ = 1 the full 1.7512″.
- **Why light collects both.** A slow body's path is almost all time, so it feels only the time leg; light moves equally along both, the 45° line.

**The gyroscope.**

- **Geodetic.** On a circular orbit of radius r a gyroscope precesses at Ω\_geo = (½ + γ)(GM)^(3/2)/(c²r^(5/2)): the ½ is the time leg and the γ the space leg. For Gravity Probe B this is 6,604.8 mas/yr.
- **Frame dragging.** It is Ω\_LT = GJ/(2c²r³), averaged over the polar orbit, with J = Iω = 8.034 × 10³⁷ × 7.292 × 10⁻⁵ kg m²/s. That gives 40.8 mas/yr for the polar orbit, of which the component along the guide star IM Pegasi (declination 16.8°) is 40.8 × cos 16.8° = 39.1; Gravity Probe B's own prediction was 39.2.

**Table 49. Gravity's readings, each with its route.**

| Reading | Route | Predicted | Measured | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| light bending, historical and radio | time leg plus space leg | 1.7512″ | 1919 eclipse 1.98 ± 0.12″ (Dyson, Eddington and Davidson 1920); radio γ = 0.99983 ± 0.00045 (Shapiro et al. 2004) | the same as Einstein; Newton and Soldner gave half |
| a squared field | z² doubles the angle | gravitational waves at twice the orbital frequency, in two modes 45° apart, at light speed | binary pulsar decay 0.9983 ± 0.0016 of the prediction (Weisberg and Huang 2016); GW170814 favours tensor modes; GW170817's speed equals light's within 3 parts in 10¹⁵ | the same as Einstein |
| carriers on the 45° line | zero difference | photon and graviton massless | photon below 10⁻¹⁸ eV; graviton at most 2.42 × 10⁻²³ eV/c² (LIGO, Virgo and KAGRA, GWTC-3) | the same as standard |
| weight and inertia | one m on both legs | equal | MICROSCOPE: η(Ti, Pt) = (−1.5 ± 2.3 ± 1.5) × 10⁻¹⁵ (Touboul et al. 2022) | the same as standard |
| the horizon | the flip's fixed sphere | 2GM/c² (2.95 km for the Sun); photon sphere at 1.5 r\_s; Hawking temperature 6.17 × 10⁻⁸ K for one solar mass | horizons imaged (Event Horizon Telescope Collaboration 2019, 2022); no white hole seen | exact in the mathematics; no exit |
| what returns | the irreducible mass is the held part | at most 1 − 1/√2 = 29.3% of the mass, as spin energy | M87\*'s jet power of at least 10⁴² erg/s from a magnetically arrested flow (Event Horizon Telescope Collaboration 2021) | the same as Christodoulou (1970) and Blandford and Znajek (1977) |

**The black hole is the flip.** In isotropic coordinates (G = c = 1)

```latex
ds^2=-\Big(\frac{1-u}{1+u}\Big)^{2}dt^2+(1+u)^4\left(d\rho^2+\rho^2 d\Omega^2\right),\qquad u=\frac{M}{2\rho}
```

- **The inversion.** Under ρ → M²/4ρ, that is u → 1/u, the lapse is unchanged and the spatial factor maps into itself, since ρ = M/2u and dρ = −M du/2u².
- **The fixed sphere.** u = 1 is ρ = M/2, the Schwarzschild radius r = ρ(1 + u)² = 2M: the horizon, where the clock rate is zero.
- **The two copies.** The inversion maps the outside onto the inside of the fixed sphere, so the solution contains two copies joined at the horizon: Einstein and Rosen's bridge. The second copy is not an exit:
  - the bridge pinches off before anything crosses (Fuller and Wheeler 1962);
  - a black hole formed by collapse has no white-hole region;
  - none has ever been seen.
- **Inside.** The coefficient 1 − r\_s/r changes sign, so the inward direction becomes the time direction, and the centre is reached in finite proper time.
- **Kerr's ring.** For a spinning hole the singularity is a ring, and the passage through it is sealed by the inner horizon's instability, mass inflation (Poisson and Israel 1990).

In the cube the torus sits on the black-hole side, the perpendicular step from √2 moves into the held √4 dimension, and the horizon is the fixed sphere.

**What returns, and how much.**

- **The horizon.** A spinning hole has horizon radius r₊ = M + √(M² − a²), with a = J/M, and horizon area A = 4π(r₊² + a²) = 8πMr₊.
- **The irreducible mass.** M\_irr² = A/16π = M²(1 + √(1 − a²/M²))/2 (Christodoulou 1970). By Hawking's area theorem A never decreases, so neither does M\_irr.
- **How much comes back.** Only M − M\_irr, the rotational energy, can be drawn out: 1 − 1/√2 = 29.29% at maximal spin, and 15 to 25% at the spins measured for M87\* and the X-ray binaries (a/M ≈ 0.9 to 0.99).
- **The mass formula is the sum table.** M² = M\_irr² + (J/2M\_irr)² (Christodoulou and Ruffini 1971) has the held mass on one leg and the rotation on the other. At maximal spin the two squares are equal: the 45° line, with the held share at the 1/√2 level.
- **The route out is magnetic.** Field lines threading the horizon are dragged by its rotation and carry away a Poynting flux P\_BZ ≈ (κ/4πc)Φ²Ω\_H², with Ω\_H = ac/(2r₊) and κ ≈ 0.05 (Blandford and Znajek 1977; Tchekhovskoy, Narayan and McKinney 2010). It is the square of the flux through the horizon times the square of the horizon's turning rate, which is why only a spinning hole drives a jet.
- **The jet's 45°.** The jet leaves at 0.9 to 0.99c along the spin axis, on the light cone: the 45° of the (ct, r) diagram, not the jet's opening angle on the sky, which is a few degrees.

**Held pairs.** Two equal masses bound by a coupling g have reduced mass m/2 and ground-state binding mg²/4.

- **Positronium.** mₑα²/4 = 510,999 eV × (1/137.036)²/4 = 6.803 eV, with a lifetime (125 ps for para-positronium) a million times its binding period.
- **Toponium.** With the colour factor, g = (4/3)α\_s at the Bohr scale, α\_s ≈ 0.14, its binding is m\_t(4α\_s/3)²/4 ≈ 1.5 GeV, against the top's own width of 1.32 GeV: barely held. This is the threshold enhancement CMS (8.8 pb) and ATLAS (9.3 pb) see (CMS Collaboration 2025; ATLAS Collaboration 2026).

Held states driven at twice their drive frequency, ball lightning and the Higgs after inflation, are worked in section 11.3.

**Beside general relativity and its rivals.**

- **Where it sides with Einstein.** The calculator sides with Einstein against:
  - Newton's half angle of 1801;
  - Le Sage's push (1748), which would heat and slow every body;
  - Kaluza and Klein's extra circle, given no extra particles at the LHC and gravity normal down to 52 μm (section 4.7.2).
- **What it recovers.** It recovers the Einstein–Rosen bridge as its inversion, with the second sheet as the held side, Kerr's ring as the torus, and Wheeler's “charge without charge” as its torus.
- **The published alternative.** White-hole remnants (Rovelli and Vidotto 2018) are the published alternative to no exit, and none has been observed.
- **What it adds.** No number to the classical tests. Its own statements are the matter share, held mass that moves with the galaxies, and the fork at the centre (section 13).

**Standing.**

- **M.** Every number above is general relativity's or the paper's own source's.
- **N.** The two-leg reading, the black hole as the flip whose interior is held, and the “ends or turns” fork at the centre (section 13) were not found in this form.

### 11.2 Worked example: gravity's four regions

1. **Frame.** Time sits on the held axis w and radial distance on y. The full black-hole geometry has four regions in Kruskal's coordinates (Kruskal 1960): the outside (I), the inside (II), a second outside (III) and the white hole (IV).
2. **One quarter turn.** J turns the (y, w) plane, so radial distance and time trade places: (T, X) → (X, −T) carries region I to II, II to III, III to IV and IV back to I. This is the dimension inversion at a horizon, where the radial direction becomes time. The quarter turn is not a symmetry of the geometry; it carries each region to the next.
3. **Two quarter turns.** (T, X) → (−T, −X) is an exact isometry that swaps I with III and II with IV (Röken 2025). Mass stays positive in region III, so the inverse of gravity is not repulsion.
4. **Observed at every position.**
   - In region I, light's two legs are equal: γ − 1 = (2.1 ± 2.3) × 10⁻⁵ (Bertotti, Iess and Tortora 2003). The shadows of M87\* (42 ± 3 μas) and Sgr A\* (51.8 ± 2.3 μas) match Kerr black holes (Event Horizon Telescope 2019, 2022).
   - The face between I and II, the horizon, grew in area in GW250114 at 4.4σ (LIGO, Virgo and KAGRA Collaborations 2025).
   - Regions II to IV send nothing to region I, so they stay empty cells.
5. **The inverse of matter falls down.** One half turn turns the electrical variables over (C) and spacetime end for end (PT), which together are CPT. Antihydrogen falls at (0.75 ± 0.13 ± 0.16) g (Anderson et al. 2023), 1.2σ below 1 g and opposite to repulsion.

### 11.3 Held states: ball lightning and the Higgs after inflation

Both systems are held states that answer at twice the drive frequency, because each couples to the square of an oscillating field.

**The record.** Cen, Yuan and Xue (2014) filmed a ball at 3,000 frames per second from 0.902 km:

- 1.64 s of light;
- an apparent diameter of about 5 m in the steady stage, from 160 to 1,080 ms;
- light pulsing at 99.4 Hz, a period of 10.06 ms;
- a 35 kV transmission line about 20 m away.

The authors suggest the pulsing is “possibly induced by the second-harmonic effect associated with the high-voltage transmission lines”.

**Why 50 Hz gives 100 Hz light.** cos²ωt = (1 + cos 2ωt)/2. Anything that answers the square of a 50 Hz field pulses at 100 Hz and peaks at twice its average, the single-phase power ratio, √4. The observed 99.4 Hz means a drive at 49.7 Hz, close to the 50 Hz of China's grid.

**How fast the glow must respond.** A glow with response time τ shows the 2f pulsing with depth m = 1/√(1 + (2ωτ)²). The paper gives no depth, so Table 50 gives the response each depth would require.

**Table 50. Response time needed for each visible depth, for a 50 Hz drive.**

| Visible pulsing depth | Glow must respond within |
| --- | --- |
| 90% | 0.77 ms |
| 50% | 2.76 ms |
| 30% | 5.06 ms |
| 10% | 15.84 ms |

**Can the line feed it?** The field from one 35 kV conductor 10 m up was computed with its image in the ground, taking a conductor radius of 1 cm and a phase voltage of 35/√3 kV rms. At 20 m sideways it is 133 to 150 V/m peak for a ball 2 to 10 m up. A single conductor overstates the field, since three phases partly cancel.

- **The electric bound.** A conducting sphere of volume V in a field E₀ e^{iωt} has interior field 3E₀/(3 − is), with s = σ/ωε₀. It absorbs P = ½σ|E\_in|²V = (9/2)ωε₀E₀²V·s/(9 + s²). This is greatest at s = 3:

  ```latex
  P_{\max}=\tfrac{3}{4}\,\omega\varepsilon_0E_0^{2}V\quad\text{at}\quad\sigma=3\,\omega\varepsilon_0
  ```
- **The magnetic bound.** It uses the exact eddy-current response of a conducting sphere (Landau and Lifshitz, Electrodynamics of Continuous Media, §59). The imaginary part of its polarisability peaks at 0.533 when the radius is 2.41 skin depths. The line current is not published.

**Table 51. The most power the line can deliver to the ball, 5 m up, maximised over the ball's conductivity.**

| Source | 25 cm core | whole 5 m glow |
| --- | --- | --- |
| the electric field, 133 to 150 V/m | 3.0 to 3.8 × 10⁻⁷ W | 2.4 to 3.1 × 10⁻³ W |
| the magnetic field, 100 A peak in the line | 5.4 × 10⁻⁷ W | 4.3 × 10⁻³ W |
| the magnetic field, 1,000 A peak in the line | 5.4 × 10⁻⁵ W | 0.43 W |

- **Under a watt.** At 1,000 A rms, the magnetic bound in the last row of Table 51 doubles to 0.87 W for the whole glow, still under a watt. A 35 kV line rarely carries that much.
- **Against a bulb.** A 100 W household bulb draws about 34,000 times the electric-field bound for the whole glow.
- **The conclusion.** The line modulates the ball but cannot feed it. The energy was stored in the ball, consistent with the soil elements in its spectrum (Si, Fe, Ca).
- **Kapitsa's idea.** Kapitsa's feeding by microwaves from the storm (1955) is a different source, untested here.

**The Higgs after inflation: the same 2f drive.** After inflation the inflaton oscillates, φ = Φ cos mt. With ρ = ½φ̇² + ½m²φ² and p = ½φ̇² − ½m²φ², the curvature is:

```latex
R=\frac{\rho-3p}{M^{2}}=\frac{m^{2}\Phi^{2}}{M^{2}}\left(\tfrac12+\tfrac32\cos 2mt\right)
```

Here M is the reduced Planck mass. So R pulses at twice the inflaton frequency and dips negative for part of each cycle, as E² does in the ball. A Higgs coupled as ξRh² obeys the Mathieu equation:

```latex
h''+\left(k^{2}+\xi R\right)h=0\;\longrightarrow\;y''+\left(A-2q\cos 2z\right)y=0,\qquad z=mt,\quad A=\frac{k^{2}}{m^{2}}+\frac{X}{2},\quad q=-\frac34X,\quad X=\frac{\xi\Phi^{2}}{M^{2}}
```

At k = 0 the lowest mass squared in each cycle is −X: negative every cycle.

**Table 52. Floquet growth rates, from the monodromy matrix over one period.**

| X = ξΦ²/M² | Growth at k = 0 | Fastest growth over k |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0.1 | 0 (held) | 0.04 |
| 0.3 | 0 (held) | 0.11 |
| 0.5 | 0 (held) | 0.19 |
| 1.0 | 0.33 | 0.37 |
| 1.33 | 0.48 | 0.48 |
| 2 | 0.62 | 0.62 |
| 5 | 0 (held) | 0.57 |
| 10 | 1.16 | 1.16 |
| 20 | 1.55 | 1.55 |

- **The drive holds it.** In Table 52, the uniform mode stays bounded for X < 0.77, and again for 4.85 < X < 5.42, even though its mass squared goes negative every cycle. This is Kapitza's inverted pendulum, stable when a²ω² > 2gl (Kapitza 1951).
- **Slow growth elsewhere.** At other k only slow, narrow growth survives, which the expansion tends to wash out.
- **Broad resonance.** It needs |q| ≳ 1, so ξ ≳ (4/3)(M/Φ)²: 1.33 for Φ = M and 0.67 for Φ = √2·M. This matches strong Higgs production for ξ ≳ 1 (Herranen, Markkanen, Nurmi and Rajantie 2015).

**The window for ξ.**

- **The bounds.** Actual vacuum decay needs the growth to reach the barrier. The lattice study of Figueroa, Rajantie and Torrenti (2018) finds instability for ξ ≳ 4 to 5 at m\_t ≈ 173.3 GeV, and for ξ ≳ 11 to 12 at m\_t ≈ 172.1 GeV.
- **The lower end.** ξ ≳ 0.06 is needed during inflation (Herranen, Markkanen, Nurmi and Rajantie 2014).
- **The interpolation.** Linear interpolation to 172.60 GeV gives ξ ≲ 8.6, an estimate made here and not a published number. So the window is roughly 0.06 ≲ ξ ≲ 9.
- **Under the balance rule.** The vacuum is metastable, so the deeper vacuum exists and the upper bound stands. Table 53 sets the two held states side by side.

**Table 53. The two held states side by side.**

| coupling | the glow goes as E² | ξRh², with R quadratic in φ |
| response | 99.4 Hz, 2f | curvature at 2m, 2f |
| held? | yes, by stored energy; the line only modulates it, well under 1 W | yes, for X < 0.77 at k = 0 |
| released? | faded after 1.64 s | broad resonance for ξ ≳ 1; decay for ξ ≳ 4 to 12, depending on m\_t |

General relativity computes every classical number on this layer, and the calculator gives the same ones; the alternatives that history tried, Newton's half angle, Le Sage's push and Kaluza–Klein's extra circle, fail the same tests for both.
