## 12. The strong scale: the proton's thirds

The calculator reads the proton as three phases on three legs, so its spin, ½ = ½ΔΣ + ΔG + L, divides into equal sixths: the quark spin share ΔΣ = ⅓, the gluon spin ΔG = ⅙ and the orbital share L = ⅙. Against the measurements and the continuum lattice, at one resolution (2 GeV), the three pieces sit at −0.1σ (ΔΣ against HERMES), −0.2σ and −0.4σ. The proton's radius computes as four reduced Compton wavelengths, 0.841236 fm against 0.84075 ± 0.00064 fm. At this scale the per-cycle load is no longer slight, so the static values the cube gives, the quark model's, are loaded by amounts that only QCD computes.

### 12.1 The proton's thirds

**Thirds.** The proton's spin is ½ = ½ΔΣ + ΔG + L. Read on three legs, each is ⅙, so the quark spin share is ΔΣ = ⅓:

- **quark spin.** HERMES measured ΔΣ = 0.330 ± 0.039 (via Liu and Ma 2015), and the lattice gives ½ΔΣ = 0.190 ± 0.022 (Alexandrou et al. 2026);
- **gluon spin.** The lattice gives ΔG = 0.231 ± 0.047 at 10 GeV² (Zhao et al. 2025), or 0.158 ± 0.039 at 2 GeV, the scale the behavior rule fixes (section 18.1);
- **orbital motion.** Folded to one resolution, L = 0.152 ± 0.043 (section 12.2).

**Beyond the spin legs.**

- **The gluon momentum fraction.** The 2026 continuum lattice decomposition gives ⟨x⟩\_g = 0.372 ± 0.035 at 2 GeV, a third within 1.1σ (Alexandrou et al. 2026).
- **Mass is the difference.** E² = (pc)² + (mc²)² is the sum, and the rest mass comes back from the difference: 938.2721 MeV for the proton. 99% of it is field energy. The quark masses add to about 9.0 MeV at 2 GeV, 1% of the total; the rest is the energy of the colour field and the quarks' motion, which QCD's trace anomaly assigns mostly to the gluon field.

**The radius.** rₚ = 4ƛₚ = 0.841236 fm, against 0.84075 ± 0.00064 fm (CODATA 2022), so rₚ/ƛₚ = 3.9977 ± 0.0030, 0.8σ off.

**The radius by method.** Against 4ƛₚ = 0.84124 fm:

- muonic hydrogen gives 0.84087 ± 0.00039 fm (−0.9σ);
- the 2026 hydrogen 2S–6P measurement gives 0.8406 ± 0.0015 fm (−0.4σ; Maisenbacher et al. 2026);
- the electron–hydrogen average gives 0.8439 ± 0.0013 fm (+2.0σ), with χ²/dof 2.5 across its inputs.

The chance of an alphabet member this close is 1 in 6 (section 17.6), and the relation is prior art (Standing, below).

**The statics, loaded.**

- **The moment ratio.** SU(6) gives μₙ/μₚ = −⅔, and the measured ratio is 2.75% larger in size.
- **The axial coupling.** SU(6) gives g\_A = 5/3, and the measured 1.27641 ± 0.00056 (Märkisch et al. 2019) is 23.4% smaller.

**The SU(6) statics.** In the quark model the proton's spin-flavour state is symmetric, and the magnetic moment sums the quarks' moments weighted by their spin projections: μₚ = (4μᵤ − μ\_d)/3 and μₙ = (4μ\_d − μᵤ)/3. With equal masses the moments scale with charge, μ\_d = −μᵤ/2, so μₚ = (3/2)μᵤ and μₙ = −μᵤ, and μₙ/μₚ = −⅔ exactly. The same state gives the axial coupling as the expectation of Σσ\_zτ\_z, which is 5/3.

**Why the loads are not slight here.**

- **The size of the load.** At the proton's scale α\_s is about 0.5, so α\_s/2π ≈ 0.08 before any count, and the counts are of order one to ten. The series does not converge there, which is the statement that the strong force confines.
- **What the loads are.** The loads on the statics, −23.4% on g\_A and +2.75% on |μₙ/μₚ|, are what the standard account calls the quarks' relativistic motion and the pion cloud. Lattice QCD gets g\_A = 1.27 ± 0.02 from first principles.
- **Their signs.** The loads run both ways, so the calculator predicts neither direction, only where the loads begin.

**The hadrons are outside the rule's domain.** A hadron's mass is 99% field energy and 1% Yukawa, so it is not a leg of the kind the balance rule uses. Near-degenerate triples give Q → ⅓ whatever the physics: the proton, Λ and Ξ give 0.335.

**What decides.** The Electron-Ion Collider measures ΔG to about 10% and ΔΣ to about 15% (Aschenauer, Sassot and Stratmann 2015), and the lattice's separate quark and gluon orbital pieces decide the third leg.

**Standing.**

- **M.** The statics are SU(6)'s. rₚ = 4ƛₚ is prior art, stated explicitly by Haramein and co-authors (Haramein et al. 2025, from Haramein 2013) and equivalent to Trinhammer and Bohr's πrₚ = 2λₚ (2019).
- **N.** Equal thirds of the spin, with the gluon share ⅙, is the calculator's own commitment.

### 12.2 Worked example: the proton as folded reactions

The author: “I suspect proton may be folded reactions.”

**Unfolded, the proton misses.**

- One reaction going round 2π(4ƛₚ) at c gives 4 μ\_N, 43.2% above the measured 2.79284734463(82) μ\_N (Mohr et al. 2024).
- One Dirac reaction on its own Compton circle gives 1 μ\_N, 64.2% below.

**Folded, it narrows.** The neutron shows the folds: it has a moment of −1.91304276(45) μ\_N and no charge, and a reaction with no charge carries no current, i = qω = 0. Fold the proton into three g = 2 reactions with charges ⅔, ⅔ and −⅓ in the quark model's symmetric spin-flavour state, each a third of the proton's mass:

- μₚ = (4μᵤ − μ\_d)/3 = 3 μ\_N, 7.42% high;
- μₙ = (4μ\_d − μᵤ)/3 = −2 μ\_N, 4.55% high in size;
- their ratio is −⅔, against the measured −0.68497934(16), which is 2.75% larger in size.

The ring's unfolded 4 μ\_N, 43% high, narrows to 7%. The rest needs the masses the folded reactions carry: 335.955 MeV from μₚ and 326.974 MeV from μₙ, against mₚ/3 = 312.757 MeV. No corner gives them. Under the behavior rule both readings are out: the moment is measured to 3 parts in 10¹⁰, so 7% is a miss of about 10⁸σ. The radius stays as a number, with no current read into its circle (section 15.3).

**The spin's legs, folded to one resolution.** The proton's spin is ½ = ½ΔΣ + ΔG + L: quark spin, gluon spin and orbital motion. The calculator puts each leg at ⅙ (section 12.1).

- **The problem.** The lattice gluon spin, ΔG = 0.231 ± 0.047, is quoted at μ² = 10 GeV² (Zhao et al. 2025), and the lattice quark spin, ½ΔΣ = 0.190 ± 0.022, at μ = 2 GeV (Alexandrou et al. 2026). Their remainder, L = ½ − ½ΔΣ − ΔG = 0.079 ± 0.052, mixes two resolutions.
- **The evolution.** At leading order ΔΣ keeps its value while α\_s(ΔG + 4ΔΣ/β₀) is invariant, so ΔG grows as the resolution rises and the orbital piece falls to match (Ji, Tang and Hoodbhoy 1996).
- **The coupling.** α\_s is 0.2476 at 10 GeV² and 0.3004 at 4 GeV², run at four loops from α\_s(m\_Z) = 0.1179 (d'Enterria et al. 2022).
- **The result at 2 GeV.** ΔG folds to 0.158 ± 0.039 (−0.2σ from ⅙), L to 0.152 ± 0.043 (−0.4σ), and ½ΔΣ stays 0.190 ± 0.022 (+1.1σ).

That is weaker than three checks. Some resolution always has ΔG = ⅙. 2 GeV is the lattice's convention, which the behavior rule keeps because its consequences elsewhere match (section 18.1), not a scale the calculator's structure names; the quark leg is the one that holds at every resolution. Folding removes only the −1.7σ that mixing two resolutions produced. The totals hold: at 2 GeV the momentum fractions sum to 0.995 ± 0.067 and the spin pieces to 0.507 ± 0.078 (Alexandrou et al. 2026).

**Standing.**

- **M.** The ratio −⅔ is SU(6) (Bég, Lee and Pais 1964, within 3% of experiment, as described by Greenberg 2008). The evolution is standard.
- **N.** Reading the proton as folded reactions, and folding the legs to one resolution before comparing them with ⅙, are the author's.

### 12.3 Beside the standard theories

On this layer the standard theory computes rather than takes inputs: lattice QCD gives the spin pieces, the momentum fractions, the radius and the axial coupling from first principles, with errors of several percent. So the comparison is three-way, the calculator's fixed fractions against the lattice's computed values against the measurements.

**Origination.** **M**: the statics are SU(6)'s; rₚ = 4ƛₚ is prior art (Haramein et al. 2025, from Haramein 2013; equivalent to Trinhammer and Bohr 2019). **N**: equal thirds of the spin, with the gluon share ⅙ and the orbital share ⅙, read at one resolution; the proton as three folded reactions.

## 13. The cosmos: the hum, the held store and the share

On the calculator the cosmos carries three numbers and one commitment. The vacuum's residual energy, the hum, is v²/M₂₃ = 2.02 ± 0.20 meV, against a measured fourth root of the dark-energy density of 2.24 meV (+1.1σ). A store that keeps its density while space grows has w = −1 exactly. The unit 4-ball fills π²/32 = 0.3084 of the side-2 tesseract, read as the matter share: −1.2σ against the supernovae with the microwave background and the baryon acoustic oscillations, +0.9σ against Planck and −1.3σ against DESI's oscillations alone, with the acceleration today, q₀ = −0.537, following from it. The galaxies' extra pull is read as held mass. At a black hole's centre the geometry either ends or turns, and the sign of the curvature and the primordial gravitational waves decide which. ΛCDM takes the share and Λ as inputs and assumes w = −1, and quantum field theory's own estimate of the vacuum energy misses by about 10¹²⁰. The data that prefer a drifting w press on ΛCDM and the calculator alike.

Four things are called held in this paper, and they are four:

- the held store, the dark energy, whose density is fixed;
- held mass, what gravitates without shining;
- the held plasma of ball lightning (section 11.3);
- the held dimension √4 on which all three are read.

### 13.1 The hum

In Bass's picture the cosmological constant is a residual of size v²/M for the scale M where the emergent symmetries dissolve. The calculator puts M at the weak = strong crossing, M₂₃ = 3.0 × 10¹⁶ GeV:

```latex
\mu_{\rm hum}=\frac{v^{2}}{M_{23}}=\frac{(246.22\ \mathrm{GeV})^{2}}{3.0\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{GeV}}=2.02\pm0.20\ \mathrm{meV}
```

The measured fourth root of the dark-energy density is 2.24 meV (Planck Collaboration 2020), +1.1σ.

**The hum's arithmetic.** (246.22 GeV)² = 60,624 GeV², divided by M₂₃ = 3.006 × 10¹⁶ GeV, gives 2.017 × 10⁻¹² GeV. The ±0.20 meV is the crossing's dependence on α\_s. At α\_s = 0.1171, 1.0σ below the PDG value, the crossing falls at the 2.7 × 10¹⁶ GeV that the measured value needs, and the hum matches it exactly (section 5.4).

- **The measured value.** ρ\_Λ = Ω\_Λ · 3H₀²/(8πG), with H₀ = 67.4 km/s/Mpc and Ω\_Λ = 0.685, is 5.25 × 10⁻¹⁰ J/m³ = 2.52 × 10⁻⁴⁷ GeV⁴, whose fourth root is 2.24 meV.
- **The overshoot.** The Higgs vacuum's own depth, λv⁴/4 = m\_H²v²/8 = 1.19 × 10⁸ GeV⁴, overshoots (2.24 meV)⁴ by 10⁵⁴·⁷: the cosmological-constant problem in the Higgs sector alone.
- **What each part is.**
  - The cancellation is the symmetry, exact for the same reason the fundamentals cancel in a balanced neutral whatever the voltage.
  - The residual is the leading operator that breaks it.
  - Its coefficient is of order one.
  - The one choice in the number is the crossing M₂₃, which the other two crossings would replace with 5.5 eV or 0.36 eV.

### 13.2 The held store and w = −1

The first law for an expanding volume is ρ̇ = −3H(ρ + p). A store that keeps its density while space grows has ρ̇ = 0, so p = −ρ and w = −1. Its gravitational source, ρ + 3p = −2ρ, is negative, so it pushes, and it gains energy with the volume, dE = ρ\_Λ dV.

**The held store and the expansion.**

- **The excess.** A model-independent reconstruction of the expansion rate from DESI DR2 with Pantheon+ and DES-SN5YR finds E(z) 3 to 4% above the Planck ΛCDM curve at z = 0.35 to 0.55, at 5σ and more, and 7.2σ above the calculator's own curve at z = 0.349: 1.247 ± 0.006 against 1.204, where Planck's ΛCDM curve gives 1.208, 6.5σ (Mukherjee and Sen 2025).
- **Where it sits.** If it stands, the excess sits in the window between the two holds, z = 0.31 and 0.65, exactly where the handover happens.
- **What it rests on.** It rests on Planck's sound horizon and on the DES-SN5YR sample, whose low-redshift calibration is disputed (Efstathiou 2025; Vincenzi et al. 2025).
- **No oscillation.** An expansion that itself oscillated would print a periodic residual on the supernova Hubble diagram and on the BAO distances, and none is found, to the few-percent level. The one oscillation the universe carries is the baryon acoustic oscillation, a sound wave frozen at recombination at a comoving 147 Mpc.

### 13.3 The share and the two holds

The unit 4-ball fills π²/32 = 0.3084 of the side-2 tesseract.

- **The split.** The calculator reads 0.3084 as the matter share and 1 − π²/32 = 0.6916 as dark energy.
- **The two holds.** Matter thins as a⁻³ while the store does not, so with the calculator's share matter and store were equal at z = 0.31 and acceleration began at z = 0.65 (0.30 and 0.63 on Planck's split). At z = 1 matter was 3.6 times the store.
- **The acceleration today.** q₀ = 3Ωₘ/2 − 1 = −0.537.

**The share and time.** In ΛCDM the split moves, because matter dilutes as a⁻³ while the store does not. The near-equality of the two today is the coincidence problem. A geometric split that held at all times would remove it, but it would need the two densities to scale together, which the calculator has not shown, so the share is stated for today (section 20). The split that can hold at all times is inside the matter sector, where both parts dilute together: dark to baryonic matter, 16/3 (below). Table 56 sets the curvature, and the shares the supernovae give on their own, against the calculator.

### 13.4 Held mass: the pull from within

The galaxies' extra pull is read as held mass. It fits four observations:

- no particle has been found in decades of searches: LZ's σ\_SI < 2.2 × 10⁻⁴⁸ cm² at 40 GeV from 4.2 tonne-years (LZ Collaboration 2025);
- the extra pull is fixed by the visible matter at every radius in 153 galaxies (McGaugh, Lelli and Schombert 2016);
- M87's inflow ring joins its jet (Lu et al. 2023);
- the Bullet Cluster's pull stays with the galaxies rather than the gas (Clowe et al. 2006).

The microwave background's third peak asks only that the held mass be cold, clustering and photon-blind, which it is (Planck Collaboration 2020). What it is made of stays open, and the data fence it:

- **Black holes.** If it is black holes, they can be all of it only between 10¹⁷ and 10²³ g, formed when the plasma was between 30 TeV and 3 × 10⁷ GeV, where the calculator has no scale.
- **The Higgs transition.** The calculator's own event, the Higgs transition at 160 GeV, makes Earth-mass holes (horizon mass 10⁻⁵ M☉).
- **Microlensing.** Twenty years of OGLE microlensing cap compact objects from 10⁻⁴ to 10³ M☉ at about a percent of the dark matter (Mróz et al. 2024).

The reading also inherits what cold mass owes: the tightness of the radial-acceleration relation and the core–cusp problem.

### 13.5 The foundation, and ends or turns

**The foundation.**

- **No exit.** With no exit, the mass that falls into black holes is held. Inside, the inward direction becomes time; from outside, everything that fell in is frozen at the horizon, a graveyard that still gravitates.
- **The area law.** GW250114, the loudest merger yet recorded, at signal-to-noise 80, confirmed that the horizon area never decreases and resolved two ringdown tones consistent with a Kerr hole (LIGO, Virgo and KAGRA Collaborations 2025).
- **The feed.** It is the held store itself, ρ\_Λ dV, not the black holes.

**Ends or turns.** Two facts frame the question.

- **The Hubble sphere.** In a flat universe the Hubble sphere sits exactly on its own horizon: ρ = 3H²/8πG makes the mass inside c/H equal to c³/2GH, whose Schwarzschild radius is c/H, at every epoch, if and only if Ω\_k = 0 (Pathria 1972). Today that is 9.2 × 10⁵² kg inside 1.37 × 10²⁶ m.
- **The inside of a hole.** In general relativity it is already a universe:
  - the interior of a collapsing dust star is a closed Friedmann universe run backwards (Oppenheimer and Snyder 1939);
  - it reaches the centre in at most πGM/c³ of proper time: 40 μs for a 2.6 M☉ hole, 67 s for Sgr A\* and 28 hours for M87\*;
  - its volume grows as 3√3π M²t (Christodoulou and Rovelli 2015), so a solar-mass hole a billion years old holds 2 × 10⁵ solar volumes behind a 3 km horizon.

The turned version has a literature of fifty years, each entry with a test:

- Frolov, Markov and Mukhanov (1989, 1990);
- Smolin's selection for the number of holes (1992), which needs no neutron star much above 2 M☉, against 2.08 ± 0.07 and 2.35 ± 0.17 M☉ measured (Fonseca et al. 2021; Romani et al. 2022);
- Popławski's torsion bounce (2010), with a preferred axis and a handedness;
- the Planck-star bounce (Rovelli and Vidotto 2014; Haggard and Rovelli 2015), black to white in a time ∝ M² rather than Hawking's M³;
- white-hole remnants as the dark matter (Rovelli and Vidotto 2018);
- Gaztañaga's bounce on the exclusion principle (Gaztañaga et al. 2025).

If the centre turns:

- the universe is closed and never open, Ω\_k ≤ 0, with |Ω\_k| < 0.0045 putting the parent above 1.4 × 10⁵⁴ kg;
- the entropy of the start is paid by a stiff contraction before the turn (Khoury, Ovrut, Steinhardt and Turok 2001), which leaves no primordial gravitational waves, r ≈ 0. Inflation's simplest potentials give r ≈ 0.003 to 0.01. BICEP/Keck has r < 0.036 at 95% (BICEP/Keck Collaboration 2021). LiteBIRD, planned for launch in Japan's fiscal year 2032, aims for δr = 0.001 (Ghigna et al. 2024); CMB-S4, designed for the same reach, lost its U.S. support on 9 July 2025 (CMB-S4 Project 2025).

**No signal yet bears on the fork.**

- **Echoes.** Searches for gravitational-wave echoes in GW150914 and GW190521 bound them below 0.15 to 0.21 of the main signal (Miani et al. 2023).
- **Fast radio bursts.** White-hole bursts cannot be their main source, whose rate is far above what returning holes could supply (Ewasiuk and Profumo 2026).

The fork's test is Ω\_k and r. The ended version keeps the balance line at zero and owes nothing further. Penrose's objection (1979), that a hole's interior is gravity's state of highest entropy while a daughter universe needs a low-entropy start, stands against every turned version until the stiff phase is derived rather than assumed.

### 13.6 Beside the standard theories

On this layer the standard theories take most of their numbers as inputs: Λ, the matter share, the dark-to-baryonic ratio and H₀ are all fitted in ΛCDM. The one standard prediction for the vacuum's energy, quantum field theory's sum of zero-point energies, fails by about 10¹²⁰. Table 58 sets the two side by side.

**What neither computes.**

- **H₀.** The Hubble constant is a number of neither. The values are 73.17 ± 0.86 km/s/Mpc from the Cepheid ladder (Breuval et al. 2024), 70.4 ± 1.9 from the JWST tip-of-the-red-giant-branch and carbon-star ladders (Freedman et al. 2025), and 67.19 ± 0.38 from the three CMB experiments combined (Camphuis et al. 2025).
- **S₈.** The combined CMB value from Planck, ACT DR6 and SPT-3G, S₈ = 0.836 +0.012/−0.013 (DES Collaboration 2026, via Pantos and Perivolaropoulos 2026), and the KiDS-Legacy lensing value, 0.815 +0.016/−0.021 (Wright et al. 2025), now agree within 1σ.
- **N\_eff.** ACT DR6 finds 2.86 ± 0.13 relativistic species, three within 1.4σ (ACT Collaboration 2025).

**The score on this layer.**

- **Inputs against numbers.** ΛCDM fits the share and Λ; the calculator gives both as numbers, the hum at +1.1σ with the crossing chosen among three, and the share at −1.2σ, weak because cube numbers crowd near 0.31.
- **The standard failure.** Quantum field theory's vacuum energy misses by about 10¹²⁰, a failure scored as one (section 2.5).
- **Shared pressure.** The drift in w, at 2.8 to 4.2σ, and the expansion excess at z ≈ 0.35, at 6.5σ and 7.2σ, press on ΛCDM and the calculator alike, and both pressures rest on DES-SN5YR's disputed calibration and on Planck's sound horizon.
- **What separates them.** The sign of Ω\_k and r: the turned version predicts Ω\_k ≤ 0 and r ≈ 0, and the simplest inflation r ≈ 0.003 to 0.01. Euclid and the final DESI data measure the share and Ω\_k to about ±0.003, and LiteBIRD r to ±0.001 (section 18).

**Origination.**

- **M.** w = −1 is ΛCDM's assumption, and the continuity equation is standard. The cancellation, with its residual v²/M, is Bass's (Bass 2015 to 2026). The Hubble sphere on its own horizon is Pathria's (1972), the interior as a closed Friedmann universe Oppenheimer and Snyder's (1939), and each turned version is its authors' (section 13.5).
- **N.** w = −1 derived from a held store; the hum at the weak = strong crossing; π²/32 as the matter share; the galaxies' extra pull as held mass, with no dark-matter particle (the author's); the author's picture of the macro scale.
- **D.** w = −1 for all time diverges from the evolving dark energy that DESI's data with supernovae prefer at 2.8 to 4.2σ, and at 3.2σ after DES's recalibration (DESI Collaboration 2025; Popovic et al. 2025).

## 14. Atoms: the tables at human scale

At the scale of atoms the calculator's two tables return as a spectrum. Hydrogen's lines are the inverse difference table, 1/λ = R(n₂² − n₁²)/(n₁²n₂²): Lyman-α is the cell (2, 1)/4 at 121.57 nm, and H-α the cell (3, 2)/36 at 656.47 nm. A subshell holds a gnomon of orbitals, 2ℓ + 1 = (ℓ + 1)² − ℓ², and the shells hold 2n² electrons, the diagonal sum cells (n, n). The cube's 27 points are the sites of crystal chemistry, and every element from hydrogen to oganesson sits in the sum table, in the difference table or one leg beyond them. Each number on this layer is the standard theories': Rydberg's and Bohr's, Fock's, quantum electrodynamics' and crystal-field theory's. What the calculator owns here is the single figure that holds them all, and the check that the figure predicts no chemistry (section 14.5).

### 14.1 Hydrogen's lines: the inverse difference table

The lines are the inverse difference table scaled by Rydberg's constant for hydrogen. Table 59 reads the first five as cells, in vacuum wavelengths.

**Table 59. The hydrogen lines as the inverse difference table.** 1/λ = R(n₂² − n₁²)/(n₁²n₂²), with R\_H = 1.0967758 × 10⁷ m⁻¹.

| Line | Cell | Wavelength |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lyman-α | (2, 1)/4 | 121.57 nm |
| H-α | (3, 2)/36 | 656.47 nm |
| H-β | (4, 2)/64 | 486.27 nm |
| H-γ | (5, 2)/100 | 434.17 nm |
| H-δ | (6, 2)/144 | 410.29 nm |

The corrections to these cells, the fine structure, the hyperfine line and the Lamb shift, are further powers of the per-cycle coupling α, and quantum electrodynamics computes them (section 5.3).

### 14.2 The shells, and the atom as the square of the oscillator

**The shells.**

- **Gnomons stacked.** A subshell with angular momentum ℓ holds 2ℓ + 1 = (ℓ + 1)² − ℓ² orbitals, a gnomon. Stacking the gnomons for ℓ = 0 to n − 1 gives n² orbitals, and two spins make 2n² electrons.
- **The filling order.** The periodic table's rows run 2, 8, 8, 18, 18, 32, 32 because the subshells fill in the Madelung order, which the squares alone do not say.
- **By dimension.** The shell sizes are 1, 3, 5, 7 in two dimensions, 1, 4, 9, 16 in three and 1, 5, 14, 30 in four, each column the running sum of the one before. Three-dimensional hydrogen is a four-dimensional sphere (Fock 1935).
- **Parity.** (−1)^ℓ is inside-out: s and d are even, p and f odd.

**The atom is the square of the oscillator.**

- **In three dimensions.** The Coulomb problem is the four-dimensional isotropic oscillator projected by the Hopf map, whose fibres are circles each linked once with every other (Kustaanheimo and Stiefel 1965). Hydrogen's orbits upstairs are those Hopf circles, the same linked circles as the held torus, and the direction lost in the projection is a U(1) phase, the electron's charge coupling.
- **In two dimensions.** The map is Levi-Civita's w = z² (1920): the planar Kepler problem is the square of the planar oscillator. The 1/r and r² potentials are each other's conformal squares, the Newton–Hooke duality in which potentials rᵃ and rᵇ pair when (a + 2)(b + 2) = 4 (Arnold 1990).

The atom is the square of the oscillator, with one circle's worth of phase folded away.

### 14.3 Crystals and the 27 points

**Crystal fields.** For point charges, the splitting of the five d orbitals in a tetrahedral arrangement is −4/9 of the octahedral one, and in a cubic arrangement −8/9. These follow by direct computation of the field at the ion from charges on the cube's face points, its alternate corners and its corners. The octahedral ligands sit on the axes and the tetrahedral and cubic ones on the diagonals, so the orbital that points along the axes is raised in one case and lowered in the other. That is why cobalt chloride is pink with six waters and blue with four chlorides. The d orbitals x² − y² and xy are the difference table and the third table of one complex square.

**Crystals and images.**

- **Neighbours.** Face-centred cubic neighbours sit across a face diagonal at a√2/2 (Cu 2.556 Å, Al 2.863 Å) and body-centred ones across the body diagonal at a√3/2 (Fe 2.482 Å, W 2.741 Å).
- **The flip.** The reciprocal lattice of FCC is BCC and of BCC is FCC, the flip exchanging the two. The method of images places a charge's mirror at R²/d from the centre of a grounded sphere of radius R: the inversion again.
- **Maxwell under inversion.** Maxwell's equations keep their form under inversion through a sphere (Bateman 1910; Cunningham 1910), and mass breaks that symmetry.

Table 60 lays everything that sits on the cube's 27 points over one another.

**Table 60. The 27 points as one overlay.**

| Points | Distance | What sits there |
| --- | --- | --- |
| the centre | 0 | the atom's site; three-phase zero; the T-bar's spin point; the hinge of the flip |
| 6 face points | 1 | the octahedron's corners; octahedral ligands; p lobes; the directions of ± phase; the points the flip fixes |
| 12 edge midpoints | √2 | FCC neighbours; t₂g directions; the three-phase hexagon (side = radius = √2) |
| 8 corners | √3 | the delimiters; the drive's switch states; BCC neighbours; alternate corners at the tetrahedral angle 109.47°, carbon's bond angle |
| the body diagonal | — | the Clarke zero axis; the cipher's ’–× axis |

The governing symmetry is the cube's full group O\_h: 48 operations, the 24 rotations doubled by inside-out.

### 14.4 Everyday matches

The same cells and rungs turn up in ordinary measurements:

- **Orbits.** Escape over circular speed is √2 for the Earth, the Moon and the Sun, and 12 km/s from the Earth leaves at 4.344 km/s.
- **Relativity.** A muon with γ = 10 sits at the cell (10, 1), √99, and its lifetime stretches from 2.197 to 21.97 μs.
- **Lamps and the neutral.** Lamps flicker at 2f. With third harmonics the neutral current can exceed the phase current, because the three third harmonics add in the neutral (section 3.7.1). For short current pulses that do not overlap between phases, as computer power supplies draw, the neutral carries √3 times the phase current: the √3 rung.

### 14.5 Every element placed

Section 3.5 gives the rules and the tally; this subsection gives:

- the corrections to her two element pages (Table 61);
- why the noble gases split as they do;
- where the tables stop fitting the atom: the filling order and the superheavy elements;
- the ledger of all 118 elements (Table 105).

**Her two pages.**

- *Root Elements* tags each whole-number cell of Tables 3 and 4 with the element of that atomic number.
- *Calc Elements* gives each element from Z = 1 to 54:
  - a tier (1D baseline, 2D sum, 2D difference, 3D nested or 4D);
  - a coordinate form;
  - a descriptor taken from chemistry.

**Table 61. Corrections to the element pages.** Checked in appA.py.

| Page and entry | Z | As written | Correct |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Root Elements*, sum cell (3, 1) | 10 | He 10 | Ne 10: 3² + 1² = 10 is neon |
| *Root Elements*, sum cell (9, 8) | 145 | √146 (Elem 146) | √145, since 81 + 64 = 145 |
| *Calc Elements*, V | 23 | 4D | within legs of 10 or less, four squares, as (3, 3, 2, 1); with any legs, 12² − 11², a difference |
| *Calc Elements*, Co | 27 | 4D, (3, 3, 3, 0) | 6² − 3², on the page; the vector (3, 3, 3) is three-dimensional |
| *Calc Elements*, Ga | 31 | 3D, (3, 3, 3, 1) “= 31” | 9 + 9 + 9 + 1 = 28. Within the cap 31 needs four squares, as (5, 2, 1, 1); with any legs, 16² − 15² |
| *Calc Elements*, Y | 39 | 4D, (5, 3, 2, 1) | 8² − 5², on the page |
| *Calc Elements*, Tc | 43 | 4D, (5, 3, 3, 0) | 25 + 9 + 9 = 43, three squares within the cap; with any legs, 22² − 21² |
| *Calc Elements*, Ru | 44 | unfinished | 6² + 2² + 2² within the cap; with any legs, 12² − 10² |
| *Calc Elements*, Ag | 47 | (5, 4, 3, 1) “= 47” | 25 + 16 + 9 + 1 = 51. Within the cap, (6, 3, 1, 1); with any legs, 24² − 23² |
| *Calc Elements*, In | 49 | 2D degenerate, (7, 0) | 7², a square; also 25² − 24² |
| *Calc Elements*, Be | 4 | 3D degenerate, (2, 0, 0) | 2², a square |
| *Calc Elements*, unmarked duals | 5, 8, 13, 17, 25, 29, 37, 41, 52, 53 | one tier each | each is both a sum and a difference of two squares |

What the corrections show:

- **The 3D rows are right as written.** Her rows labelled 3D (C 6, Si 14, Ti 22, Zn 30, Sr 38, Mo 42, Pd 46 and Xe 54) are exactly the elements up to 54 that need three squares (section 3.5).
- **The 4D rows come from the leg cap.** Every 4D row is either a slip or an effect of capping the legs at 10, never of the numbers themselves:
  - V 23, Ga 31 and Ag 47 are of the form 8m + 7, so within the cap they need four squares;
  - with any legs, each is a difference.

**The elements now being sought sit one leg off the page.** 119 = 12² − 5² and 120 = 11² − 1², both differences only. The next closed shell is a difference either way:

- 168 by the Madelung order: 13² − 1²;
- about 172 in Pyykkö's relativistic table (Pyykkö 2011): 44² − 42².

**Why the noble gases split as they do (added).**

- **Periods.** The period lengths are the diagonal sum cells, (n, n) = 2n². These are 2 more than a multiple of 4 for odd n, and a multiple of 8 for even n.
- **Running sums.** The noble-gas numbers are the running sums 2, 2 + 8, 2 + 8 + 8, …. So a noble gas lands in the difference table only when an even number of odd-n periods have closed: krypton at 36, and next at 168.
- **The heaviest three.** They need three squares because 54 = 2 · 3³, 86 = 2 · 43 and 118 = 2 · 59 each carry a prime of the form 4k + 3 to an odd power.
- **Abundance.** The even–odd alternation of cosmic abundance, the Oddo–Harkins rule (Lodders 2021), shows the same parity, but its cause is nuclear pairing, not the tables.

**Where the tables stop fitting the atom: the filling order.** The Madelung order, by n + ℓ, turns the shell sizes 2, 8, 18 and 32 into the period lengths 2, 8, 8, 18, 18, 32 and 32. That order is not in the tables, and in 2025 it was shown not to be exact in the atoms either:

- **Baez (2025)** shows that the bound states of the spin-½ Kepler problem are unitarily equivalent to solutions of the Weyl equation on ℝ × S³, Fock's sphere with spin added. He derives the Madelung rules by modifying that free field's Hamiltonian, and calls them “a reasonable approximation to the observed filling of subshells”.
- **Bjerg, Fournais and Hearnshaw (2025)** prove that for large Z the angular-momentum distribution of a neutral atom follows the Thomas–Fermi distribution and departs from the Madelung rule.
- **The measured exceptions** run from chromium (24) to lawrencium (103):
  - lawrencium's ground state is predicted to be 7s²7p¹ rather than 6d¹, the first element whose configuration the relativistic stabilisation of 7p₁/₂ changes outright;
  - its first ionisation potential, 4.96 +0.08/−0.07 eV (Sato et al. 2015), agrees with relativistic coupled-cluster calculations (Guo et al. 2024).

**Where the table's own columns stop fitting: the superheavy elements, observed.**

- **Nihonium and moscovium.** Yakushev et al. (2024) measured nihonium (113) and moscovium (115) adsorbing on silicon oxide with −ΔH\_ads = 58 +8/−3 and 54 +11/−5 kJ/mol. That is about 100 kJ/mol weaker than their homologues thallium and bismuth, yet more reactive than their closed-shell neighbours copernicium (112) and flerovium (114): “the large energy splitting in the 7p shell and the stabilization of the 7p₁/₂² subshell has been confirmed experimentally”.
- **Flerovium and copernicium.** Flerovium behaves as a volatile metal rather than a noble gas (Yakushev et al. 2022), and copernicium as a volatile metal (Eichler et al. 2007).
- **Oganesson (118).** It is predicted to have no shell structure left in its electron density (Jerabek et al. 2018), to be a semiconductor (Mewes et al. 2019) and to be a solid at room temperature (Smits et al. 2020). That ends “noble gas” as a property of a column.
- **The same relativity elsewhere.** It turns gold yellow and keeps mercury liquid (Pyykkö 1988; Calvo et al. 2013). The limits of the table are reviewed by Smits et al. (2024).

**The search for elements 119 and 120.**

- **Berkeley.** In 2024 Berkeley Lab made two atoms of ²⁹⁰Lv with a titanium-50 beam on plutonium-244, at 0.44 +0.58/−0.28 pb (Gates et al. 2024). This is the beam that reaches element 120 on californium.
- **Other searches.** RIKEN's vanadium-51 on curium-248 search for element 119 has run since 2020, and JINR's chromium-54 on curium-248 search for element 120 is in preparation.
- **No atom yet.** None of these searches had reported an atom by September 2026, when Oak Ridge was fabricating the californium-249 target for Berkeley's search for element 120 (Myhre 2026). GSI's limits from 2007 to 2012 are 90 to 200 fb.

The tables say nothing about whether 119 or 120 will be found; they say only where those numbers sit.

**The ledger.**

- **Sum and difference cells.** These are listed with a ≥ b ≥ 1; for differences, the first three routes are given.
- **Three squares.** The three-square cell is given only where neither table reaches the element. It is written as the sum cell plus the extra leg of section 3.5.
- **The last column.** It gives the element's standing on her page as printed, with legs 1 to 10.

**Table 105**, all 118 elements in the three tables, is with the supplementary tables at the end of the paper.

### 14.6 Beside the standard theories

On this layer the standard theories compute everything, from the lines to the chemistry of the superheavy elements, and the calculator gives the same numbers as cells of its tables. Table 62 sets the two side by side, including where the tables stop.

**Table 62. Atoms on one yardstick.**

| Quantity | Standard theory | The calculator | Measured | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| hydrogen's lines | Rydberg's formula (Rydberg 1888); Bohr's levels (Bohr 1913) | the inverse difference table | the same lines; the residue is the fine structure and the Lamb shift (section 5.3) | same |
| the corrections | quantum electrodynamics, to parts in 10¹⁵ | further powers of the per-cycle coupling α; no new digit | the 1S–2S interval, 2,466,061,413,187,035 ± 10 Hz (Parthey et al. 2011) | same; QED computes |
| the n² degeneracy | Fock's four-dimensional symmetry (Fock 1935) | gnomons stacked; the diagonal sum cell (n, n) = 2n² | shells of 2, 8, 18 and 32 | same |
| the Coulomb problem | the four-dimensional oscillator under the Hopf map (Kustaanheimo and Stiefel 1965); w = z² in the plane (Levi-Civita 1920) | the atom as the square of the oscillator, one circle of phase folded away | — | same mathematics; reading |
| the filling order | the Madelung rule, approximate (Baez 2025), departing at large Z (Bjerg, Fournais and Hearnshaw 2025); configurations computed from first principles | not in the tables | exceptions from chromium (24) to lawrencium (103) | the standard theory computes; the calculator is silent |
| crystal-field splitting | point charges: tetrahedral −4/9 and cubic −8/9 of the octahedral splitting (Bethe 1929) | the same, from the face points, the alternate corners and the corners | cobalt chloride pink with six waters, blue with four chlorides | same |
| lattice neighbours | face-centred at a√2/2, body-centred at a√3/2 | the edge midpoints and the corners | Cu 2.556 Å, Al 2.863 Å; Fe 2.482 Å, W 2.741 Å | same |
| the reciprocal lattice | FCC and BCC are each other's reciprocal | the flip | — | same; reading |
| the periodic table | ordered by Z (Mendeleev 1869), its chemistry from quantum mechanics | every Z in the sum table, the difference table or one leg beyond; the tiers carry no chemistry | — | reading; number theory |
| the superheavy elements | relativistic quantum chemistry: the split 7p shell, flerovium and copernicium as volatile metals, oganesson a semiconductor | not in the tables | nihonium and moscovium on silicon oxide (Yakushev et al. 2024) | the standard theory computes and is confirmed; the calculator is silent |
| the next closed shell | 168 by the Madelung order, about 172 relativistically (Pyykkö 2011) | both are differences, 13² − 1² and 44² − 42²; the tables do not choose | — | the standard theory predicts; the calculator is silent |

**The score on this layer.**

- **The same numbers.** Every number here is the standard theories'; the calculator reads each as a cell and adds no digit.
- **Where the tables stop.** The filling order, the chemistry of the superheavy elements and the next closed shell are computed by quantum mechanics with relativity, and confirmed where they have been measured; the tables say only where the numbers sit.
- **Checked against chance.** The element tiers are a property of the integer Z, and three checks show that they carry no chemistry (section 3.5).

**Origination.**

- **M.** Rydberg (1888), Bohr (1913) and Fock (1935); Levi-Civita (1920) and Kustaanheimo and Stiefel (1965); the Newton–Hooke duality (Arnold 1990); the point-charge crystal field (Bethe 1929); Maxwell's equations under inversion (Bateman 1910; Cunningham 1910); the theorems on sums of squares (section 3.5).
- **N.** Reading the lines, the shells, the crystals and the elements as cells of the two tables, the author's single figure (*Root Elements*; *Calc Elements*); the extension of her element page to all 118, with its corrections (added); the noble gases' split as parity (added).
- **D.** None: the tables predict no chemistry, as checked.

## 15. The layout: what survives every permutation

A layout is one choice for each of the calculator's relations. Each choice has alternatives inside the cube's own sets, and this section tries them all: every arrangement is carried to every measurement it reaches and scored by the author's behavior rule, that an alignment stays only if nothing downstream of it contradicts a measurement (section 1.3). The rule leaves the calculator's layout standing, and in every family no surviving alternative does better on data it was not chosen with. Sections 5 to 14 compute reality silo by silo with that layout, and section 20 names what each silo still lacks. The search is reproduced by permute.py and oos.py (section 21.1).

### 15.1 The space of permutations

Each of the calculator's relations is built from a few choices, and each choice has alternatives inside the cube's own sets: the 40 strict angles of section 17.7.4, the alphabet of section 17.7, the ball's share of the n-cube, the three places where the forces cross, and simple fractions and multiples. A permutation is one choice for each. Table 63 lists the nine families, the choices in each and the measurements each reaches. Together they make 4.4 × 10²⁰ permutations of the whole calculator. The families share only the two choices of section 15.5, so each is scored on its own: 43,188 permutations in all.

**Table 63. The nine families of permutations.**

| Family | Choices | Permutations | Measurements reached |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| charged-lepton masses | the balance angle (23 values of Q from the strict angles); a phase (the rationals m/n with n ≤ 12, and the strict angles, below 2π/3); the phase spacing (90°, 120° or 180°); the slot order of e, μ and τ; the branch; the rule: the balance alone, the phase alone, both, or the phase as Q/n with n ≤ 6 | 41,320 | m\_τ; with two rules, m\_μ/mₑ as well |
| neutrino masses | the balance angle, the ordering, which root is flipped | 12 solutions | the cosmological cap and effective sum, KATRIN's m\_β, KamLAND-Zen's m\_ββ |
| lepton mixing | a column or a row kept, which one, the tilt (0° and the 40 strict angles, either sign), the phase real or free | 738 | sin²θ₁₃; sin²θ₁₂ from NuFIT and from JUNO; sin²θ₂₃ |
| the quark angle α | α among the 40 strict angles and 100°, 105°, 109.47° and 120°, read as the angle between the up and down legs or not | 88 | α in all channels, from B → ρρ, and as 180° − β − γ; the legs' angle from lattice ratios and from PDG masses |
| the load on the Higgs vacuum | the couplings loaded (y\_t² alone, or with g², g′², g\_s², or g² and g′²), their spacing (0°, 60°, 90°, 120° or 180°), the normalisation (4π², 8π² or 16π²) | 63 | the top, direct and pole; the Higgs; the electron's α/2π |
| the proton's spin | the split of ½ into ½ΔΣ, ΔG and L in 24ths | 91 | ΔΣ from HERMES and the lattice; ΔG from the lattice |
| the proton's radius | rₚ/ƛₚ among the 310 alphabet members from 1 to 8, with or without a charge at c round the circle | 620 | rₚ from CODATA, muonic hydrogen and the electron–hydrogen average; with the ring, the proton's moment |
| the matter share | the ball's share of the n-cube, n = 1 to 8, or its complement | 16 | Ωₘ and Ω\_Λ from six data sets, q₀ from two, E(z = 0.349) |
| the vacuum's energy | the crossing (M₁₂, M₁₃ or M₂₃), the scale (v, m\_H, m\_t, m\_Z or m\_W) and a coefficient (16 simple values) | 240 | the vacuum energy scale |

### 15.2 What survives

Each permutation is carried to every measurement it reaches. One measurement per quantity decides, the primary of Tables 69 and 102, and the others are reported beside it. A permutation stays if no primary misses by more than 2σ. A miss that every permutation in a family shows is inherited: it stays on the record and eliminates nothing. Table 64 gives the result. Of the 4.4 × 10²⁰ permutations of the whole calculator, 76,800 survive, one in 5.7 × 10¹⁵. That fraction measures how tightly observation binds the cube's choices. It is not by itself the improbability of the layout, which was built with the data in view; section 17 measures what the surviving agreement is worth beyond what the sets could supply by chance.

**Table 64. What survives.**

| Family | Permutations | Survive | Inherited miss | The calculator's current choice | Best survivor on the primaries |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| charged-lepton masses | 41,320 | 3 | none | the balance and the phase together: out, m\_μ/mₑ at −442σ; the balance alone stays, m\_τ −0.43σ | the phase 2/9 alone, −0.41σ |
| neutrino masses | 12 | 4 | the effective sum, −3.4σ | stays | all four inside the caps |
| lepton mixing | 738 | 2 | none | stays: θ₁₃ −0.59σ, θ₁₂ −1.49σ | the same frame turned the other way |
| the quark angle α | 88 | 10 | none | 90° as the legs' angle: out, the legs at −2.05σ (lattice); 90° alone stays, −1.59σ | 84.74° alone, −0.17σ |
| the load | 63 | 16 | none | stays: top +0.07σ, Higgs −0.07σ | y\_t² and g′² at 180°: top +0.13σ |
| the proton's spin | 91 | 4 | none | stays and is best: ΔΣ −0.09σ, ΔG −0.22σ | the current choice |
| the proton's radius | 620 | 1 | none | 4ƛₚ stays, −0.77σ; with the ring, out: the moment misses by 1.5 × 10⁹σ | the current choice |
| the matter share | 16 | 1 | E(z = 0.349), +7.2σ | stays, −1.23σ | the current choice |
| the vacuum's energy | 240 | 5 | none | stays, +1.11σ | m\_t²/(8π² M₁₃), +0.09σ |

### 15.3 Three readings withdrawn

The rule removes three readings the calculator carried, and keeps the number each was attached to.

- **The lepton phase as a second rule.** Q = ⅔ alone gives m\_τ = 1,776.9690 MeV (−0.43σ), δ = 2/9 alone gives 1,776.9665 MeV (−0.41σ), and δ = Q/3 alone gives 1,776.9671 MeV (−0.41σ). Any two of them together fix the muon from the electron: Q = ⅔ with δ = 2/9 gives m\_μ/mₑ = 206.770316, against 206.7682827 ± 0.0000046 (Mohr et al. 2024), a miss of 442σ. The calculator keeps the balance, which its rule states and which is the most selective of the three (section 15.4), and computes the phase from the electron and the muon: δ = 0.2222220471 ± 0.0000000004 rad, 2/9 to 0.8 parts per million but not on it, so 3δ = Q to the same 0.8 parts per million. The phase measured from all three masses, 0.2222248 ± 0.0000063 rad, tests the same τ mass as Q and is not independent of it.
- **The right angle as the legs' angle.** α = 90° stays as a value: −1.6σ in all channels, +0.6σ from B → ρρ, +0.4σ as 180° − β − γ. The legs do not supply it. They sit at 85.9° ± 2.0° on lattice ratios and 83.5° ± 2.1° with PDG masses, 2.05σ and 3.1σ from 90°.
- **The proton's ring.** A charge at c round 4ƛₚ gives a moment of 4 μ\_N, and folded into three reactions it gives 3 μ\_N (section 12.2). The moment is 2.79284734462(82) μ\_N (Schneider et al. 2017), so both readings miss by more than 10⁸σ. The radius stays as a number, with no current read into its circle.

### 15.4 The frontier: alternatives tested on data they were not chosen with

A search this large finds better-looking fits by luck. Two tests separate luck from precision.

**Out of sample.** Each family chooses its permutation on one measurement and is scored on the others (Table 65). Where a permutation fits the training measurement better than the current choice, it fails a test the current choice passes. Elsewhere the training measurement picks the current choice.

**Table 65. Chosen on one measurement, tested on the others.**

| Family | Trained on | Chosen | Tested on | The chosen | The current choice |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| lepton mixing | sin²θ₁₃ | the second column tilted 10.53°, −0.46σ | sin²θ₁₂ from NuFIT; from JUNO | −5.50σ; −3.65σ | −1.49σ; −1.02σ |
| the quark angle α | α in all channels | 84.74°, −0.17σ | 180° − β − γ; B → ρρ | +2.24σ; +1.67σ | +0.36σ; +0.55σ |
| the load | the direct top mass | the current choice, +0.07σ | the top's pole mass | −0.66σ | −0.66σ |
| the proton's spin | ΔΣ from HERMES | ½ΔΣ = 4/24, with ΔG left open among 9 splits | ΔΣ and ΔG on the lattice | +1.06σ; 4 of the 9 within 2σ | +1.06σ; −0.22σ |
| the proton's radius | CODATA | 4ƛₚ, −0.77σ | muonic hydrogen; the electron–hydrogen average | −0.94σ; +2.05σ | the same |
| the matter share | supernovae with the CMB and BAO | π²/32, −1.23σ | seven other data sets | five within 2σ; −2.32σ and −2.56σ | the same |

The share's two misses are tensions among the data sets themselves: no single Ωₘ passes all seven, and Planck's 0.3153 misses three. The electron–hydrogen average carries χ²/dof = 2.5 across its inputs (section 12.1).

**Look-elsewhere.** For each family the test draws the true values at random within ±25% of the measured ones, ±5% for the top and five decades for the vacuum's energy, and asks how often some surviving permutation matches them as closely as the current choice matches the real ones: 20,000 draws for two measurements, 200,000 for one. Table 66 gives the chance as close as observed, and with each window floored at 1σ, the convention section 17.8 applies to the standard theories.

**Table 66. How often a family of this size matches random values as well.**

| Family | Reference | As close as observed | Floored at 1σ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| charged-lepton masses, every rule | Q = ⅔ | 0.0096, 6.7 bits | 0.022, 5.5 bits |
| the balance alone, 16 permutations | Q = ⅔ | 0.00015, 12.8 bits | 0.00046, 11.1 bits |
| the phase alone, 4,268 permutations | δ = 2/9 | 0.0087, 6.8 bits | 0.022, 5.5 bits |
| the phase as Q/n, 58 permutations | δ = Q/3 | 0.00026, 11.9 bits | 0.00053, 10.9 bits |
| lepton mixing | the 15° tilt | 0.073, 3.8 bits | 0.073, 3.8 bits |
| the quark angle α | 90° | 1, 0 bits | 1, 0 bits |
| the load | y\_t² and g² at 120° | 0.071, 3.8 bits | 0.28, 1.9 bits |
| the proton's spin | sixths | 0.075, 3.7 bits | 1, 0 bits |
| the proton's radius | 4ƛₚ | 0.16, 2.6 bits | 0.21, 2.2 bits |
| the matter share | π²/32 | 0.049, 4.4 bits | 0.056, 4.1 bits |
| the vacuum's energy | v²/M₂₃ | 0.94, 0.1 bits | 0.94, 0.1 bits |
| **the calculator's own seven, by Fisher's method** |  | **0.030, 5.1 bits** | **0.27, 1.9 bits** |

Counted within the cube's own sets, the calculator's own seven numbers together carry 5.1 bits, p = 0.030: families of alternatives this size match random values as well about once in 33 draws. Floored at 1σ, the convention section 17.8 applies to the standard theories' predictions, they carry 1.9 bits. Counted against the whole alphabet, which holds members the layout never uses, they carry 0.6 to 4.0 (section 17.7.4). The balance Q = ⅔ carries 6.7 bits against every lepton rule tried and 12.8 within its own class, beside 9.7 bits against the alphabet. The families were defined after the data were seen, so any freedom in defining them makes these numbers overstate the evidence, never understate it.

So no permutation is more precise than the current values on data it was not chosen with. Two alternatives fit their primaries better and remain alternatives. α = 84.74° read as the legs' angle (the balance angle plus 30°) is 5.26° from 90°, which Belle II and LHCb at ±2° separate at 2.6σ by about 2030. The vacuum's energy as m\_t²/(8π² M₁₃) comes from a family that matches by chance.

### 15.5 One layout across the families

Two choices are shared across families, and they agree.

- **The spacing.** The leptons' three phases sit 120° apart. Four of the sixteen surviving loads use that spacing, and the current load is one of them.
- **The balance.** Q = ⅔ is shared by the charged leptons and the neutrinos. Of the four neutrino solutions it picks one, with a sum of 59.16 meV.

The share and the vacuum's energy together fix H₀: the current pair gives 54.5 +11.3/−10.3 km/s/Mpc, 1.1σ below the microwave background's value and 1.7σ below the ladder's (section 22.4).

### 15.6 What the layout leaves missing

In this layout every relation the rule retains meets its measurement. What it does not reach is of two kinds, and neither is a choice the search could change:

- **Inherited misses.** Two measurements miss for every arrangement of their family: ΛCDM's effective neutrino mass sits 3.4σ below every neutrino solution, and the expansion rate at z = 0.349 sits 7.2σ above every share. The standard cosmology misses them the same way, the oscillation floor by 3.0σ and Planck's ΛCDM curve by 6.5σ, and both come from the same late-time data (section 16.3).
- **Silences.** Nine of the 36 behaviours of Table 101 no arrangement reaches, among them θ₂₃, the light elements and the mass scale of a family.

Rearranging cannot supply either kind, so each marks a component, relationship or factor the layout does not yet contain. Section 20 names them, silo by silo.

### 15.7 Precision: digits beyond the measurement

A calculator earns its place by speed and precision: it should give a right number sooner, or to more digits, than the alternatives. With the layout fixed, this subsection and the next measure both. The numerics are reproduced by numerics.py and bench43.js (section 21.1).

Where a relation holds, a closed form carries no error of its own, so its precision is that of its inputs. Table 67 sets the calculator's precision beside the measurement's and the standard theories'.

**Table 67. Precision, where each number holds.**

| Quantity | The calculator | Measured | The standard theories |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| the τ mass, from mₑ and m\_μ | 1,776.96903 ± 0.00004 MeV | 1,776.93 ± 0.09 MeV (PDG 2026) | an input |
| sin²θ₁₃ | sin²15°/3 = 0.0223291, exact | 0.02200 ± 0.00056 | an input |
| sin²θ₁₂ | 0.3181073, exact | 0.3096 +0.0057/−0.0073 | an input |
| ΔΣ at 2 GeV | ⅓, exact | 0.330 ± 0.039 (HERMES) | the lattice: 0.380 ± 0.044 (Alexandrou et al. 2026) |
| ΔG at 2 GeV | ⅙, exact | no full moment yet | the lattice: 0.158 ± 0.039 (Zhao et al. 2025) |
| rₚ | 4ƛₚ = 0.8412356 fm, to 3 parts in 10¹⁰ | 0.84075 ± 0.00064 fm (CODATA 2022) | the lattice: 0.820 ± 0.014 fm (Djukanovic et al. 2024) |
| Ωₘ | π²/32 = 0.3084251, exact | 0.3045 ± 0.0032 | fitted |
| the neutrino masses | 0.364, 8.662 and 50.131 meV, fixed by the two splittings | the sum below 64 meV | none in the minimal theory |
| the top's pole mass | 172.57 ± 0.38 GeV | 172.60 ± 0.27 GeV direct; 172.1 ± 0.6 GeV pole | an input |
| the vacuum's energy scale | 2.02 ± 0.20 meV | 2.24 ± 0.01 meV | about 10³⁰ too large in energy, 10¹²⁰ in density |

The τ mass is 2,500 times finer than its measurement; the angles, the shares and the radius are exact, the radius two million times finer than its measurement. Each of those digits counts only if its relation holds, which the frozen predictions decide (section 18.1). The top and the vacuum's energy are coarser than their measurements.

### 15.8 Speed: the calculator's numerics

The law of motion and the vacuum's edge were the calculator's slowest parts. Both are now computed by methods that converge to the last digit, or that keep the error far below what the inputs allow (Table 68).

- **Orbits and light, exactly.** Between turning points the swept angle is one integral, Δφ = 2∫ L dr/(r² p\_r) − 2π. With the substitution r = r̄ − Δr cos θ the turning points become the ends of a smooth periodic integrand. With the turning points factored out by divided differences, no small quantity is formed as the difference of two large ones, and the trapezoid rule converges exponentially (Trefethen and Weideman 2014). For both legs, in Schwarzschild's radial coordinate, the integral is Darwin's elliptic one (Darwin 1959). Light's deflection is the same integral over the index n = √(B/A), taken by Gauss–Legendre quadrature outward from the closest approach. Against 50-digit quadrature of the same integrals, every case agrees to 6 parts in 10¹⁵ or better: Mercury's advance is 42.9807236394″ per century with both legs and 14.3269060639″ with the time leg alone.
- **The vacuum's edge.** The beta functions are evaluated without allocation and without powers. The searches run at 400 steps, where the step error is 2 × 10⁻⁸ GeV, and a secant search starts beside the answer, with the bracketing search kept as the fallback. The edge's numerical error grows from 10⁻¹⁰ to 2 to 5 × 10⁻⁸ GeV, still about ten million times below the 0.38 GeV the inputs allow.
- **The Higgs's off-shell decays.** H → WW\* and ZZ\* are double integrals over two Breit–Wigner peaks. The lighter boson is kept below m\_H/2, the heavier one's peak is flattened by s = m² + mΓ sinh u, and both integrals are taken by double-exponential quadrature (Takahasi and Mori 1974). The earlier grid ran 0.14% and 0.16% low, so the calculator showed a total width of 4.03 MeV at 125.00 GeV; it now shows the 4.04 MeV of Table 34.

**Table 68. The calculator's numerics, Version 42 against Version 43.** Median times in V8, the engine of Chromium-based browsers (Node 22), on the machine that ran the scripts; errors against converged references.

| Computation | Version 42: time; error | Version 43: time; error | Faster |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Mercury's perihelion advance, both legs | 34 ms; 6 × 10⁻⁸ | 2.0 μs; 4 × 10⁻¹⁶ | 17,000 times |
| the same, the time leg alone | 28 ms; 1 × 10⁻⁷ | 2.6 μs; 3 × 10⁻¹⁶ | 11,000 times |
| an orbit with e = 0.95, both legs | 23 ms; 2 × 10⁻³ | 1.5 μs; 7 × 10⁻¹⁶ | 16,000 times |
| light at the Sun's edge, both legs | 17 ms; 1.5 × 10⁻⁹ | 26 μs; 3 × 10⁻¹⁶ | 640 times |
| the same, the time leg alone | 19 ms; 1.4 × 10⁻⁹ | 5.6 μs; 1 × 10⁻¹⁶ | 3,400 times |
| the vacuum's edge, for the top | 53 ms; 9 × 10⁻¹¹ GeV | 0.38 ms; 2 × 10⁻⁸ GeV | 140 times |
| the same, for the Higgs | 63 ms; 8 × 10⁻¹¹ GeV | 0.44 ms; 5 × 10⁻⁸ GeV | 140 times |
| the edge's error budget | 319 to 341 ms | 2.3 to 2.4 ms | 130 to 150 times |
| the running shown, 3,000 steps | 7.5 ms | 0.61 ms, the same to 10⁻¹⁵ | 12 times |
| where the forces cross, 6,000 steps | 21 ms | 1.6 ms, the same | 14 times |
| H → WW\* | 3.7 ms; 1.4 × 10⁻³ low | 0.83 ms; 4 × 10⁻¹¹ | 4 times |
| H → ZZ\* | 3.6 ms; 1.6 × 10⁻³ low | 0.87 ms; 8 × 10⁻¹² | 4 times |
| the page's computation in its first nine seconds, in Chromium | 1.6 to 1.8 s | 0.72 to 0.79 s, most of it the lightning animation | about 2 times |

None of this changes a number at the digits the paper quotes. The speed of the law of motion and of the edge is a property of the method, and the method is standard: the calculator now computes the established results as fast and as precisely as the best standard methods do.

**Origination.** **M**: the trapezoid rule's exponential convergence is Trefethen and Weideman's (2014), the orbit integral Darwin's (1959), and double-exponential quadrature Takahasi and Mori's (1974). **N** (added): the divided-difference forms of the integrals.

### 15.9 What the elimination shows

- **The layout.** Of 4.4 × 10²⁰ arrangements inside the cube's own sets, 76,800 survive the behavior rule, and the calculator's layout is the frontier of every family: no survivor beats its values on measurements it was not chosen with, and the choices the families share agree.
- **Three readings out.** The lepton phase as a second rule, the legs as the source of the right angle, and the proton's ring. Their numbers stay: the balance, α = 90° and 4ƛₚ.
- **Agreement with observation.** Every relation the layout retains meets its measurement, and nothing downstream of any of them is broken; what is missing is named in section 20.
- **What kind of result this is.** A numerological fit matches constants one at a time and owes nothing to its neighbours. The layout is one arrangement that holds across nine families at once, whose shared choices agree, whose downstream consequences hold, and which no alternative inside its sets beats on data it was not chosen with. That is a structural result, and the paper counts it as one. What it does not do by itself is show that the cube is reality's mechanism rather than the best notation its sets allow: a correct layout and a best fit look alike from inside their family, which is why section 17 measures the difference and section 18 freezes the predictions that decide it.

**Origination.** **M**: the behavior rule is the author's, and Fisher's method Fisher's (1932). **N** (added): the permutation families, and the out-of-sample and look-elsewhere tests applied to them.

## 16. The scorecard: the layout and the standard theories on one yardstick

Every number the calculator commits to sits within 1.6σ of the primary measurement in its row (Table 69). For the same 22 quantities the standard theories mostly take inputs: eleven are free parameters or assumptions of the Standard Model or ΛCDM, one follows from them, six are predictions the calculator shares, three are lattice computations, and one, the vacuum's energy, is a standard prediction that fails by about 10¹²⁰. The exposures appear where the same data are read another way. The ΛCDM effective neutrino mass, the curvature from DESI with the microwave background and the preferred drift in w are one cosmological discrepancy, and it presses on ΛCDM as hard as on the calculator (section 16.3). The table records where the layout stands against the data; section 17 weighs how much of that agreement chance could give. Pulls are (measured − computed)/σ; for an asymmetric error the side facing the computed value is used.

**Independence.** Counted honestly, Table 69 holds fewer independent numbers of the calculator's own than it has rows.

- **Standard results.** The gravity rows, the curvature, the black-hole return and the deceleration are standard results, or follow from other rows.
- **Followers.** The Higgs follows from the top, the τ and the phase from the angle, and sin²θ₁₂ from the same 15° as sin²θ₁₃.
- **Inherited.** The Koide angle is Koide's, and the three-phase form of its phase Brannen's. α = 90° is Fritzsch and Xing's value, and rₚ = 4ƛₚ is prior art.
- **The calculator's own.** That leaves the 15° tilt, the δ₃ load, the spin thirds (ΔΣ and ΔG), the hum and π²/32, with w = −1 shared with ΛCDM.

**What the table cannot settle.** Every one of these numbers sits within 1.6σ of its primary measurement, and section 17 asks whether that is more than chance. The standard theories score no pull on the eleven inputs, because a fitted number cannot miss.

### 16.2 New numbers, re-readings and deviations

**Where the standard theories take an input.** The Standard Model has 19 free parameters before neutrino masses and 26 after. General relativity and ΛCDM add G, Λ, the matter densities and the initial conditions. A theory that fits a number cannot miss it, so on the yardstick of section 2.5 those rows earn no pull for either theory; they score where the calculator gives a number and the standard theory does not, and section 17 asks whether those numbers beat chance.

Three kinds of statement appear in this paper and should not be confused:

- **A re-reading** keeps every standard number and changes the account.
- **A new number** stands where the standard theory has an input, and can be wrong.
- **A deviation** says something the standard theory denies or leaves open, with a consequence that can fail.

Table 70 sorts each committed statement into the three kinds, with the standard answer beside it and what would show it wrong.

**What only the calculator says.** The ten tables headed “on one yardstick” (Tables 28, 30, 31, 32, 45, 48, 54, 55, 58 and 62) set the standard answer beside the calculator's on every layer. Everything in them that is not listed here is the standard result reached by a geometric road. The calculator's own statements are:

- a reason for the Koide relation;
- the neutrino masses from the same equation;
- a value for the mixing tilt, and the reason the α axis survives;
- the top and the Higgs from the edge and one load;
- the three-phase start as the reason the forces cross in pairs;
- the anomaly conditions as a code's parity checks;
- w = −1, with the feed from a held store;
- a size for the hum and a number for the share;
- the gluon share;
- one picture in which the black hole's inversion, the torus, the held foundation and the pull from within are one object.

Of these, section 17 scores only the ones with a number. Table 87 holds the frozen ones.

### 16.3 Published tensions, cell by cell

Thirty-six published tensions and tests were placed in the calculator's cells. Scored for the calculator:

- **14 agree;**
- **5 expose it**, four of them through cosmology;
- **15 land in named cells where it commits to no number:** H₀, S₈, the matter dipole, R(D), wide binaries and the Higgs self-coupling among them;
- **2 have no cell:** X17 and the lithium abundance.

The same 36, scored for the standard theories: 13 agree, 15 are tensions of 2σ or more, 4 are inputs and 4 are open or experimental. The list was assembled from published tensions, so it is weighted against the standard theories by construction. It shows where each theory is exposed, not its overall record, which section 17.8 scores.

The exposures cluster:

- **Shared.** Four of the calculator's five exposures are tensions of ΛCDM in the same data: the floor neutrino mass, w = −1, the curvature and the expansion rate. Only sin²θ₁₂ is the calculator's alone.
- **One discrepancy, several faces.** Flat curvature, w = −1 and the floor neutrino mass are one 3σ discrepancy with three faces. Elbers et al. (2025) find the ΛCDM effective neutrino mass 3.0σ below the normal-ordering floor, a tension that eases only if w evolves.
- **One edge.** All of them sit on one edge, the matter share. DESI's BAO gives Ωₘ = 0.2975, the supernovae with the CMB give 0.317, and π²/32 = 0.3084 lies between them. DESI with Planck and ACT gives 0.3003 ± 0.0035, which puts π²/32 at −2.3σ (Garcia-Quintero et al. 2025).
- **Recalibration.** DES's recalibrated supernovae lower the preference for evolving dark energy from 4.2σ to 3.2σ (Popovic et al. 2025).

A rate cell gives almost any rate a side, so the 15 placements without numbers are bookkeeping, not evidence.

Across the ten circles of the layers, 33 of the 40 positions hold a published measurement (section 4.10.2). The other seven are named empty cells, each with the measurement that would fill it: for example, an Aharonov–Bohm measurement with positrons, the antitop's width, and the cosmic jerk.

### 16.4 The author's physical descriptions against observation

The author described eighteen physical behaviours in words before any of them was compared with data. This section scores each one. The derivations behind several items are in sections 5 to 14, and are not repeated here.

#### 16.4.1 How each was tested

- **Predictions first.** Each description was turned into something it predicts we should see, stated before looking.
- **What counts.** Only measurements count: published observations, instrumented recordings and the paper's own runs on public collision data. Simulations and eyewitness reports are marked as such.
- **The verdicts.**
  - Confirmed: the prediction is observed.
  - Denied: an observation contradicts it.
  - Open: the effect is observed, but cannot yet tell the author's mechanism from the standard one.
- **Two readings.** Where a description has two readings, both are scored.

#### 16.4.2 The scorecard

Table 72 scores each of the eighteen descriptions against the data, with the standard account of the same observation beside it.
