**The tally.** Fifteen descriptions are confirmed, two are confirmed in part, and one is open. None, as stated, is contradicted. For every confirmed item the standard account describes the same observation, so the verdicts weigh the author's descriptions against nature, not one theory against another. They are not evidence that the calculator's numbers are more than numerology; that question belongs to section 17.

#### 16.4.3 The evidence, item by item

**1. Lightning radiates in all directions, and we see one zigzag.** Where the field is even, a discharge spreads from its source in every direction and branches as it goes: Lichtenberg figures and laboratory streamer bursts. In a storm the field points mostly down, so leaders spread down and sideways.

- **The growth model.** Grow a discharge on a grid of legs by the dielectric-breakdown rule, each new piece where the field is strongest with probability ∝ E^η (Niemeyer, Pietronero and Wiesmann 1984). For η = 1 the pattern has fractal dimension 1.66 in the paper's runs, against the published 1.7.
- **The connecting path.** Each centre-to-tip path is 1.51 times its straight distance, 41% of its steps change direction, and the connecting branch points every way round the circle over 16 runs.
- **Each step is an event in the air.** 13 of 14 recorded leader steps fired an X-ray burst (Dwyer 2005), with steps every 10 to 50 μs.
- **No fixed grid.** Stereo cameras agree on one three-dimensional zigzag (Willett, Le Vine and Idone 2008). Real channels turn 17.3° per segment on average (Hill 1968), not 90°, so the legs cannot be a fixed grid in space; the folding of the turns is in sections 4.5 and 4.10.3.

**2. Lightning picks one path.**

- **One contact.** The downward leader branches and several upward leaders rise from the ground, but only one upward leader meets a branch. That contact fixes the strike point, and the return stroke follows it (Dwyer and Uman 2014).
- **The video record.** High-speed video of one flash caught seven upward leaders that never connected beside the one that did (Tran and Rakov 2017).
- **Not a photon.** The difference from a photon is that the winner is whichever leader reaches the other first, not a quantum measurement.

**3. The three-phase diagonal.**

- **Third harmonics.** The three third harmonics of a balanced set are in phase, pure (1, 1, 1), invisible to the Clarke view, and they add in the neutral (section 3.7.1).
- **The √3 ceiling.** For current pulses that do not overlap between phases, as rectifier loads draw, the cross terms vanish. Then I\_N,rms² = 3 I\_p,rms², so the neutral carries √3 = 173% of the phase current (section 14.4).
- **The 2f pulse.** Single-phase power pulses at twice the line frequency. Lamps flicker at 100 Hz on 50 Hz grids and at 120 Hz on 60 Hz grids (Wilkins, Veitch and Lehman 2010; Pacific Northwest National Laboratory 2016), and the 2012 ball lightning pulsed at 99.4 Hz beside a 50 Hz line (section 11.3).
- **The reading.** “Absorbs at √2, holds at √4” is the author's reading of these exact ratios. The ratios themselves are measured.

**4. The missing leg is filled by phases.**

- **W and Z have the third leg.** Massive W and Z bosons carry the third, longitudinal, polarisation.
  - In top-quark decays its share is 0.684 ± 0.015, against 0.687 ± 0.005 predicted (ATLAS Collaboration 2023, W helicity).
  - Pairs with both legs filled are seen in WZ at 7.1σ (ATLAS Collaboration 2023, WZ polarisation) and in ZZ at 4.3σ (ATLAS Collaboration 2023, ZZ).
  - Same-sign WW pairs with at least one leg filled are seen at 3.3σ (ATLAS Collaboration 2025).
- **The photon stays two-legged.** Its mass is below 10⁻¹⁸ eV (PDG).
- **The Higgs fills it at the predicted strength.** Its coupling to W and Z is κ\_V = 1.035 ± 0.031 (ATLAS Collaboration 2022). The Goldstone equivalence behind this is in section 3.7.1.

**5. Gravity as the perpendicular pull.**

- **Gravity acts through time.**
  - A clock raised 33 cm runs faster by (4.1 ± 1.6) × 10⁻¹⁷ (Chou et al. 2010).
  - Light climbing a 22.5 m tower shifts as predicted, with a ratio of 1.05 ± 0.10 (Pound and Rebka 1960).
- **Light is pulled differently.** Light bends twice the Newtonian amount:
  - γ = 0.99983 ± 0.00045 from radio deflection (Shapiro et al. 2004);
  - γ − 1 = (2.1 ± 2.3) × 10⁻⁵ from the Cassini signal delay (Bertotti, Iess and Tortora 2003).
- **Vacuum energy pushes.** Its pressure-to-energy ratio is w = −1.03 ± 0.03 (Planck Collaboration 2020), so it accelerates the expansion. DESI's second data release prefers an evolving w at 2.8 to 4.2σ (DESI Collaboration 2025).
- **Horizons exist.** The Event Horizon Telescope imaged M87\*'s ring of 42 ± 3 μas, consistent with a spinning black hole, and Sgr A\*'s ring within about 10% of prediction (Event Horizon Telescope Collaboration 2019, 2022).
- **The literal reading is denied.** Ordinary laboratory masses, with no black hole involved, attract by the inverse-square law down to 52 μm (Lee et al. 2020).

**6. Ball lightning held on the √4 side.** The one instrumented ball, its 2f pulse and the power bound are in section 11.3.

- **Laboratory balls.** Balls of vaporised silicon last up to 8 s on stored energy (Paiva et al. 2007). Microwave fireballs last only while the power is on (Dikhtyar and Jerby 2006).
- **Invisible until plasma.** Below breakdown, current in air is a dark discharge with no visible light. Light appears once electrons gain enough energy to excite atoms (Treumann, Kłos and Parrot 2008).

**7. The fabric is dynamic.** Ripples in spacetime were detected directly: GW150914, with peak strain 1.0 × 10⁻²¹, above 5.1σ (LIGO Scientific and Virgo Collaborations 2016).

**8. Our perspective carries the distortion.**

- **Our motion.** Our motion, about 370 to 384 km/s, distorts the microwave sky. The aberration is seen at 4 to 5σ in each of four methods (Planck Collaboration 2014).
- **Terrell rotation.** The relativistic apparent rotation predicted by Terrell and Penrose was photographed in 2025 (Hornof et al. 2025).
- **Muon clocks.** Fast muons live 29.33 times longer, as relativity predicts, to 0.2% (Bailey et al. 1977).

**9. The T-bar flip; negative is not reverse.**

- **The flip.** The T-handle flip was shown in orbit in 1985; there is a historical report but no primary paper (Trivailo and Kojima 2017). Montgomery's formula describes it (Montgomery 1991). The flip time in sums and differences of squares is in section 4.4.1, and a phone's gyroscope record is in section 4.7.4.
- **Antimatter falls.** Antimatter, the “negative” of matter, still falls down: a\_g = (0.75 ± 0.13 ± 0.16) g, and repulsive antigravity is ruled out (Anderson et al. 2023).

**10. Balance: just past the edge, held.** The full test, with the friction angles, is in section 9.4.

**11. Lightning carries what the gravity well holds.**

- **The well builds the air.** Each layer of air holds up the weight above it, dp/dz = −ρg, so pressure falls on a scale H = RT/(Mg) = 8.43 km at the ground. Integrated with the standard atmosphere's temperatures, this gives 1.05 Pa at 80 km, about 1/96,000 of ground pressure; the U.S. Standard Atmosphere (1976) lists 1.052 Pa.
- **The air sets the scale.** A discharge depends on the field per molecule E/N and the number of molecules crossed, N·d (Townsend similarity). So when the density falls by a factor F, every length stretches by F, and streamer width times pressure stays at 0.20 ± 0.02 mm·bar (Briels et al. 2008; Ebert et al. 2010). At 80 km the air is about 10⁵ times thinner, so a 0.2 mm streamer at the ground is 19 m wide, the width of real sprites.
- **A different well.** With the same temperatures, gravity 10% weaker would shrink the minimum sprite streamer to 6 m, and 10% stronger would grow it to 60 m. A local gravity anomaly of 1 part in 10⁴ changes the pressure at 80 km by only 0.1%, so it is the planet's mass that counts.
- **The circulation is perpendicular.** Storms charge inside buoyant updrafts while heavier graupel falls through them, and the collisions sort charge into vertical layers (Saunders 2008). The flash rate tracks the volume of updraft faster than 5 to 10 m/s in the below-freezing zone, r = 0.93 (Deierling and Petersen 2008).
- **What the well holds matters.**
  - Ship-exhaust particles double the lightning over the busiest shipping lanes (Thornton et al. 2017).
  - Jupiter, at 2.5 times Earth's gravity with hydrogen air and water and ammonia clouds, has lightning, including shallow lightning high in ammonia–water clouds (Brown et al. 2018; Becker et al. 2020).
  - Mars, at 0.38 g with thin carbon dioxide and no rain clouds, gives only small sparks inside dust devils (Chide et al. 2025).

**12. The fabric as massive filament pathways.**

- **The pathways exist.** Galaxies lie along filaments and walls around empty voids, first mapped as the CfA “Great Wall” (Geller and Huchra 1989) and named the cosmic web (Bond, Kofman and Pogosyan 1996).
  - Lensing shows that a filament between the clusters Abell 222 and 223 pulls far harder than its visible matter can (Dietrich et al. 2012).
  - The glowing gas of a filament joining two quasars, seen as it was about 11.5 billion years ago, was imaged directly (Tornotti et al. 2025).
- **We sit in a flow.** The Milky Way lies inside Laniakea, a basin about 520 million light-years across defined by galaxy motions, in which galaxies flow inward toward the Great Attractor region (Tully et al. 2014).
- **The whole observable universe in one path is open.**
  - For: across 1.36 million quasars, the pile-up in our direction of motion is over twice what our motion explains, at 4.9σ (Secrest et al. 2021). The Giant Arc (3.3 billion light-years) and the Big Ring (1.3 billion) exceed the expected size limit of about 1.2 billion light-years (Lopez et al. 2022, 2024).
  - Against: once our motion is removed, the microwave background is the same in every direction to about 1 part in 100,000.
  - The observable universe is about 93 billion light-years across, 28 times the Giant Arc. A path wide enough to hold it would reach beyond what we can see, so only a direction could show.
- **The same shape as lightning.** Both are branching networks in which the flow keeps using paths already made.

**13. No dark matter: the pull from within.** The torus geometry, the Hopf picture and the share rule are in sections 4.6, 13.3 and 13.4. The observations:

- **No separate particle.** The most sensitive search found nothing, with a limit of 2.2 × 10⁻⁴⁸ cm² at 40 GeV (LZ Collaboration 2025). In the author's picture there is nothing separate to find.
- **The extra pull sits inside the matter.** Across 2,693 points in 153 galaxies, the full gravity at every radius is fixed by the visible matter there (McGaugh, Lelli and Schombert 2016):
  - with one acceleration scale, 1.20 × 10⁻¹⁰ m/s²;
  - with a scatter of 0.13 dex, mostly measurement error.
- **Torus flows at black holes.** At 86 GHz, M87's black hole shows a 64 μas ring of inflowing matter that joins straight onto the base of its jet (Lu et al. 2023): in around the middle, out along the poles.
- **The pull stays with the black holes.** In the Bullet Cluster collision the lensing pull stayed with the galaxies, where the black holes are, and left behind the hot gas that holds most of the ordinary mass (Clowe et al. 2006).
- **The hard test.** The microwave background, 380,000 years after the start, already needs about 5.36 ± 0.06 times as much extra pull as ordinary matter, from something that does not oscillate with the glowing gas (Planck Collaboration 2020). The author's picture must have the held flow in place from the start, and the share held at √4 must be stated before it is compared with 5.36.

**14. A pull next to the perpendicular, with spin.**

- **Measured around Earth.** Gravity Probe B's gyroscopes were dragged by Earth's spin at 37.2 ± 7.2 mas per year, against 39.2 predicted (Everitt et al. 2011). The LAGEOS orbits are dragged at 99% of the prediction, ±10% (Ciufolini and Pavlis 2004).
- **Measured in a star pair.** A white dwarf's spin drags the orbit of its pulsar partner, PSR J1141−6545 (Venkatraman Krishnan et al. 2020).
- **The disc on edge, at a black hole.** M87's jet swings round every 11 years, seen across 22 years of radio data: a tilted disc around the spinning black hole is dragged into a wobble (Cui et al. 2023).
- **The size.** In standard gravity this sideways pull is about (v/c)² of the ordinary pull, 5.4 × 10⁻⁷ for a galaxy's 220 km/s. Two analyses of 2023 found it about a million times too small to explain galaxy rotation (arXiv:2303.06115; arXiv:2303.16679). So spin adds the sideways, looping part, and the extra pull must still come from within.

**15. The fabric as a capacitive–inductive board.** The board's constants, base ohms and the near-field impedances are in section 5.1 and Table 24. The virtual-particle rows are in Table 26. The further checks:

- **Light on the board.** Light carries equal electric and magnetic energy, and E/H is the base ohms.
- **The T-bar flip on the fields.** (E, cB) → (cB, −E) swaps electric and magnetic energy, and twice gives −1. The empty-space Maxwell equations are unchanged by it: duality (Heaviside; Larmor).
- **A proton is a reaction.** The quark masses are 9.0 of 938.3 MeV, 0.96%. The lattice gets the nucleon at 936 ± 34 MeV from the field alone (Dürr et al. 2008).
- **Mass as an added reaction.** ω² = ω\_c² + c²k² gives a mass ħω\_c/c². Radio reflects off the ionosphere below its plasma cutoff, waveguides have a cutoff, and the plasmon led to the Higgs mechanism (Anderson 1963).
- **No wind.** A static cloud everywhere must look the same to every steady observer. It does, to Δc/c ≈ 10⁻¹⁷ (Herrmann et al. 2009), which the board meets only if ε₀ and μ₀ are the same in every frame.
- **The hum.** A hum of spacetime is seen in pulsar timing at 3.5 to 4σ (NANOGrav Collaboration 2023). A summed board hum would be 10¹²³ times too large, which is the problem section 13.1 addresses.
- **History.** Maxwell's vortex medium (1861); Michelson and Morley (1887); Einstein (1920), space with physical properties but no state of motion; Casimir (1948); Dirac (1951), “Is there an aether?”; Anderson (1963); Sakharov (1967), gravity as the vacuum's elasticity; Rañada (1989), light with linked field lines; Wilczek (2008), “the Grid”.

**16. Base ohms, semiconductive before action.** In the difference table the gap is the space between the two branches of E² = (pc)² + (mc²)², 2mc² wide. Inside it the momentum would be imaginary, so a reaction is held and the board only polarises. Table 73 reads the board as a semiconductor.

**Table 73. The board as a semiconductor.**

| Semiconductor feature | The board | Number | Observed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| band gap | 2mc² for each kind of reaction | electron 1.0220 MeV (9.1 × 10⁵ times silicon's 1.12 eV); muon 211.32 MeV; proton 1,876.54 MeV | pairs from photons above 1.022 MeV; the first positrons (Anderson 1933) |
| below the gap, polarisation only | vacuum polarisation | −27 MHz of hydrogen's 1,057.8 MHz Lamb shift (Uehling 1935) | measured |
| recombination glow | a pair falls back across the gap as two photons | 2 × 510.999 keV | the Galaxy's 511 keV line; PET scanners |
| exciton | positronium | binding 6.8028 eV; 1S–2S at the hydrogen-like level, 1,233.691 THz | 1,233.607 THz (Fee et al. 1993): the sub-reactions close the last 0.0068% |
| breakdown, as Zener breakdown in a diode | rate ∝ exp(−πE\_c/E) | E\_c = 1.323 × 10¹⁸ V/m; the strongest laser reaches 6.9 × 10⁻⁴ of it, a rate ∝ 10⁻¹⁹⁸³ | no spontaneous breakdown; pairs from light plus high-energy photons (Burke et al. 1997) |
| intrinsic carriers | pairs fill the board when kT ≈ mc² | 5.93 × 10⁹ K; freeze-out leaves neutrinos cooler by (4/11)^⅓ = 0.71377, 1.945 K | N\_eff = 2.99 ± 0.17 against 3.044 predicted (Planck Collaboration 2020) |
| today | a perfect insulator to steady current | the pair factor at 2.7 K is about e^(−2.2 × 10⁹) | the board carries waves at base ohms and nothing else |

The laser figure uses the record intensity of 1.1 × 10²³ W/cm² (Yoon et al. 2021). The history is Dirac (1930), Sauter (1931), Zener (1934), Heisenberg and Euler (1936) and Schwinger (1951): standard physics in the author's language, not a new number.

**17. Gravity on a gyroscope.** The geodetic and frame-dragging derivations are in section 11.1. The further rows:

- **Perpendicular response.** A pull in y turns a spinning axis in x: precession at 90°, the i-turn, not falling.
- **A static cling in constant motion.** The same m appears in weight and in F = ma, and inertia resists a change of speed as an inductor resists a change of current. MICROSCOPE finds weight and inertia equal to (−1.5 ± 2.7) × 10⁻¹⁵ (Touboul et al. 2022).
- **Cling from the whole universe.** Sciama's inertia by induction needs GM/(Rc²) ≈ 1 (Sciama 1953), and a flat universe gives Ω/2 = 0.50. Local gyroscopes do not turn against the distant universe, to below 4.7 × 10⁻¹¹ of the expansion rate (Saadeh et al. 2016).
- **Held past the tipping point.** A sleeping top stays up while its spin exceeds 2√(mglI⊥)/I₃. For a 100 g toy top with its centre of mass 3 cm up, I⊥ = 4.0 × 10⁻⁵ and I₃ = 2.0 × 10⁻⁵ kg m², that is 108 rad/s, 1,036 rpm. Tops sleep, then wobble and fall as friction slows them.
- **History.** Foucault (1852); Lense and Thirring (1918); Schiff (1960); Sciama (1953); Everitt et al. (2011).

**18. The roots and the grids.** The ladder, the moves as roots of 1, the 16-state generation and the hypercharge root are in sections 4, 7.1 and 8.1. The observations:

- **Charge in thirds.** Charges 0, ⅓, ⅔ and 1 sit on the cube's corners (Harari 1979; Shupe 1979). Quark charges of ⅓ and ⅔ are seen in deep inelastic scattering, Drell–Yan and the R ratio.
- **Pairs in halves.** Weak isospin and spin are ±½, in the doublets (u, d) and (ν, e).
- **One generation balances.** All the balance sums vanish over the 16 states (ΣY, ΣY³, ΣQ, SU(2)²Y, SU(3)²Y), which is the anomaly cancellation the theory needs, and inside-out sends all 16 to Q → −Q (Georgi 1975; Fritzsch and Minkowski 1975).
- **The spin third.** Each leg carries ⅓ of the diagonal, and the quark spin share is ΔΣ = 0.330 ± 0.039 (HERMES Collaboration 2007), −0.09σ. It is consistent but not selective: 79 members of the alphabet sit within 2σ of it (section 17.7.4).
- **The ¼ grid's fourth slot.** Reading the lepton as the fourth colour (Pati and Salam 1974) is a reading, not a test.
- **The weak phase is one-handed** (Wu et al. 1957). This is where inside-out (−1) and the 120° turn (ω) differ: ω is a rotation, while −1 in 3D is a mirror.

#### 16.4.4 What it means, and what would move the open items

**What it means.**

- **Motion.** The author's descriptions of motion hold up. Phases, legs and hypotenuse, rotation, time, gravity and the flip all describe what nature does. Several observed numbers land on the root ladder:
  - the neutral's ceiling is √3;
  - single-phase power and the ball pulse at 2f;
  - light bends twice as much as matter.
- **Gravity, air and lightning.** The chain holds end to end: gravity builds the air's pressure profile, the pressure sets the discharge's size, and the result matches real sprites.
- **Filaments.** The filament pathways fit, from lightning to the cosmic web. Whether the whole observable universe sits in one path is open.
- **No dark matter.** It fits four observations. The microwave background is the hard test.
- **Balance.** Balance fits the Higgs vacuum; its size is the δ₃ lead.

**Tests that would move the open items.**

- **Balance (10).** The e⁺e⁻ top threshold scan, about ±0.035 GeV. A pole mass above 170.94 GeV keeps the vacuum just past the edge; one at or below it breaks the rule.
- **The whole-observable path (12).** The matter dipole from Euclid, Rubin and the SKA. If the excess holds and lines up with the giant structures, sitting inside a larger flow becomes a live explanation; if it shrinks to what our motion explains, the idea fades.
- **The extra gravity (13).** State the share held at √4 against the share acting on √2, and compare it with 5.36. Then check the microwave background's peaks. A second check: galaxies whose black holes have passed more matter should carry more pull from within at the same visible mass.

**Standing.**

- **M.** Every measurement and derivation here is standard, with its source given.
- **N.** The descriptions are the author's, as are the readings that join them.
- **D.** The no-dark-matter picture diverges from the particle reading of the extra pull. It is scored open, not confirmed.

### 16.5 Borrowed observations

Papers written for other purposes were read whole, not by their abstracts, for measurements that land on the calculator's numbers. Three add detail not given elsewhere.

**The proton's spin, piece by piece.** The continuum-limit lattice decomposition of Alexandrou et al. (2026) used four ensembles with physical up, down, strange and charm masses at four lattice spacings, with all renormalisation, including the quark-singlet–gluon mixing, done non-perturbatively. Their Table II (MS-bar, 2 GeV) gives:

- ½ΔΣ = 0.190(22), J\_g = 0.209(26)(66) and L\_q = 0.108(37)(24);
- the sums J\_N = 0.507(43)(65) and ⟨x⟩\_N = 0.995(60)(29), so both sum rules close.

Per flavour, the up quark carries +0.417 of spin and −0.177 of orbital, and the down quark −0.203 of spin and +0.228 of orbital. These cancel in J\_d, so the proton's spin is mostly up-quark spin plus gluon. The same group's earlier single-ensemble result (Alexandrou et al. 2020: ½ΔΣ = 0.191(15), J\_g = 0.187(46), L\_q = 0.094(51)) is reproduced. ΔG = 0.231(17)(44) at 10 GeV² in the continuum limit is 46(9)% of the spin, and “clearly rules out a negative gluon helicity” (Zhao et al. 2025). Table 74 sets the thirds against the continuum lattice.

**Table 74. The thirds against the continuum lattice.**

| Piece | The calculator | Lattice | Pull | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

Folded to one resolution, the orbital sixth is the closest of the three. As a zeroth-order split the thirds survive. As a prediction, the lattice central values say spin-heavy, and the three errors are not independent, since the orbital piece is what is left. The full paper also gives the gluon's share of the momentum as a third within errors, a thirds statement the calculator never made, sitting beside the one it did.

| Measurement | Method | Value (MeV) | Pull |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Belle II (2023) | pseudomass endpoint, 175 million pairs | 1,777.09 ± 0.08 ± 0.11 | +0.9σ |
| BESIII (2014) | threshold scan | 1,776.91 ± 0.12 +0.10/−0.13 | −0.4σ |

The prediction sits between the two precise methods. A BESIII scan at the 2026 energy-calibration level, or Belle II's full sample, decides at ±0.05 MeV.

**α from B → ρρ.** Belle II measured on 387 million Υ(4S) decays (Belle II Collaboration 2025):

- B(B⁰ → ρ⁺ρ⁻) = (2.89 +0.23/−0.22 +0.29/−0.27) × 10⁻⁵;
- the longitudinal fraction f\_L = 0.921 +0.024/−0.025 +0.017/−0.015;
- S = −0.26 ± 0.19 ± 0.08 and C = −0.02 ± 0.12 +0.06/−0.05.

The isospin analysis of this result alone gives 91.5 +4.8/−5.2°. Combined with all other B → ρρ results it gives 92.6 +4.5/−4.7°, with the second solution, at 177.4°, excluded by the other angles and unitarity. The ρρ channel has the smallest penguin contamination of the three, which makes it the cleanest reading of α. The all-channel average, 84.1 +3.7/−3.0° (PDG 2026), is the one that sits 1.6σ low.

**What the borrowed observations change.**

- **The sharpest new fact is cosmological, and it cuts.** DESI's neutrino analysis puts two of the calculator's commitments, w = −1 and the floor neutrino mass, on one scale and finds them 3σ from the data together. The same 3σ shows as the drift in w and the open curvature: one discrepancy with three faces (section 16.3).
- **The thirds gain a witness.** The continuum lattice gives all three sixths at once, each within 1.4σ once the orbital piece is folded to one resolution.
- **α = 90° gains its cleanest channel.** B → ρρ alone says 92.6°; only the all-channel average says 84°.

## 17. How strong the evidence is: tests for chance, applied to both theories

The layout works wherever it reaches (sections 15 and 16). The best fit of a large family would also work, so this section asks how strong that agreement is as evidence that the layout is reality's own. Five tests whose rules were fixed before they were run, applied on 10 October 2026 to the calculator and the standard theories alike, find the calculator:

- **exact** where it restates established law;
- **consistent but not discriminating** where it reads physics: its readings change no measured number;
- **supported by its layout, not by its matches,** where it supplies numbers: a loose match to a measured constant is worth nothing at the alphabet's density, so the paper counts none; the layout's own numbers carry 5.1 bits against the chance that families of their size match random values as well (1.9 floored), and 0.6 to 4.0 against the whole alphabet (sections 15.4 and 17.6), with the lepton balance it explains at 9.7.

The frozen predictions decide whether that evidence grows. The same tests find the standard theories' predictions of independent measurements confirmed far beyond chance, 102 bits (section 17.8). They also find six failures, among them the vacuum's energy and the Hubble constant, which under the same rule reject the standard theories' claim to be complete (section 17.9).

### 17.1 The question, made testable

“Is this how reality works?” splits into four claims:

- **A, the encoding.** The calculator's corner values and turns satisfy established physics exactly.
- **B, the readings.** Its physical readings describe nature beyond restating standard physics.
- **C, the numbers.** The numbers its layout commits to are reality's own, and not only the best fit the cube's sets allow.
- **D, the predictions.** Its frozen numbers will be borne out. This one is testable only later.

The tests of this section serve claim C in two steps. The calibration of sections 17.3 to 17.5 measures what a match to a measured constant is worth at the alphabet's density, and finds that a loose match is worth nothing; that finding is applied to the calculator's own matches first, and every loose one is discounted. The information test of section 17.6 and the look-elsewhere test of section 17.7 then weigh what remains, and section 15.4 weighs the layout within its own sets. The standard theories face the same four claims, stated for them in section 17.9.

**The alphabet.** The alphabet was fixed on 3 October 2026, before any test. It has 37 base numbers:

- the levels and grids: 1, √2, √3, 2, 1/√2, 1/√3, ½, ⅓, ¼, ⅔, ¾, 3/2, √3/√2 and √3/2;
- the shares: π/4, π/6 and π²/32, their complements 1 − π/4, 1 − π/6 and 1 − π²/32, and 4/π, 2π/3, 1/(2π) and 1/(8π²);
- the golden numbers φ, 1/φ and √5;
- 3/8 and 5/3;
- the counts 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16, 24 and 27.

With every product and quotient of two, merged at 10⁻⁹, it holds 998 numbers from 0.00016 to 2,132.

**The outside list.** It is every dimensionless parameter of five standard sets, 29 in all:

- the electroweak masses over v, α\_s and 1/α;
- the lepton and light-quark mass ratios;
- the CKM and PMNS parameters;
- the nucleon's ratios;
- Planck's ΛCDM numbers.

The values are those of CODATA 2022, NuFIT 6.0 and 6.1, Planck 2018 and PDG 2026.

**The nulls and the scripts.**

- **Hit.** A hit is an alphabet member within 2σ of a target.
- **Primary null.** Alphabets built by the same recipe, with π, √2, √3 and φ replaced by random numbers of similar size.
- **Scripts.** blindtest.py and decide.py, with their hashes in section 21.
- **Decision rule.** The author's Quad-Core test (section 2.3).

### 17.2 Test 1: the identities, and where the calculator is exact

Every identity holds:

- **The turns.** J is unique, J⁴ = 1, det J = +1, and all 16 corners and 32 edges are kept. J carries the Front string onto the Back string. K² = (JK)² = −1, JK = −KJ and TJT = J³.
- **The electron's corners.** qv = φi = mₑc², qφ = h, v/i = φ/q = h/e², and i·πƛ² = μ\_B to 30 digits.
- **The board.** Z₀/(h/e²) = 2α, rₑa₀ = ƛ², Cₑ = 4πε₀rₑ and Lₑ = πμ₀a₀.

**Verdict:** the encoding is exact. That makes the calculator a correct notation for established relations: circuit laws, the Planck–Einstein relation, the flux and resistance quanta, the Standard Model's charges. It does not make it new physics, because each identity is a known law written on a cube.

**Where the calculator is exact.** The statements in Table 77 are identities or proved statements. They are what the calculator runs on, and the verdict above applies to them: they make it a correct notation for established relations, not new physics.

- **Hits by numbers it never chose.** Today's sin²θ₁₂ = 0.3096 lies nearer 1/(2φ) = 0.3090 than the calculator's 0.318, and Planck's Ωₘ = 0.3153 lies nearer 1/√10 = 0.3162 than π²/32.

**Verdict:** on an outside list the alphabet lands on measured numbers as often as alphabets made from random ingredients: a loose match is worth nothing at this density, and none is counted as evidence.

The alphabet hits 1 of the 11. Alphabets of the same recipe with random ingredients average 1.83, and all 400 tried hit at least one. Two facts explain the gap:

- **The shared hit.** The one hit is the integer 4, which every alphabet of the recipe contains, so every random alphabet hits rₚ/ƛₚ too.
- **The hits it lacks.** 43% of random alphabets also hit m\_H/v and 32% hit g\_A. The cube's alphabet hits neither.

Good's judgement of 1990 is the historical parallel: of eight formulas for mₚ/mₑ he found only Lenz's 6π⁵ worth anything, and it now sits 3 × 10⁵ standard deviations off.

**Verdict:** on the constants physics knows best, the calculator's alphabet does worse than the average alphabet of random ingredients: its reach is not evidence, and no match from that reach is counted.

### 17.5 Test 4: with the load rule

**The rule.** The calculator's own rule moves a static value by its load: k × αᵢ/2π for up to three phases, of either sign, of each coupling:

- α/2π = 0.001161;
- α₂/2π = 0.005380;
- α\_s/2π = 0.018780.

**The result.** The loaded alphabet holds 18,962 numbers and hits 3 of the 11 precise constants: m\_H/v at +0.3σ, g\_A at +0.1σ and rₚ/ƛₚ at −0.8σ. Random alphabets given the same loads hit 4.30 ± 0.73, and 99.8% of them hit at least 3.

**Verdict:** the load rule adds reach, not discrimination; any alphabet loaded this way reaches as far or farther, so matches reached through it are not counted either.

The chance probability of each committed match comes from the look-elsewhere test of 5 October 2026 (section 17.7.4), converted to bits as −log₂ p and combined by Fisher's method (Fisher 1932). Table 80 gives each match.

For scale, p = 0.01 is 6.6 bits, a factor of 100 and Jeffreys's grade “decisive”; 3σ is 8.5 bits and 5σ is 20.7 bits, both two-sided.

**What the test gives.** Against the whole alphabet the calculator's own numbers carry 0.6 to 4.0 bits, and the lepton balance 9.7. The alphabet is the harsher yardstick, because it holds members the layout never uses; counted within the cube's own sets (section 15.4) the own numbers carry 5.1 bits as observed and 1.9 floored. The strongest single piece of evidence in the lepton sector is the relation Koide found, which the calculator explains as balance but did not find.

### 17.7 The sweep of the alphabet, and the look-elsewhere test

Two searches were run before the five tests of sections 17.2 to 17.6:

- **The sweep (3 October 2026).** It set every number of the cube's alphabet against about 60 measured dimensionless numbers from ten fields, most of which no standard theory derives.
- **The look-elsewhere test (5 October 2026).** It asked how often the alphabet would match the committed numbers this well by accident.

Their rules were fixed before looking, and both are rerun here with PDG 2026 and NuFIT 6.1. The alphabet is the one of section 17.1. The sweep's own count was 971; rebuilt with near-duplicates merged at 10⁻⁹ it holds 998 numbers, and the densities below use the rebuild.

#### 17.7.1 The rules

- **The targets.** About 60 measured dimensionless numbers:
  - mass ratios, mixing angles, cosmic shares and couplings;
  - nuclear and atomic ratios and critical exponents;
  - fluid constants, scaling exponents and bond angles;
  - the spin-½ period.
- **A nail** is within 1σ of the measurement, with no other alphabet member within 3σ, and with a reason for that number in the calculator, not just membership of the alphabet.
- **A break** is a reading the rules naturally give, excluded by the data at 5σ or more.
- **Not selective** means several alphabet members fit within 2σ: the measurement cannot tell them apart, and neither can the calculator.
- **No number** means nothing in the alphabet within 3σ, and no reading.

**The score.**

- one nail, a single fact with three faces (section 9.1);
- two leads with a reason: the tilted Clarke frame for lepton mixing (section 10.1), and, thinner, the proton's radius (section 12.1);
- eleven breaks;
- twelve not selective;
- two with no number;
- one check the same as standard physics;
- one prediction.

Eighteen operations were checked as well, fourteen of them repeating what standard theory derives.

#### 17.7.2 Why the fits that work, work

A fit has a reason when it is one equation, not one number. The equation behind every exact fit in this paper is the same: two parts carry equal shares. Standard physics calls it the virial theorem, equipartition or resonance. Where that reason is present, a fit is a constraint and has to work. Where it is absent, a match is a coincidence until a reason is found. Table 81 lists the equation behind each fit.

**Table 81. The equal-share equation behind each fit.**

| Fit | The equal-share equation | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- |

| three-phase power is flat | three equal phases 120° apart sum to zero, 1 + ω + ω² = 0 | same as engineering |

#### 17.7.4 The look-elsewhere test

For each committed number the test took three measures, given in Table 83:

- the alphabet's local density, as members per unit value within ±25% of the measurement;
- the count of members within 2σ;
- the chance that a random true value in that neighbourhood would have a member as close as the observed pull: density × 2|pull|σ, checked by Monte Carlo with 20,000 draws.

**Table 83. How often the alphabet would match this well by accident.**

| Committed number | Pull | Members within 2σ | Chance of an accident this close | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Koide's Q = ⅔ | −0.4σ | 1 | 1 in 850 | selective; inherited (Koide 1982) |
| the lepton phase 2/9 | +0.4σ | 1 | 1 in 300 | not independent of Q = ⅔, and not exact beside it (section 15.3); not combined |
| rₚ/ƛₚ = 4 | −0.8σ | 1 | 1 in 6 (1 in 2.3 for a 2σ window) | weakly selective; prior art |
| the dark share 1 − π²/32 | −0.9σ | 9 | about 1 | not selective |
| the quark spin share ⅓ | −0.1σ | 79 | about 1 | not selective |
| the gluon share ⅙ | +1.4σ | 115 | about 1 | not selective; the prediction is pending (the Electron-Ion Collider) |
| Ω\_c/Ω\_b = 16/3 | +0.5σ | 5 | 0.8 | noted |
| a₀/cH₀ = 3/16 | −1.4σ | 8 | about 1 | noted |
| nₛ = 26/27 | +0.5σ | 4 | 0.55 to 0.75 | noted |
| H₀t₀ = 2(1 − π/6) | −0.5σ | 2 | 0.6 to 0.9 | noted |
| the Tully–Fisher slope 4 | −1.7σ | 11 | about 1 | noted |

**The 15° tilt.** The measured tilt is 14.89° ± 0.19°. There are two definitions of “the cube's angles”:

- **Strict.** The angles between the 26 cube directions (35.26°, 45°, 54.74°, 60°, 70.53°, 90°), the ladder's step angles arctan(1/√k) (45°, 35.26°, 30°, 26.57°), and one sum or difference of two of them: 40 angles. 15° is the only one within 3σ. The density is 0.47 per degree between 5° and 45°, so the chance of an accident is 0.12.
- **Generous.** The arctan, arcsin or 90° × any alphabet member up to 1: 1,422 angles, 32 of them within 3σ. Not selective.

So “the only cube angle within 3σ” holds under the strict definition only.

**The hum.** The three crossing scales give v²/M = 5.5 eV, 0.36 eV and 2.0 meV, against 2.24 meV.

- With the coefficient fixed at 1 beforehand, the chance that one of three candidates spread over five decades lands within ±10% is 0.052.
- With an order-one coefficient allowed, within a factor of 3, it is 0.57.

**The top and the Higgs.** There is one formula with a discrete load count. Two constructions land within 0.4σ, and the Higgs follows from the top along the edge, so the two hits are one. With about three constructions, a top sensitivity of about 1.7 GeV per 1% of load, a plausible load range of 0 to 3%, and a hit window of ±0.5 GeV, the chance is about 0.6. This estimate is rough.

**Combined.** Fisher's method (Fisher 1932) gives the results of Table 80:

- the inherited lepton relation at 1 in 850, from Q = ⅔ alone: the phase tests the same measurement and is not combined with it;
- the calculator's own numbers at 0.064 under the strict rules and 0.68 under the generous ones.

**What it means.**

- **The strong evidence is inherited.** The strong numerical evidence in the paper is the lepton sector, and it is Koide's. The calculator inherits it and supplies a reason, which carries no number of its own.
- **The calculator's own content.** Today it lies between a 1-in-15 surprise and none, depending on how much freedom its rules are read as having. That is what the pre-registration is for: the calculator's own evidence has to come from the pending measurements of Table 87, each written down first.
- **Not counted either way.** The gravity tests, w = −1, Ω\_k = 0 and the return fraction are the same as the standard theories. The neutrino sum cannot be told from the floor.

### 17.8 The same tests, applied to the standard theories

The standard theories pass the first test by construction and stand outside the second: their equations are the laws the calculator's identities restate, and the outside list is mostly their own inputs. On the tests that measure evidence, the third and the fifth, their predictions of independent measurements beat chance by a wide margin. They also carry failures that the calculator does not share. Table 85 sets the two side by side, test by test.

**Test 1, the identities.** The standard theories' equations are the laws the identities restate: Maxwell's for the board, Einstein's for the two tables, the Standard Model's charges for the register. They pass by construction and, as for the calculator, passing says nothing about new physics.

**Test 2, the outside list.** Most of the 29 targets are inputs of the Standard Model or ΛCDM: masses, mixings, couplings and densities, fitted to the very measurements the list holds. A fitted number cannot miss, so the standard theories score neither hit nor miss here. Where they compute a target rather than take it, they compute it with a stated error and agree within it: the W mass from the other inputs, and the nucleon's radius and axial coupling on the lattice (Table 55).

**Test 3, the precise constants.** Of the eleven constants known better than 0.1%, five are inputs. The electroweak theory computes m\_W/m\_Z from α, G\_F, m\_Z, m\_t and m\_H to 1.2 × 10⁻⁴, within 0.6σ (Table 84). The lattice computes the nucleon's mass to 4% (Dürr et al. 2008), its radius to 2% (Djukanovic et al. 2024) and its axial coupling to about 2% (Table 55): short of their measured precision, but within its own errors. Where the calculator hits one of the eleven, with an integer every alphabet contains, the standard theories predict at full precision the one precise ratio they do not take as input.

**Test 4, the load rule.** It has no counterpart. The standard theories have no alphabet and no rule applied quantity by quantity; each prediction is one computed number.

**Test 5, information.** Table 84 scores the standard theories' predictions of independent measurements on the convention of section 17.6, with one change that their form forces. A single computed number has no alphabet to search, so its chance probability is the width of its window, ±max(|pull|, 1)σ, against one decade of possible values. The floor of 1σ can only lower their score. Each number was computed from the theory and from other measurements, and none was fitted to the measurement it is compared with.

**Table 84. The standard theories' predictions of independent measurements, scored for chance.**

| Prediction | Theory | Predicted | Measured | Pull | Chance | Bits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| the speed of gravitational waves | general relativity (1916) | c | within −3 × 10⁻¹⁵ and +7 × 10⁻¹⁶ of c (LIGO Scientific and Virgo Collaborations 2017) | 0 | 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁵ | 49.1 |
| the electron's moment against α from rubidium | quantum electrodynamics | 1/α = 137.035999166(15), from g/2 (Fan et al. 2023) | 137.035999206(11) (Morel et al. 2020) | +2.2 | 2.5 × 10⁻¹⁰ | 31.9 |
| light bending, γ | general relativity (1915) | 1 | 1 + (2.1 ± 2.3) × 10⁻⁵ (Bertotti, Iess and Tortora 2003) | +0.9 | 2.0 × 10⁻⁵ | 15.6 |
| the W mass | the electroweak theory, from α, G\_F, m\_Z, m\_t and m\_H | 80,357 ± 6 MeV (CDF Collaboration 2022) | 80,362.5 ± 7.7 MeV without CDF (PDG 2026) | +0.6 | 1.1 × 10⁻⁴ | 13.2 |
| the binary pulsar's orbital decay | the quadrupole formula (Einstein 1918) | 1 | 0.9983 ± 0.0016 of it (Weisberg and Huang 2016) | −1.1 | 1.5 × 10⁻³ | 9.4 |
| geodetic precession | general relativity (de Sitter 1916) | 6,606.1 mas/yr | 6,601.8 ± 18.3 (Everitt et al. 2011) | −0.2 | 2.4 × 10⁻³ | 8.7 |
| helium-4, Yp | nucleosynthesis with the microwave background's baryon density | 0.24691 ± 0.00018 | 0.2449 ± 0.0040 (Fields et al. 2020) | −0.5 | 0.014 | 6.1 |
| deuterium, D/H | the same | (2.57 ± 0.13) × 10⁻⁵ | (2.55 ± 0.03) × 10⁻⁵ (Fields et al. 2020) | −0.2 | 0.045 | 4.5 |
| N\_eff | three neutrinos in standard cosmology | 3.044 | 2.86 ± 0.13 (ACT Collaboration 2025) | −1.4 | 0.053 | 4.2 |
| frame dragging | general relativity (Lense and Thirring 1918) | 39.2 mas/yr | 37.2 ± 7.2 (Everitt et al. 2011) | −0.3 | 0.16 | 2.6 |
| the Higgs mass | the electroweak fit before the discovery | 91 +30/−23 GeV (Baak et al. 2012) | 125.13 ± 0.11 GeV (PDG 2026) | +1.1 | 0.29 | 1.8 |
| **combined, Fisher's method** |  |  |  |  | **1.8 × 10⁻³¹** | **102** |

Combined by Fisher's method, the eleven give p = 1.8 × 10⁻³¹, 102 bits. A narrower prior, ±10% instead of a decade, lowers each entry by 3.5 bits and the combination to 70 bits. Against caesium's α the electron's moment sits at −3.9σ instead, but the two atomic values disagree with each other at 5.5σ, and only the nearer is counted. The muon's moment is not counted: its 2025 prediction agrees at +0.6σ only after lattice input replaced the e⁺e⁻ data, with which the 2020 prediction sat about 5σ from the measurement (Muon g−2 Theory Initiative 2025). Figure 3 sets each prediction beside the calculator's own numbers and the lepton relations it inherits.

&#91;embedded content: Figure 3 · evidence against chance, in bits: the standard theories’ eleven predictions (Table 84), the lepton relation the calculator inherits and its own numbers (Table 80)\]

**Failures.** The rule of section 2.5 counts the standard theories' failures the same way as the calculator's:

- the vacuum's energy, about 10¹²⁰ too large (Weinberg 1989);
- lithium-7 from nucleosynthesis, (4.72 ± 0.72) × 10⁻¹⁰ predicted against (1.6 ± 0.3) × 10⁻¹⁰, 4.0σ (Fields et al. 2020);
- the neutrino masses, zero in the minimal Standard Model and contradicted by oscillations (Fukuda et al. 1998);
- the Hubble constant from the microwave background in ΛCDM, 4.9 to 7.1σ from the ladders (Riess et al. 2022; H0DN Collaboration 2025; Freedman et al. 2025);
- first-row unitarity of the quark mixing matrix, 2.3σ short (PDG 2026);
- lepton universality in R(D) and R(D\*), 3.7σ (HFLAV 2026).

The calculator shares one of these, the Hubble gap, which it inherits when its share is combined with the microwave fit (section 22.4). It is silent on three, and gives a number for two (the hum, +1.1σ; the neutrino masses, inside the cap). Its own failures are of another kind. Among its committed numbers none misses at 2σ, but eleven readings its rules naturally give are excluded by the data (section 17.7.1), and the permutation search withdraws three readings it carried (section 15.3). The standard theories made their failed predictions; the calculator did not commit to its breaks and kept only what survived them, which is why its survivors are weighed against chance in Table 80.

**Table 85. The five tests, applied to both.**

| Test | The calculator | The standard theories |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. the identities | exact: a correct notation for established law | exact by construction: they are that law |
| 2. calibration on an outside list | 19 of 29 hit, as often as random alphabets (P = 0.79) | the targets are mostly their inputs; those they compute agree within stated errors |
| 3. the precise constants | 1 of 11 hit, below random alphabets' 1.83 | m\_W/m\_Z at 1.2 × 10⁻⁴, +0.6σ; the rest inputs, or lattice values to 2 to 4% |
| 4. the load rule | adds reach, not discrimination (P = 0.998) | no counterpart |
| 5. information | its own numbers 0.6 to 4.0 bits, 1.9 to 5.1 counted within the cube's own sets (section 15.4); the lepton relation it inherits 9.7 bits | eleven predictions, 102 bits |
| failures | none among committed numbers; the Hubble gap, inherited through the microwave fit; eleven breaks among readings it did not commit to, and three readings withdrawn by the permutation search | six, among them the vacuum's energy and the Hubble constant |

### 17.9 The verdict through the Quad-Core, for both

Table 86 puts each of the calculator's four claims, and the four matching claims of the standard theories, through the author's four cores: outcome, constraint, alternative and consequence.

**Table 86. The four claims under the author's four cores.**

| Claim | Outcome | Constraint | Alternative | Consequence | State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A. Its identities are exact | not rejected: all hold to 30 digits; every electron corner meets a measurement | not rejected: they hold at every scale and frequency, being laws | not rejected: equivalent notations, bond graphs and phasors, do no better | not rejected: an error would show at once | **STABILIZED** |
| B. Its readings describe nature beyond standard physics | undetermined: they change no measured number | undetermined: carried into classical field theory the electron's half becomes ħ/3, a break if the reading is dynamical and none if it is bookkeeping | not rejected: standard physics gives the same numbers, so neither has fewer violations | undetermined: if wrong, nothing measurable changes, so the error is deferred | **UNSTABLE** |
| C. The numbers its layout commits to are reality's own, not only the best fit its sets allow | not rejected: all 22 within 1.6σ of their primary measurements; the one precise constant the layout commits to, rₚ, hit at −0.8σ; nothing downstream of any of them broken | undetermined: at the precision of the best constants the layout commits to one number and hits it; the alphabet's loose reach, 1 of 11 against a random mean of 1.83, is discounted and not counted (sections 17.4 and 17.6) | undetermined: no arrangement inside the cube's sets does better on data it was not chosen with (section 15.4); families of that size match random values as well with p = 0.030, and alphabets of this density match loose outside constants as often as the calculator's (P = 0.79) | not rejected: every consequence that can be checked holds, and each prediction has a decider and a date | **UNSTABLE** |
| D. Its frozen predictions will be borne out | undetermined: the data are not in | not rejected: each definition and scale is fixed, two of them by the author's behavior rule (section 18.1) | undetermined: today's data cannot yet separate its values from where the data sit | not rejected: each has a decider, a date and a refutation rule | **UNSTABLE** |
| A′. The standard theories' equations hold where tested | not rejected: the electron's moment to 0.13 parts per trillion, γ to 2 × 10⁻⁵, the binary pulsar to 0.2% | not rejected within the tested range of scale and energy | not rejected: no rival matches as many measurements with fewer violations | not rejected: an error would show in the next digit | **STABILIZED** |
| B′. Their structures describe nature beyond what was known when they were written | not rejected: the positron (Anderson 1933), gravitational waves (LIGO Scientific and Virgo Collaborations 2016) and the Higgs boson's couplings at their predicted strength (ATLAS Collaboration 2022) | not rejected | not rejected: no other account predicted them | not rejected | **STABILIZED** |
| C′. Their predictions of independent measurements beat chance | not rejected: 102 bits over eleven predictions (Table 84) | not rejected: they hold at precisions from tens of percent to parts in 10¹⁵ | not rejected: no random procedure matches eleven independent measurements at these precisions | not rejected | **STABILIZED** |
| D′. They are complete: they account for every measurement | **rejected**: the vacuum's energy misses by about 10¹²⁰, and the minimal theory's massless neutrinos are contradicted by oscillations | **rejected**: general relativity's singularities and the vacuum's energy break at the extremes of scale | undetermined: no complete rival exists, the calculator included | **rejected**: the dark sector and the Hubble tension defer the error rather than remove it | **REJECTED** |

The conjunction, that the calculator is how reality works, stabilises only if every claim does. Under the author's synthesis rule, with no core overriding another and no majority, claim A stabilises and claims B, C and D are unstable as of 11 October 2026: no core rejects them, and the cores that would decide them are undetermined until the frozen predictions report. The same rule rejects the claim that the standard theories are the whole of how reality works, because claim D′ is rejected, while it stabilises their claims to hold where tested, to describe nature beyond what was known, and to beat chance. For the calculator, stated plainly:

- **Notation.** Where it restates known law, the calculator is a correct notation for how reality works (A, stabilised).
- **Readings.** Its readings change no measured number, so observation cannot yet favour them (B, unstable).
- **Numbers.** Every number the layout commits to meets its measurement, nothing downstream of them breaks, and no arrangement of the cube's sets does better on data it was not chosen with. At the sets' density that agreement carries 5.1 bits: families of that size match random values as well about once in 33 draws, so the agreement is unlikely to be the sets' flexibility alone, and not yet beyond doubt (C, unstable).
- **Predictions.** What decides C is D.

For the standard theories: their equations, their structures and their numbers stand (A′, B′ and C′, stabilised), and their completeness does not (D′, rejected). What separates the two theories today is the weight of independent predictions already borne out: 102 bits over a century for the standard theories, against 5.1 for the calculator's layout and 9.7 for the lepton relation it explains. That is a difference of evidence, not a finding that the calculator's numbers are wrong: every one of them meets its measurement, and the predictions of section 18 are where its own evidence will come from.

## 18. Predictions: what decides both theories

The layout's own evidence has to come from measurements not yet made. Nine predictions are frozen with their definitions and a scoring rule, deposited unchanged before any of their data (section 18.1). If the calculator's numbers are right, the seven of its own give a median Bayes factor of 240, decisive with probability 0.68; if they are wrong, about 10⁻¹², decisive against with probability 0.92. JUNO's sin²θ₁₂ (about 2031), the angle α (about 2030) and the Electron-Ion Collider's ΔG (the 2030s) carry most of that power. Four measurements of the coming decade bear on both theories at once: w, the curvature and the neutrino mass sum test commitments the two share, and r separates them (section 18.5).

### 18.1 Nine frozen predictions

The predictions below are frozen with their definitions and are deposited, unchanged, before any of their data. Table 87 lists them, with today's value, the decider and what a pass would weigh.

**Table 87. The pre-registered predictions.** “If wrong” means each value lies where today's data put it.

| # | Prediction and definition | Value | Today | Pull | Decider and date | P(pass) if wrong | B if it passes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | PMNS sin²θ₁₂: the tribimaximal frame tilted 15° with its first column fixed | 0.318107 | 0.3096 +0.0057/−0.0073 | −1.5 | JUNO, six years, ±0.0016, about 2031 | 0.14 | 6.9 |
| 2 | PMNS sin²θ₁₃ = sin²15°/3 | 0.022329 | 0.02200 ± 0.00056 | −0.6 | global fits, ±0.0004 (assumed), about 2030 | 0.70 | 1.4 |
| 3 | CKM unitarity angle α = 90° | 90° | 84.1 +3.7/−3.0° | −1.6 | Belle II and LHCb, ±2°, about 2030 | 0.35 | 2.7 |
| 4 | Ωₘ = π²/32 in flat ΛCDM | 0.308425 | 0.3045 ± 0.0032 | −1.2 | Euclid and final DESI, ±0.003, 2027–30 | 0.68 | 1.4 |
| 5 | ΔG = ⅙ at μ = 2 GeV, MS-bar, the scale fixed by the behavior rule (0.236 at 10 GeV² at leading order) | 0.166667 | 0.158 ± 0.039 (lattice, evolved to 2 GeV) | −0.2 | Electron-Ion Collider, about 10%, evolved to 2 GeV, 2030s | 0.54 | 1.8 |
| 6 | ΔΣ = ⅓, MS-bar | 0.333333 | 0.352 ± 0.029 (HERMES and lattice combined) | +0.6 | Electron-Ion Collider, about 15%, 2030s | 0.90 | 1.1 |
| 7 | Top pole mass from the δ₃ load (MS-bar 162.76 ± 0.37 GeV, the same alignment) | 172.57 ± 0.38 GeV | 172.1 ± 0.6 (pole average) | −0.7 | FCC-ee threshold scan, ±0.035 GeV, 2040s | 0.67 | 1.4 |
| 8 | rₚ = 4ħ/(mₚc) (prior art) | 0.841236 fm | 0.84075 ± 0.00064 | −0.8 | hydrogen and muonic hydrogen, ±0.0002 fm (assumed), about 2030 | 0.35 | 2.7 |
| 9 | mτ from Koide's relation (prior art) | 1776.969 MeV | 1776.93 ± 0.09 | −0.4 | Belle II, BESIII, STCF, ±0.05 MeV (assumed) | 0.63 | 1.5 |

**How the definitions are fixed.** Two predictions can be stated in more than one alignment, and the behavior rule fixes both (rule 9 of section 4.1; step 8 of section 4.8). It was applied on 10 October 2026 to the data then published. Section 18.2 gives the scans, and section 18.3 applies the rule to the whole set.

- **The scale of ΔG: μ = 2 GeV.** Carried to 10 GeV², where the lattice gives the full value, the rule admits 1.50 to 3.04 GeV and is best met at 2.01 GeV. ⅙ at 10 GeV² misses the two lattice results combined by 2.2σ (1.4σ against Zhao et al. alone) and is not the best match, so it is left out. At the collider's 10 GeV², ⅙ at 2 GeV is 0.236 at leading order.
- **The top: the pole mass.** m\_t(m\_t) = 162.76 ± 0.37 GeV in MS-bar is the same alignment. Read as an unconverted MS-bar mass, 172.57 GeV would mean a pole mass of 182.9 GeV, 14.9σ from the pole data, so that reading is left out. The direct-mass reading misses nothing, but it differs from the pole reading by about 0.5 GeV, which only the threshold scan can resolve. The pole mass is kept because it is the mass the edge calculation takes, and row 7's value today is the pole average.

The lattice match that fixes ΔG's scale is the reason for the choice, not evidence for it; the deciders in Table 87 are the test.

**The scoring rule.**

- **The score.** A future result m ± σₘ on a prediction c ± σ\_c scores the Bayes factor B = N(m; c, √(σ\_c² + σₘ²))/p₀(m), where p₀ is today's value taken as a split normal and spread by σₘ.
- **The verdict.** The calculator's own seven are scored together by multiplying their B. A total above 100 is decisive for its numbers, below 1/100 decisive against, and anything between is open. The threshold of 100 is Jeffreys's grade “decisive” (Jeffreys 1961; Kass and Raftery 1995).
- **Refutation.** It is stated as a range. Each result's pull |m − c|/σ is taken on two yardsticks: σ = √(σ\_c² + σₘ²), with σ\_c the calculation's precision as frozen in Table 87, and σ = √(σ\_c′² + σₘ²), with σ\_c′ the same calculation at the input precision its decider brings. A pull above 5 on both refutes that prediction's rule, whatever the total; above 5 on the second alone, the rule is refuted at the decider's precision and stands at the frozen one; below 5 on both, it stands. Only the top carries a precision of its own: σ\_c = 0.38 GeV, and σ\_c′ = 0.15 GeV with α\_s at ±0.0002, so at the scan's ±0.035 GeV its line runs from 0.77 to 1.91 GeV. For the other eight σ\_c = σ\_c′ = 0, and both lines are 5σₘ.
- **Prior art.** The prior-art pair is scored separately.
- **What is not evidence:** matches to numbers known no better than about 0.1%, which the calibration shows the alphabet reaches by density; readings that reproduce standard physics; and anything fitted after the data.

**What the set can decide.**

- **If right.** If the calculator's numbers are right, the seven give a median B of 240, above 100 with probability 0.68.
- **If wrong.** If they are wrong, the median is about 10⁻¹², below 1/100 with probability 0.92.
- **The three that carry it.** JUNO's sin²θ₁₂, the angle α and ΔG carry most of the power; passing all three by chance has probability 0.026 (1 in 38).
- **The rest.** ΔΣ, Ωₘ and the top add little until their precision improves. The Electron-Ion Collider's projected 15% on ΔΣ (Aschenauer, Sassot and Stratmann 2015) is no better than the lattice today.

**Recorded, not scored.** Five numbers are recorded with the set and not scored, because standard physics shares them or because they wait on the calculator's own precision:

- Σmν = 59.2 meV with normal ordering;
- w = −1;
- Ω\_k = 0;
- r ≈ 0;
- the vacuum energy scale, 2.02 ± 0.20 meV against 2.24 meV.

Their deciders are in Table 94 (section 18.4).

**The deposit.** The predictions, their definitions, the scoring rule and the script hashes are written into one pre-registration file, deposited unchanged, before any of their data, in the author's OSF project *Tesseract Logic Calculator* (DOI [10.17605/OSF.IO/C9W7K](https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/C9W7K)), beside this paper, the scripts of Table 100 and the calculator's page, and mirrored in the repository [github.com/Tifinchi/Tesseract-Logic-Calculator](https://github.com/Tifinchi/Tesseract-Logic-Calculator). Its SHA-256 is 624b271c504c25191447350a1ffbb90ade7f6da2ee7cf752dd109588ec8305df, and the OSF timestamp of the file is the freeze.

### 18.2 The behavior rule applied to the two definitions

Two definitions in Table 87, the scale of ΔG (prediction 5) and the top-mass scheme (prediction 7), are fixed by the author's behavior rule (section 4.1, rule 9): an alignment is kept only if nothing it touches down-wind is missed. This subsection sets out every published observation under each alignment, including observations made for other purposes, and the scans that decide between them. Scripts: deltaG\_scale.py, top\_scheme.py, downstream\_dg.py, downstream\_top.py and choices\_power.py.

**The gluon spin and its scale.**

- **ΔG runs.** At leading order ΔΣ stays fixed while ΔG grows with the scale and the orbital part falls to match (Ji, Tang and Hoodbhoy 1996). So ½ΔΣ, ΔG and L can all equal ⅙ at one scale only, and the calculator's structure does not yet name that scale (Table 97), so the behavior rule fixes it.
- **Where the data put ⅙.** Evolving the lattice values with the leading-order equation of section 12.2 and four-loop α\_s (α\_s(m\_Z) = 0.1180):
  - from Zhao et al. (2025), ΔG = ⅙ at μ = 2.11 GeV, 1.69 to 2.79 GeV at 1σ;
  - from Yang et al. (2017), at 1.91 GeV, 1.55 to 2.49 GeV;
  - with HERMES's ΔΣ = 0.330 in place of the lattice's 0.380, both move down by 0.05 to 0.06 GeV.

  Both crossings lie at the lattice's conventional 2 GeV, where ½ΔΣ = 0.190 ± 0.022 and L = 0.152 ± 0.043 are quoted (section 12.2).

**Table 88. The gluon spin by scale.** The pull is (value − ⅙)/σ.

| Determination | Method | Scale | Value | Pull from ⅙ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Zhao et al. 2025 | lattice, continuum limit, MS-bar | μ² = 10 GeV² | ΔG = 0.231 ± 0.047 | +1.4σ |
| Yang et al. 2017 | lattice, MS-bar | μ² = 10 GeV² | ΔG = 0.251 ± 0.050 | +1.7σ |
| DSSV14 (de Florian et al. 2014), as quoted by Aschenauer et al. (2015) | global fit, x > 0.05 only | Q² = 10 GeV² | 0.20 +0.06/−0.07 at 90% C.L. | +0.8σ, truncated |
| NNPDF reweighted with STAR's 2009 jets, as quoted by Aschenauer et al. (2015) | global fit, 0.05 < x < 0.2 only | Q² = 10 GeV² | 0.17 ± 0.06 | +0.1σ, truncated |
| COMPASS Collaboration 2017 | high-p\_T hadrons, leading order | μ² = 3 GeV², x\_g ≈ 0.10 | Δg/g = 0.113 ± 0.052 | a local ratio, not ΔG |
| Zhao et al., evolved (section 12.2) | lattice with leading-order evolution | μ = 2 GeV | ΔG = 0.158 ± 0.039 | −0.2σ |

- **The sign is settled.** The sign is fixed by two kinds of data:
  - PHENIX's direct photons reach the gluon through quark–gluon scattering at 0.02 < x < 0.08 and disfavour a negative gluon spin by more than 2.8σ (PHENIX Collaboration 2023);
  - high-x DIS together with lattice data rules out the negative solutions of the global fits (Hunt-Smith et al. 2024).

  STAR's jets reach x ≈ 0.015 (STAR Collaboration 2019).
- **The full moment is not settled by experiment.** Below x ≈ 0.05 the fits cannot extrapolate:
  - an NNLO global analysis states that it cannot reliably determine the full first moments (Borsa et al. 2024);
  - inclusive and semi-inclusive DIS alone leave the polarised gluon largely unconstrained at NNLO (Bertone, Chiefa and Nocera 2025).

  The full ΔG therefore comes from the lattice today. In the 2030s it will come from the Electron-Ion Collider, whose projections are quoted at 10 GeV² (Aschenauer, Sassot and Stratmann 2015).

**The rule applied to ΔG.** Each row of Table 89 puts ΔG = ⅙ at one scale and carries it to 10 GeV², where the lattice gives the full value, with the leading-order evolution of section 12.2 and ΔΣ = 0.380 (downstream\_dg.py).

**Table 89. The behavior rule on the scale of ΔG.** Against the two lattice results combined as independent, 0.2405 ± 0.0342, and against Zhao et al. alone, 0.231 ± 0.047.

| ΔG = ⅙ at | ΔG at 10 GeV² | Against both | Against Zhao et al. | Under the rule |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1.2 GeV | 0.383 | +4.2σ | +3.2σ | left out |
| 1.73 GeV (3 GeV², the COMPASS scale) | 0.272 | +0.9σ | +0.9σ | admitted |
| **2 GeV, the lattice's scale** | **0.241** | **+0.0σ** | **+0.2σ** | **kept: the best match** |
| 2.5 GeV | 0.201 | −1.2σ | −0.6σ | admitted |
| 3.16 GeV (10 GeV², the earlier definition) | 0.167 | −2.2σ | −1.4σ | left out |
| 4 GeV | 0.138 | −3.0σ | −2.0σ | left out |

- **The window.** The rule admits 1.50 to 3.04 GeV within 2σ and 1.72 to 2.42 GeV within 1σ, and is best met at 2.01 GeV. With Zhao et al.'s value alone, the 1σ window is 1.69 to 2.79 GeV, centred at 2.11 GeV.
- **The alignment kept.** ΔG = ⅙ at μ = 2 GeV, where the lattice quotes the other two sixths, so all three are compared at one resolution. With the thirds exact (ΔΣ = ⅓), its value at the collider's 10 GeV² is 0.236.
- **What it costs.** The lattice match is the reason for the choice, not evidence for it. The Electron-Ion Collider remains the test: the alignment fails if the collider's full moment, evolved to 2 GeV, lands away from ⅙. Because the alignment predicts what the lattice already shows, a pass adds less: ΔG's chance of passing if the calculator is wrong is 0.54 (Table 87), against 0.34 under the earlier definition (Table 92).

**The top mass and its scheme.**

- **The mass the calculation uses.** The edge is computed with the pole mass as the input to the matching (Buttazzo et al. 2013), and δ₃ multiplies that edge.
- **MS-bar.** The pole and MS-bar masses differ by a nearly fixed factor at these masses, 1.0603 at four loops (Marquard et al. 2015, the conversion the PDG uses). So the rule is one prediction in both schemes: 172.57 ± 0.38 GeV pole is m\_t(m\_t) = 162.76 ± 0.37 GeV MS-bar, and the edge is 161.21 ± 0.36 GeV.
- **The conversion's own limit.** The four-loop term alone is 0.19 GeV. The terms beyond add about 0.3 GeV, with an intrinsic renormalon ambiguity of about 110 MeV in the pole mass itself (Beneke et al. 2017). A deposited MS-bar value should therefore be computed from the matching directly, not converted from the pole value.
- **The direct mass is a third quantity.** It is the simulation's mass parameter:
  - its difference from the pole mass is of order 0.5 GeV (PDG 2026);
  - the PDG's electroweak fit carries that difference as 0 ± 0.52 GeV;
  - one calibration, in e⁺e⁻ event shapes, puts the pole mass 0.57 ± 0.28 GeV below the Pythia parameter, and the parameter within uncertainties of the MSR mass at 1 GeV (Butenschoen et al. 2016).

**Table 90. The top mass by scheme, against the rule (172.57 ± 0.38 GeV) and the exact edge (170.94 ± 0.38 GeV).** An asymmetric error is taken on the side that faces the prediction.

| Determination | Scheme | Value (GeV) | Against the rule | Against the edge |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PDG 2026, average of pole determinations | pole | 172.1 ± 0.6 | −0.7σ | +1.6σ |
| PDG 2026, direct average with the difference carried as 0 ± 0.52 GeV | pole-equivalent | 172.60 ± 0.59 | +0.0σ | +2.4σ |
| the direct average with the calibration of Butenschoen et al. (2016) | pole-equivalent | 172.03 ± 0.39 | −1.0σ | +2.0σ |
| CMS, normalised multi-differential cross sections, 13 TeV (CMS Collaboration 2020) | pole | 170.5 ± 0.8 | −2.3σ | −0.5σ |
| CMS, tt̄ + jet, 13 TeV (CMS Collaboration 2023) | pole | 172.93 ± 1.36 | +0.3σ | +1.4σ |
| ATLAS, tt̄ + 1 jet, 13 TeV (ATLAS Collaboration 2025) | pole | 170.7 ± 1.5 | −1.2σ | −0.2σ |
| ATLAS and CMS, inclusive cross sections, 7 and 8 TeV (ATLAS and CMS Collaborations 2023) | pole | 173.4 +1.8/−2.0 | +0.4σ | +1.2σ |
| electroweak fit, indirect (PDG 2026) | pole | 175.2 ± 1.8 | +1.4σ | +2.3σ |

- **Observations made for other purposes.** Two carry top-mass information without being measurements of it:
  - **The electroweak fit.** Built from Z-pole data and the W mass, it gives an indirect pole mass of 175.2 ± 1.8 GeV (PDG 2026), 1.4σ above the rule and 2.3σ above the edge.
  - **Toponium.** CMS set the ηt mass at 343 GeV, twice the top mass less a binding of about 2 GeV, with m\_t fixed at 172.5 GeV and varied by ±1 GeV as a nuisance. With a resolution in m(tt̄) of about 15% at threshold, the peak's position is not used to extract the mass (CMS Collaboration 2025). Its position carries the mass in a threshold scheme, which is what the decider will measure.
- **The decider.** An e⁺e⁻ threshold scan measures the potential-subtracted mass with a statistical error of 4.3 MeV, an experimental error of 6.8 MeV and a theory error of about 35 MeV. Converting it to MS-bar adds 7 to 23 MeV (Defranchis et al. 2025). Converting it to the pole mass inherits the pole mass's ambiguity of about 110 MeV.

**The rule applied to the top.** Each row of Table 91 reads the rule's 172.57 ± 0.38 GeV as one kind of mass and carries it to the pole-type observables (downstream\_top.py). Every reading puts the pole mass above the edge, 170.94 GeV, on the metastable side the balance rule requires.

**Table 91. The behavior rule on the top's alignment.**

| 172.57 GeV read as | Pole mass implied (GeV) | Against the pole average, 172.1 ± 0.6 | Against the electroweak fit, 175.2 ± 1.8 | Under the rule |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **the pole mass (MS-bar 162.76, the same alignment)** | **172.57 ± 0.38** | **−0.7σ** | **+1.4σ** | **kept: the mass the calculation takes** |
| an MS-bar mass, unconverted | 182.88 ± 0.40 | −14.9σ | −4.2σ | left out |
| the direct mass, calibrated as by Butenschoen et al. (2016) | 172.00 ± 0.47 | +0.1σ | +1.7σ | admitted; not separable today |
| the direct mass, with the difference carried as 0 ± 0.52 GeV (PDG 2026) | 172.57 ± 0.64 | −0.5σ | +1.4σ | admitted; not separable today |

- **The alignment kept.** The pole mass, as written. Pole and MS-bar are one alignment in two rulers, so nothing down-wind separates them. The threshold scan reads it best in MS-bar, to about 0.04 GeV, against about 0.12 GeV as a pole mass.
- **Table 87.** Row 7 is compared with the pole average, 172.1 ± 0.6 GeV, at −0.7σ, and its chance of passing if the calculator is wrong is 0.67.

**Table 92. What the rule's choices do to the set's power.** Scored as in section 18.1, with 20,000 draws (choices\_power.py); the first row is the earlier definitions, and the last the deposited ones, which blindtest.py scores in Table 87 within sampling error. At 2 GeV, ΔG today is Zhao et al. evolved, 0.158 ± 0.039, with the collider's 10%.

| Choice | ΔG passes if wrong | Top passes if wrong | The seven if right: median B; P(B > 100) | The seven if wrong: median B; P(B < 1/100) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| the earlier definitions: ΔG at 10 GeV², the top against the direct average | 0.34 | 1.00 | 310; 0.72 | 2 × 10⁻¹³; 0.93 |
| ΔG at 2 GeV | 0.54 | 1.00 | 160; 0.61 | 6 × 10⁻¹²; 0.91 |
| the top against the pole average | 0.34 | 0.67 | 440; 0.77 | 6 × 10⁻¹⁴; 0.94 |
| both: the deposited definitions (Table 87) | 0.54 | 0.67 | 230; 0.68 | 2 × 10⁻¹²; 0.92 |

**Standing.** Both definitions are fixed by the behavior rule: ΔG = ⅙ at μ = 2 GeV, and the top as a pole mass, with m\_t(m\_t) = 162.76 ± 0.37 GeV in MS-bar the same alignment. The lattice match that fixes the scale is the reason for the choice, not evidence for it, and the Electron-Ion Collider tests it. The choices cost power: if the calculator is right, the set's median B falls from about 300 to 240, and the three predictions that carry it pass together by chance 1 in 38 rather than 1 in 60 (Table 87; Table 92).

### 18.3 The behavior rule across the set

Each scored prediction and each recorded number is carried to the observables it touches down-wind (behavior\_audit.py; the values are those of sections 4.10 and 5 to 16). A miss that every alignment shows, the standard one included, is inherited: the rule cannot remove it, and it stays on the record.

- **The scored set.** All nine predictions pass. Two needed the rule to choose between alignments, ΔG and the top, and one, α = 90°, to drop a reading, the legs' angle (section 15.3); nothing down-wind of what is kept is missed.
- **What the rule cannot do.** The cosmological misses are shared with ΛCDM: the same data miss the standard model of cosmology the same way, so no alignment the calculator offers removes them. They stay on the record, and Euclid and the final DESI data decide them. Dropping them because the data lean against them would be fitting after the data, which the scoring rule counts as no evidence (section 18.1).

### 18.5 What decides the standard theories

The standard theories face deciders of their own in the same years, and four of them are the calculator's too. Table 95 lists them, with what would show each standard commitment wrong.

**Table 95. Deciders for the standard theories' open commitments.**

| Commitment | Standard theory | Today | What would show it wrong | Decider | The calculator |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| w = −1 for all time | ΛCDM | a drift preferred at 2.8 to 4.2σ (DESI Collaboration 2025), 3.2σ after DES's recalibration | a confirmed drift | Euclid and the final DESI data, 2027 to 2030 | the same commitment, derived from a held store |
| flatness | inflation | Ω\_k = 0.0007 ± 0.0019 (Planck with BAO); +0.0023 ± 0.0011 (DESI with the CMB) | curvature beyond about ±0.001 | Euclid and the final DESI data, 2027 to 2030 | flat if the centre ends; Ω\_k ≤ 0 if it turns |
| the neutrino mass sum above the oscillation floor | ΛCDM with three neutrinos | the effective mass 3.0σ below the normal-ordering floor (Elbers et al. 2025) | a sum confirmed below 58.8 meV | the final DESI and Euclid with the microwave background; KATRIN on the scale | the same floor, at 59.2 meV |
| primordial gravitational waves | inflation's simplest potentials, r ≈ 0.003 to 0.01 | r < 0.036 (BICEP/Keck Collaboration 2021) | no r above 0.002 | LiteBIRD, launch planned for Japan's fiscal year 2032 (Ghigna et al. 2024) | r ≈ 0 if the centre turns: the measurement that separates the two |
| the spectral tilt | plateau inflation, 1 − 2/N = 0.964 to 0.967 | 0.975 ± 0.003 (PDG 2026): 2.8 to 3.8σ high | the high value confirmed | the Simons Observatory | 26/27, noted, pressed the same way |
| the Hubble constant | ΛCDM with the microwave background, 67.19 ± 0.38 km/s/Mpc (Camphuis et al. 2025) | 73.17 ± 0.86 from the Cepheid ladder (Breuval et al. 2024); 70.4 ± 1.9 from JWST ladders (Freedman et al. 2025) | a ladder value confirmed free of systematics | the JWST ladders | silent |
| the Higgs self-coupling | the Standard Model, κ\_λ = 1 | −0.71 < κ\_λ < 6.1 at 95% (ATLAS and CMS Collaborations 2026) | κ\_λ away from 1 | the high-luminosity LHC, 2030s | silent: too loose to test the balance |
| a cold dark-matter particle | ΛCDM's usual reading | none found: σ\_SI < 2.2 × 10⁻⁴⁸ cm² at 40 GeV (LZ Collaboration 2025) | none found down to the neutrino floor | the next xenon detectors | held mass; no particle |

- **Shared deciders.** w, the curvature and the neutrino sum will move ΛCDM and the calculator together. A confirmed drift in w would break both.
- **The separating decider.** r separates the turned version of the calculator from inflation's simplest potentials; the ended version predicts no r.
- **The standard theories' own.** The Hubble constant, the Higgs self-coupling and the dark-matter particle are tests the calculator does not take, because it commits to no number there.

## 19. Origination ledger

Every piece of this paper is listed here with its originator and its closest prior work. The 62 pieces fall into four groups:

- **7 are classical results** reached independently (M), one of them, row 60, Claude's completion of her charts;
- **5 are new**, with no published counterpart found (N);
- **1 is a slip** (D);
- **49 are composite:** a classical result with a new reading, use or construction.

Twelve pieces carry a divergence:

- **six are slips or overstatements**, corrected or qualified here: rows 3, 6, 20, 21, 53 and 54;
- **one is a deliberate departure from teaching convention:** row 4;
- **five depart from published physics or from its rival proposals:** rows 34, 40, 41, 49 and 57.

Each row of Table 96 names the piece, its closest prior work and what that work says. “Not found” means not found in the searches of 9 and 10 October 2026.

| 2 | c² ± d² = 2(leg)²; ½c² − ½d² = b²; 2c² − 2d² = (2b)² | elementary algebra; the congruum | M |
| 3 | The e construction and the (d, e) lock | no construction found; that two measurements fix a right triangle is classical; Mansfield and Wildberger 2017 (exact ratio trigonometry on Plimpton 322) | N construction; M fact; D: her e² = (½b)² contradicts the construction (section 3.2) |
