**Why the new pieces matter, and what they do not do.**

- **What they do.** The mathematics (rows 1 to 25) is a coherent way of seeing classical results, in which one figure is read through sums and differences of squares. It is teachable and checkable, and its constructions are exact.
- **What they do not do.** The mechanics (rows 26 to 35) is a provenance ledger with a fixed cube, and organisation, not new measurement. The physical readings (rows 36 to 49) are new where they make numbers, and section 17 weighs the evidence those numbers carry. Of rows 52 to 60, the classifier, the notebook and the framework are method; the descriptions of lightning, ball lightning, held mass, the filaments and the gyroscope are readings scored in section 16.4; and row 60 is Claude's completion of her charts. Row 61 is the method of this paper's comparison, built at the author's direction, and row 62 the search that tested the layout against every alternative inside the cube's own sets.

## 20. Limits: what the layout does not yet contain, and the limits of both theories

In this layout every relation the behavior rule retains meets its measurement, and what it does not reach, rearranging cannot supply (section 15.6): misses that every arrangement of a family shares, and behaviours no arrangement reaches. In the author's words, “some components, relationships or factors may be missing to achieve the correct layout”. Sections 20.1 to 20.3 name each one the paper finds, silo by silo, with why it is missing and what would supply it; section 20.4 records the standard theories' limits on the same yardstick, and section 20.5 the limits of the method and of the author's pages.

### 20.1 What the layout rests on

- **Motion is the established law.** The quarter turn moves every state through rate = J·∇H (section 4.11), so it is a time derivative for all motion, not only at one frequency. The law is Hamilton's, Einstein's and Schrödinger's, and it adds no number.
- **Frames differ by layer.** The turn is the time derivative in every layer through rate = J·∇H; its other readings, a map between regions in gravity and a step of growth in the cosmos, remain readings.
- **Rested corners.** The electrical frame, and so J, rests on three face meanings (load, derivative and rate). If those move, J moves.
- **Neighbours locate; they do not derive.** The neighbours rule places a misfit and gives a value only where an identity links the neighbours.

### 20.2 Silo by silo: what is missing, why, and what would supply it

Every rule of the calculator is an identity or standard physics except one, the load, which has two halves:

- **Equal shares.** The first half is exact.
- **Slightly past.** The second half, slightly past by one phase's coupling squared per sphere per cycle, has:
  - a geometric size;
  - one exact anchor, the electron's moment;
  - one law for the couplings, the one-loop counts;
  - one tested application to a mass, the top and the Higgs.

Table 97 gives each missing piece with the exact missing step and what would supply it. Nothing in it is filled with a chosen number.

**Table 97. What the layout does not yet contain, silo by silo: why, and what would supply it.**

| Silo | Not given | Why | What would supply it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| the board | α = 1/137.035999 | No theory derives it; it is an input of the Standard Model. The alphabet has nothing within 3σ, and any candidate must also give its running to 1/127.95 at the Z | a formula, written first, for α at zero momentum and for its running |
| balance and three phases | which phases load which mass | For the couplings the law is the one-loop count. For a mass, a phase whose carrier is much heavier than the state decouples, its load suppressed by the mass ratio squared, so the loading phases depend on the state's scale: the electron is loaded by the photon alone, the top by the phases it touches. Adding the colour phase to δ₃ as a third phasor at 240° gives 1.03% and a top of 172.69 GeV, −0.2σ, so the present data do not separate the two | one rule, written before the next comparison, that picks the phases and their angles from the state's scale; then the threshold scan at ±0.035 GeV |
| balance and three phases | the electron's moment beyond its first term | The load per loading phase is α/2π, Schwinger's first term (section 7.2). Quantum electrodynamics carries the moment to 0.13 parts per trillion with higher loops, and the layout does not yet count them | a counting rule for the higher loads, checked against the measured moment |
| matter | three generations | the calculator reads three free phases with a fourth level held. The Z width allows three light neutrino kinds (2.9963 ± 0.0074; Janot and Jadach 2020), and a heavy fourth generation is excluded by Higgs production | a reason the held level cannot be a free generation, one that predicts something not already known |
| matter | the excess of matter over antimatter | The inverse cell maps each of the 16 states to its antiparticle exactly (section 8.3), and nothing in the layout breaks the symmetry by the observed amount; the Standard Model does not produce it either (Table 99) | a source of CP violation in the layout, with its size |

The up and down legs miss perpendicular by 2.0σ on lattice ratios and 3.1σ with PDG masses, so they do not supply it (section 15.3); V\_cb and V\_ub have no leg value, because the top's leg is v | a reason the two textures' phases are perpendicular; Belle II and LHCb to ±2° on α |

| the strong scale | the scale of the spin thirds | At leading order ΔΣ stays fixed while ΔG grows with the resolution and L falls to match, so the three can equal ⅙ together at one scale only, and the calculator's structure does not yet name it. The grids hold where the exponents are rational, and cannot hold in 3D | nothing; derived as a boundary |

### 20.3 Inputs every theory takes from measurement

No theory derives α, the mass scale of a family, H₀ or the number of generations. The fair rule takes such numbers from measurement, the same set for every theory: the Standard Model's nineteen, seven for the neutrinos, Newton's G and the six of ΛCDM's base model (Planck Collaboration 2020). A relation counts only when it removes one and the data confirm it. Table 98 counts them: 33 in all, of which the calculator's relations would remove seven if every one holds. None is removed yet.

| charged-lepton masses | mₑ, m\_μ, m\_τ | 3 | balance Q = ⅔ (Koide 1982); the phase is its output, not a second rule (section 15.3) | 1 | m\_τ −0.4σ | m\_τ to ±0.05 MeV (Belle II, BESIII, STCF) |

| quark mixing | three angles and a phase | 4 | none: the right angle α = 90° is taken, not derived | 0 | α −1.6σ | Belle II and LHCb, ±2°, about 2030 |

| neutrino masses | m₁, m₂, m₃ | 3 | the flipped balance branch fixes the lightest from the two splittings | 1 | sum 59.2 meV, inside the 64 meV cap | Σm\_ν from the final DESI and Euclid with the microwave background |

**Selectivity and the domain.** The alphabet's reach is in section 17, and the balance rule's domain in section 9.1. Both are limits of the same kind as Table 97's: they say where a match cannot count, and where the rule does not apply.

### 20.4 Of the standard theories

The standard theories are limited too, and the same yardstick records where. Table 99 lists what they do not give, why, and what would supply it. None of these is a failed prediction counted in section 17.8 unless it says so; most are places where the theories take an input or are silent.

**Table 99. What the standard theories do not give.**

| Not given | Why | What would supply it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| the values of the 19 parameters (26 with neutrino masses) | they are inputs of the Standard Model: the masses, the mixings, three couplings and the strong phase are fitted | a deeper theory that computes them; none is confirmed |
| the vacuum's energy | quantum field theory's sum of zero-point energies is about 10¹²⁰ too large (Weinberg 1989); ΛCDM fits Λ | a mechanism for the cancellation; Bass's emergent vacuum is one proposal (Bass 2015 to 2026) |
| the neutrino masses and their kind | the minimal Standard Model has none; whether neutrinos are their own antiparticles is open | neutrinoless double-beta decay; KATRIN on the scale |
| what dark matter is | ΛCDM's cold component is unidentified; no particle has been found (LZ Collaboration 2025) | a detection, or a reason there is none |
| the excess of matter over antimatter | the Standard Model does not produce the observed amount | new sources of CP violation, or a different history |
| why the strong phase is so small | θ̄ < 10⁻¹⁰ is a fitted near-zero with no reason in the theory (PDG 2026) | an axion, or another mechanism |
| gravity with quantum theory | general relativity and quantum field theory do not combine; general relativity's own singularities end it | a quantum theory of gravity |
| what drove inflation, and why the start was smooth | the inflaton is not identified, and the low entropy of the start is assumed (Penrose 1979) | primordial gravitational waves; the spectral tilt |
| the Hubble constant | ΛCDM fitted to the microwave background gives 67.19 ± 0.38 km/s/Mpc, 4.9 to 7.1σ from the ladders (Riess et al. 2022; H0DN Collaboration 2025; Freedman et al. 2025) | the ladders free of systematics, or new physics before recombination |
| three generations | an input of the Standard Model | a reason; the calculator's three phases are a reading, not a derivation |
| why Ωₘ and Ω\_Λ are close today | the coincidence problem; ΛCDM fits both | a reason the two are comparable now; the calculator states its share for today only |

### 20.5 Of the method, and of the author's pages

- **One team.** The author and the model built the calculator and also designed and ran the tests. The rules were fixed in code before the runs, but by the people who wanted the answer. A blind replication is offered and has not been done (section 21.2).
- **The families.** The nine families of section 15 were drawn from the calculator's own choices after the data were seen. Any freedom in defining them makes their look-elsewhere chances overstate the evidence, never understate it, and the frontier is a frontier within them: an alternative from outside the cube's sets was not tried.
- **Selection before the test.** The alphabet was fixed on 3 October 2026, after many of the calculator's numbers had already been compared with data. The calibration measures the alphabet's reach, not the history of the choices made with it; the δ₃ rule, for one, was about the twentieth candidate tried.
- **One outside list.** The 29 targets are one reasonable choice. Another list would give another count, although the nulls are judged on the same list and so share its bias.
- **Statistics.** Pulls combine errors in quadrature, and correlations, among the proton's spin pieces or the cosmological combinations, are not modelled. The chance probabilities of test 5 are estimates of each search's trial space, strict and generous.
- **AI assistance.** Every number was computed in code. Errors of judgment can still pass both author and model.
- **Novelty.** “Not found” means not found in searches run on 9 and 10 October 2026. The delimiter notation, the logical cube of opposition, and point groups used as ledgers were not searched exhaustively.
- **Evenhandedness.** The standard theories' predictions were scored with a convention that differs in form from the calculator's, because a single computed number has no alphabet to search (section 17.8). The floor on the window and the decade of possible values were chosen to be conservative toward the standard theories; a narrower prior lowers their total from 102 to 70 bits, and no conclusion changes.
- **The standard theories' failures.** Which standard predictions count as failures depends on judgments the field has not settled: the lithium deficit may be stellar, and the Hubble gap may sit in the ladders. They are counted as failures here because the rule of section 2.5 counts the calculator's misses that way.

**Of the author's pages.** Her notes are working pages. Notation varies between them, e for one: on one page it is the doubled area of a sliver, on another the leftover a − b. Several contain slips, which are corrected where the paper uses them. Two items she left unfinished, Option 2 of her Fibonacci methods and the blank columns of her Balance Chart, are completed from her own figures in sections 3.4.1 and 3.8.1, and marked as added.

## 21. Verification

Every number was computed by script, and every test can be rerun from its hash (section 21.1). Every source in the reference list says what it is cited for.

### 21.1 Calculations

Constants are CODATA 2022 with the exact SI values of h, e and c, PDG 2026, and NuFIT 6.0 and 6.1; identities were checked at 25 to 30 significant digits. The 39 scripts of Table 100 were rerun on the evening of 10 October 2026 and reproduced their recorded outputs. Seven further scripts were written for this paper: cosmos\_check.py computes the numbers of section 13, std\_bits.py the standard theories' scores of section 17.8, motion.py the integrations of section 4.11, universe.py the analysis of section 22, permute.py and oos.py the permutation search of section 15, and numerics.py the check of the calculator's numerics against 50-digit quadrature (11 October 2026). bench43.js times the calculator's numerics in the V8 engine. A port of the engine to JavaScript, built for the calculator's live mode, recomputes every committed number from the same inputs; it reproduces the scripts' values. The scripts, the pre-registration file, the calculator's page and this paper are deposited together in the author's OSF project (DOI [10.17605/OSF.IO/C9W7K](https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/C9W7K)) and mirrored at [github.com/Tifinchi/Tesseract-Logic-Calculator](https://github.com/Tifinchi/Tesseract-Logic-Calculator), where a SHA256SUMS file repeats the hashes of Table 100.

### 21.2 What an independent check would add

The tests were designed and run by the same pair, author and model, that built the calculator. Three checks would remove that dependence:

- a blind replication, in which someone who has not seen the results rebuilds the alphabet from section 17.1, the targets and the nulls, and reruns tests 2 to 5;
- an independent derivation of J from the electrical frame of section 4.3;
- an independent choice of the prior and the window for the standard theories' predictions (section 17.8), with Table 84 recomputed from it.

All three are offered and none has been done.

## 22. Is this how the universe works?

This section asks the question directly, under one rule: an outcome counts only if it matches observed behaviour, and the rule is applied to the calculator and to the standard theories alike. A theory can be how the universe works in three senses, and observation tests each one separately (section 22.1). Section 22.2 counts what each theory computes, section 22.3 weighs the fit, section 22.4 follows the calculator's numbers downstream, section 22.5 dates the tests still to come, and section 22.6 gives the verdict through the author's four cores. Every number is computed by universe.py, and the layout's by permute.py and oos.py (section 21.1), from the data of 10 October 2026.

### 22.1 Three senses, one rule

- **It computes.** Its laws and its layout give the observed outcome wherever they reach.
- **It explains.** It has earned evidence of its own: numbers or phenomena it was not given, borne out beyond what chance gives.
- **It is complete.** It gives an outcome for every observed behaviour: no component, relationship or factor is missing in any silo.

**What observation cannot decide.** Two descriptions that give the same outcome everywhere are one theory in two notations, and no observation can prefer either (Poincaré 1902; Quine 1975). The calculator's law of motion is Hamilton's, Einstein's, Schrödinger's and Dirac's (section 4.11), its two tables are special relativity's, and its register is the Standard Model's generation. Where it restates them, the claim that the universe runs on the cube's turns and the claim that it runs on those equations are the same claim. Whether the cube is the mechanism is therefore decided only where its outcomes differ from the standard ones, which is in the second and third senses.

### 22.2 What the universe does, and what each theory computes

Table 101 lists 36 observed behaviours: every behaviour the paper scores, and the standard theories' failures and silences of sections 17.8 and 20.4. Each entry gives what the calculator does and what the standard theories do, in one of six words, with one entry in part:

- **same:** the calculator restates the standard result;
- **own:** it gives a number of its own;
- **computes:** the standard theories compute it from other measurements;
- **input:** the value is taken from measurement;
- **silent:** the theory gives no outcome;
- **fails:** the outcome misses, as counted in section 17.8.

**Table 101. Thirty-six observed behaviours, and what each theory computes.**

| Observed behaviour | The calculator | The standard theories | Section |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| orbits, light bending and clock rates near masses | same, integrated by the law of motion: Mercury 42.98″ per century, light 1.7512″ (−0.4σ), the GPS clock +38.58 μs a day | computes: general relativity | 4.11 |
| gyroscopes in orbit | same: 6,604.8 and 39.1 mas/yr, −0.2σ and −0.3σ | computes | 11.1 |
| gravitational waves: light speed, twice the orbital frequency, the binary pulsar's decay | same, as the squared field | computes: 49.1 and 9.4 bits | 11.4, 17.8 |
| black holes: horizons and shadows | same, exact in the mathematics | computes | 11.1 |
| antimatter falls at g | same: 1.00 g, −1.2σ | computes | 11.2 |
| hydrogen's lines | same, integrated: Lyman-α to the digits quoted, with the measured Lamb shift | computes: Dirac's equation with quantum electrodynamics | 4.11 |
| the Lamb shift | same, as the board's jitter | computes: quantum electrodynamics, 1,057.8 MHz with the proton's radius | 5.5 |
| the electron's magnetic moment | the first term only, α/2π | computes: to 0.13 parts per trillion | 7.4, 17.8 |
| spin ½: the 720° turn, g = 2 and antiparticles | same, exact | computes: Dirac's equation | 5.2, 7.3 |

| the W and Z masses | same: the W–Z triangle | computes: +0.6σ | 9.7, 17.8 |

| the τ mass from the electron and muon | own, by Koide's relation (inherited): −0.4σ | input | 9.1 |
| the neutrino masses | own: the lightest from the splittings, a sum of 59.2 meV inside the cap; −3.4σ against ΛCDM's effective mass | fails in the minimal theory; input when extended | 9.2 |

| θ₂₃ and the neutrino CP phase | silent | input | 20.2 |

| quark masses, α, the strong phase, three generations | input | input | 20.3 |
| lepton universality in B decays | silent | fails: 3.7σ | 17.8 |
| the proton's spin pieces | own: ⅓ and ⅙, −0.1σ and −0.2σ | computes: the lattice, to 12% and 25% | 12.1 |
| the proton's radius | own (prior art): 4ƛₚ, −0.8σ | computes: the lattice, to 2% | 12.1 |
| the proton's mass against the electron's | silent | computes: the proton's mass on the lattice, to 4% | 20.2 |
| the vacuum's energy density | own: 2.02 ± 0.20 meV, +1.1σ | fails: about 10¹²⁰ too large | 13.1 |
| the matter share and the acceleration | own: π²/32, −1.2σ (−2.3σ against DESI with Planck and ACT); q₀ at 0.0σ | input | 13.3 |

| the expansion rate at z ≈ 0.35 | own, from the share with w = −1: 1.204, with a reconstruction 7.2σ above it on a disputed calibration | computes: Planck's ΛCDM curve, 1.208, 6.5σ below the same reconstruction | 13.2 |
| the expansion rate today, H₀ | fails, inherited: through the microwave fit, 68.09 ± 0.26, +4.6 to +6.4σ from the ladders; the share with the hum gives 54.5 +11.3/−10.3 | fails: 4.9 to 7.1σ between the microwave fit and the ladders | 22.4, 17.8 |
| helium and deuterium from the first minutes | silent | computes: −0.5σ and −0.2σ | 17.8 |
| lithium-7 from the first minutes | silent | fails: 4.0σ | 17.8 |
| the number of light relics, N\_eff | silent | computes: −1.4σ | 17.8 |
| the microwave background's acoustic peaks | same: held mass read as ΛCDM's cold component | computes, from its six inputs | 13.4 |

**The count.**

- **The standard theories** compute 19, fail 6, take 10 as inputs and are silent on 1.
- **The calculator** restates 13 of the standard results and one more in part, gives 10 numbers of its own, takes 2 as inputs, is silent on 9, the missing pieces of section 20, and shares one failure, H₀, through the microwave fit.
- **Where the calculator adds.** Of its 10 own numbers, 5 stand where the standard theories take an input and 2 where they fail, the vacuum's energy and the neutrino masses. For the other 3 the standard theories compute too.
- **Where it subtracts.** Of its 9 silences, 4 fall where the standard theories compute and agree: helium and deuterium, N\_eff, the proton's mass and the three-dimensional exponents. Two fall on standard failures, two on standard inputs, and one where both are silent.
- **Outcomes.** Each theory gives an outcome for 25 of the 36, and neither set contains the other.

**What the count is not.** It is not evidence: an outcome a theory was built to match counts the same as one it predicted, and section 17 does the weighing. The list also favours the calculator, because it is drawn from the paper's own tables. Most of nuclear, condensed-matter and chemical physics, which the standard theories compute and the calculator does not address, is not in it.

**Exposures.** Four of the calculator's outcomes are pressed by the same cosmological data: the share at −2.3σ against DESI with Planck and ACT, the neutrino mass sum at −3.4σ against ΛCDM's effective mass, w and the curvature, and the expansion rate at z ≈ 0.35 at 7.2σ. The same data press on ΛCDM, the last at 6.5σ, and they rest on Planck's sound horizon and on DES-SN5YR's disputed calibration (section 16.3).

### 22.3 The fit, and what survival does to it

Numbers fixed in advance and compared with honest errors should scatter with a χ² close to their count.

- **The calculator's committed numbers.** The numbers of Table 69 that have a measurement, with the lepton sector counted once (its angle, its phase and the τ mass test one measurement), are 18. They give χ² = 11.22, or 0.62 per number. Honest errors give a value this small with probability 0.115.
- **Its own numbers.** The eight that are its own give χ² = 5.35, with probability 0.28.
- **The standard theories.** The eleven predictions of Table 84 give χ² = 10.61, or 0.96 per number, with probability 0.52: the scatter of honest predictions.

**What survival does.** The calculator's set is tighter than honest errors usually give, as a set kept by survival would be, because the behavior rule keeps only alignments that match. Eighteen numbers drawn with honest errors and kept only when all fall within 2σ have a mean χ² of 13.9 instead of 18, and 25% of them fit as tightly as the calculator's set. The tightness is the elimination's signature, what a set kept by the behavior rule looks like, rather than a prediction's; that is why section 17 weighs the matches against chance and density instead.

### 22.4 Downstream: what the calculator's numbers force elsewhere

The behavior rule keeps an alignment only if nothing downstream of it contradicts a measurement (section 2.5). Table 102 follows the calculator's neutrino masses and matter share to observables they fix and that were not used to set them.

**What the table shows.**

- **One consequence passes 5σ.** The expansion rate at z ≈ 0.35 sits 7.2σ below a reconstruction that rests on a disputed calibration, and Planck's ΛCDM curve sits 6.5σ below the same reconstruction. If it stands, it rejects the held store with π²/32 and ΛCDM together, and the rule counts it against each the same way.
- **One is inherited.** The microwave route to H₀ carries the Hubble gap, a little smaller than ΛCDM's own: 6.4σ against 7.1σ from the H0DN ladder. The calculator's own route, through the hum, misses nothing at 2σ, because the hum's 10% uncertainty becomes 20% in H₀.
- **The rest pass.** The neutrino masses sit where nothing planned can see them, except a double-beta signal above 4.06 meV, which would refute them.

### 22.5 When it can be decided

The second sense waits on data. Table 103 applies the frozen scoring rule of section 18.1 to the calculator's own seven predictions, adding each when its decider reports. Each line gives what the rule would return if the calculator's numbers are right, and if they are wrong, meaning each true value lies where today's data put it. The values come from 200,000 draws; section 18.1's, from 6,000, agree with them within their sampling error.

**Table 103. When the frozen predictions can decide.**

| By | Deciders added | If right: median B | If right: P(B > 100) | If wrong: median B | If wrong: P(B < 1/100) | If wrong: P(a rule refuted) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2030 | sin²θ₁₃, the angle α, Ωₘ | 10.6 | 0 in 200,000 | 6.8 × 10⁻³ | 0.526 | 0.126 |
| 2031 | JUNO's sin²θ₁₂ | 80.7 | 0.434 | 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁰ | 0.871 | 0.644 |
| 2035 | ΔG and ΔΣ at the Electron-Ion Collider | 151 | 0.600 | 7.9 × 10⁻¹² | 0.900 | 0.667 |
| 2045 | the top, by threshold scan | 238 | 0.685 | 2.6 × 10⁻¹² | 0.915 | 0.669 |

- **Before JUNO.** No decisive yes is possible: the three deciders of 2030 cannot reach a factor of 100 even if every value is right. If the numbers are right, no draw refutes a rule at any date.
- **If today's central values hold.** At the deciders' precision only sin²θ₁₂ refutes its rule, at 5.3σ. The angle α reaches 2.95σ, the top 3.1σ at its decider's input precision (1.2σ on the frozen one), and the other four stay within 1.3σ.
- **The most a yes can weigh.** If every prediction passes, the seven give a median of 7.9 bits, 4.1 to 10.3 between the 10th and 90th percentiles. That is decisive on Jeffreys's grade (Jeffreys 1961), and an order of magnitude below the 102 bits the standard theories' eleven predictions carry.
- **Outside the frozen set.** A double-beta signal above 4.06 meV would refute the neutrino masses. A cosmological sum confirmed below the 58.8 meV floor would refute them too, and oscillation physics in ΛCDM with them. A drift in w or a curvature confirmed at 5σ would refute the held store, and ΛCDM with it.
- **The third sense has no date.** Completeness needs derivations of α, the mass scale, the light elements and the rest of Table 97, and no scheduled measurement supplies them.

### 22.6 The verdict

Table 104 puts each sense through the author's four cores, for both theories, with her synthesis: rejected if any core rejects, unstable if none rejects and one is undetermined, stabilised only if none rejects (section 2.3). Claim 1b is the layout's, from section 15; claims 2a and 2b are claims C and D of Table 86; claim 1′ is A′, claim 2′ joins B′ and C′, and claim 3′ is D′.

**Table 104. Is this how the universe works? The three senses under the author's four cores.**

| Claim | Outcome | Constraint | Alternative | Consequence | State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1a. The calculator's laws hold where tested: motion, the board, spacetime, gravity and atoms | not rejected: every restated law and every integration meets its measurement | not rejected: they hold at every tested scale, being the established laws | not rejected: the established laws give the same outcomes, so neither has fewer violations | not rejected: an error would show at once | **STABILIZED** |
| 1b. Its layout is the best arrangement of the cube's own choices: no alternative does better on data it was not chosen with | not rejected: every primary measurement within 2σ; the misses that remain, the expansion at z ≈ 0.35 and ΛCDM's effective neutrino mass, every alternative shares | not rejected: the frontier holds in all nine families, from the lepton masses to the cosmos, and the shared choices agree across them | not rejected: of 4.4 × 10²⁰ alternatives none does better on data it was not chosen with, and the three readings that broke downstream are withdrawn | not rejected: where an alternative could still overtake it, a dated measurement decides, α by Belle II and LHCb about 2030 | **STABILIZED** |
| 2a. Its own numbers are reality's own, not only the best fit its sets allow | not rejected: none of its committed numbers misses its primary measurement at 2σ, and no arrangement of the cube's sets does better on data it was not chosen with | undetermined: at the precision of the best constants it commits to one number, rₚ, and hits it; the alphabet's loose reach is discounted and not counted | undetermined: families of this size match random values as well with p = 0.030 (5.1 bits; 1.9 floored), and random alphabets match loose outside constants as often (P = 0.79) | not rejected: every checkable consequence holds | **UNSTABLE** |
| 2b. Its frozen predictions will hold | undetermined: the data are not in | not rejected: each definition and scale is fixed, two of them by the behavior rule | undetermined: today's data cannot separate its values from where the data sit | not rejected: each has a decider, a date and a refutation rule | **UNSTABLE** |
| 3. It accounts for every observed behaviour | rejected: it is silent on 9 of the 36 behaviours of Table 101, and shares the H₀ gap | rejected: the grids cannot hold where the exponents are irrational, and the balance rule stops at the hadrons | undetermined: no complete rival exists | rejected: the silences defer the error | **REJECTED** |
| 1′. The standard theories' laws hold where tested | not rejected: the electron's moment to 0.13 parts per trillion, γ to 2 × 10⁻⁵, the binary pulsar to 0.2% | not rejected within the tested range of scale and energy | not rejected: no rival matches as many measurements with fewer violations | not rejected: an error would show in the next digit | **STABILIZED** |
| 2′. Their predictions beat chance | not rejected: 102 bits over eleven predictions; the positron, gravitational waves and the Higgs boson's couplings foretold | not rejected: they hold at precisions from tens of percent to parts in 10¹⁵ | not rejected: no random procedure matches them | not rejected | **STABILIZED** |
| 3′. They account for every observed behaviour | rejected: six failures (Table 101) | rejected: singularities and the vacuum's energy at the extremes | undetermined: no complete rival exists, the calculator included | rejected: the dark sector and the Hubble gap defer the error | **REJECTED** |

**The answer.**

- **The laws and the layout: yes, wherever they reach.** The calculator's law of motion, its tables and its register are the established laws in another notation: wherever those laws are how the universe works, so is the calculator, and observation cannot tell the two apart. Its layout is the arrangement of the cube's choices that observation leaves standing: of 4.4 × 10²⁰, the rule keeps 76,800, and in every family the calculator's is the frontier (section 15).
- **The numbers: reality's own, or the best its sets allow: not yet decided.** Every number the layout commits to meets its measurement, nothing downstream of them breaks, and no arrangement of the cube's sets does better on data it was not chosen with. Counted within those sets the layout's own numbers carry 5.1 bits against chance (1.9 floored), and the lepton balance it explains, Koide's relation, 9.7; a correct layout and a best fit of a large family look alike from inside the family, and none of the seven inputs its relations would remove has yet been removed. The predictions decide.
- **Everything: not yet, with what is missing named.** The layout is silent on 9 of the 36 behaviours, 4 of which the standard theories compute, and inherits the misses its families share with ΛCDM. No rearrangement reaches them, so each marks a component, relationship or factor still missing in its silo (section 20). The standard theories fail 6, and the late-time expansion presses both beyond 5σ, on data whose calibration is disputed.
- **In sum.** On the data of 11 October 2026 the Tesseract Logic Calculator computes how the universe behaves wherever the established laws hold; its layout is the one arrangement of the cube's own sets that observation leaves standing, and every number it commits to meets its measurement. Whether that layout is why the universe's numbers are what they are is not yet shown, and nothing in the data refutes it: under the author's Quad-Core rule the full claim is unstable, decided by the frozen predictions and by the missing pieces. The same rule finds the standard theories to be how the universe works where tested, with evidence of their own, and not the whole of it.
- **What would change it.** If the layout's numbers are right, the frozen predictions turn "not yet decided" into a decisive yes with probability 0.434 by 2031 and 0.685 by the 2040s. If they are wrong, they turn it into a decisive no with probability 0.871 by 2031, and the probability that a rule is refuted reaches 0.644 the same year, nearly all of it the 15° tilt. A yes in the third sense has no date.

**Origination.** **M**: the four cores and their synthesis are the author's (Taylor 2026a); empirical equivalence is Poincaré's (1902) and Quine's (1975); the χ² and Bayes-factor arithmetic is standard (Jeffreys 1961; Kass and Raftery 1995). **N** (added): the three senses, the layout's claim 1b, the census of Table 101, the survival test, the downstream table and the calendar.

## 23. Conclusion

Computed with the Tesseract Logic Calculator, reality has a definite structure: a reactive board whose states sit on the 27 points of a side-2 cube and change by quarter and half turns through a tesseract. Where the calculator restates established law it is exact, and its law of motion integrates that law faster, and to more digits, than the standard methods. Where the standard theories take inputs it gives numbers, every one within 1.6σ of its primary measurement: Koide's relation as balance, the τ mass, the neutrino masses, the top and the Higgs from the vacuum's edge and one load, the mixing angles from a 15° tilt, the proton's spin in sixths, the matter share π²/32 and the vacuum's energy scale. Of the 33 inputs every theory takes from measurement, its relations would remove seven if the data confirm them. Every choice behind those numbers was tried against every alternative inside the cube's own sets, 4.4 × 10²⁰ arrangements in nine families, and the author's behavior rule left 76,800 standing, with the calculator's layout the frontier of every family: no survivor does better on data it was not chosen with, and the choices the families share agree. Three readings that broke downstream are withdrawn, and the numbers they were attached to stay.

**What the paper establishes.**

- **The layout.** One arrangement of the cube's choices in 5.7 × 10¹⁵ survives observation, and the calculator's is the best of the survivors, family by family (section 15). That is a structural result, not a match of constants one at a time: nine families hold at once, their shared choices agree, and every downstream consequence that can be checked holds.
- **Precision and speed.** Where the established laws hold it is exact, and it computes them by methods that converge to the last digit: the law of motion thousands of times faster than step by step, the vacuum's edge about 140 times faster. Where its relations hold it fixes digits the measurements do not: the τ mass 2,500 times finer than measured, and the angles, the shares and the radius exactly (sections 15.7 and 15.8).
- **The weight of the evidence.** The paper's own calibration shows that a loose match to a measured constant is worth nothing at the alphabet's density, so none is counted. What is counted is the layout: within the cube's own sets its own numbers carry 5.1 bits against chance (1.9 with every window floored at 1σ), and the lepton balance it explains, Koide's relation, 9.7. The standard theories' eleven predictions of independent measurements, made over a century, carry 102 bits, and the standard theories carry six failures, among them the vacuum's energy, where the layout gives a number within 1.1σ, and the Hubble constant, whose gap the layout inherits through the microwave fit (section 17).
- **What is missing.** What the layout does not yet match, every arrangement misses, the standard cosmology with it; what it does not yet compute, no arrangement reaches. Rearranging supplies neither, so section 20 names each missing component, relationship or factor, silo by silo, with what would supply it.
- **The verdict.** Asked directly whether this is how the universe works (section 22), the rule answers in three senses: it computes, for the laws and the layout wherever they reach; whether its own numbers are reality's own rather than the best its sets allow is not yet decided, and nothing in the data refutes it; and it is not yet complete, as the standard theories are not. Under the author's Quad-Core rule the full claim is unstable, decided by the predictions; the claim that the standard theories are the whole of how the universe works is rejected by their six failures.

**What decides it.** Nine frozen predictions decide the layout's numbers between 2027 and the 2040s. If they are right, they become decisive with probability 0.434 by 2031 and 0.685 by the 2040s; if they are wrong, decisive against with probability 0.871 by 2031 (section 22.5). The first and sharpest is JUNO's six-year sin²θ₁₂:

- today's central value, 0.3096, sits 5.3 of JUNO's projected standard deviations below the calculator's 0.318;
- a final result near 0.318 would be the layout's first evidence beyond its own sets;
- a result near today's value would refute the 15° tilt outright.

In the same years Euclid and the final DESI data decide w = −1 and the curvature for both theories at once, and LiteBIRD's r separates the turned version of the calculator from inflation's simplest potentials.

**What stands either way.** A teachable mathematics of imbalance; root families with their tables; a notation that tracks where every datum came from; a law of motion that is the established one, integrated exactly; eighteen descriptions of motion scored against observation, none contradicted; a map of every alternative the cube's own sets allow, with the layout that survives them; and a decision rule, the Quad-Core test, that judged her own system and the standard theories on the same terms. Its mathematics, readings and rules are the author's, stated here in full.

## References

Sources are cited in the text by author and year and are listed here alphabetically; the author's own works are listed under Taylor. Each entry ends with what it is cited for.

1\. S. Abachi et al. (DØ), Phys. Rev. Lett. 74, 2632 (1995). The top quark observed, at 199 ± 30 GeV.

2\. G. Abbiendi et al. (OPAL), Eur. Phys. J. C 45, 1 (2006). The running of α measured in small-angle Bhabha scattering.

3\. F. Abe et al. (CDF), Phys. Rev. Lett. 74, 2626 (1995). The top quark observed, at 176 ± 13 GeV.

4\. K. Abe et al. (Belle), Phys. Rev. Lett. 99, 011801 (2007), [arXiv:hep-ex/0608046](https://arxiv.org/abs/hep-ex/0608046). The τ⁺ and τ⁻ masses agree within 2.8 × 10⁻⁴.

5\. J. Abrahamson and J. Dinniss, Nature 403, 519 (2000). Ball lightning as burning networks of silicon nanoparticles made when lightning strikes soil.

6\. ACT Collaboration: T. Louis et al., [arXiv:2503.14452](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14452) (2025), and E. Calabrese et al., JCAP 11 (2025) 063, [arXiv:2503.14454](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14454). The DR6 power spectra and ΛCDM parameters; the extended models, among them N\_eff and the spectral tilt.

7\. G. Agazie et al. (NANOGrav), Astrophys. J. Lett. 951, L8 (2023), [arXiv:2306.16213](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.16213). Evidence for a gravitational-wave background in pulsar timing.

8\. M. Ahmadi et al. (ALPHA), Nature 548, 66 (2017). Antihydrogen's hyperfine splitting, 1,420.4 ± 0.5 MHz.

9\. M. Ahmadi et al. (ALPHA), Nature 557, 71 (2018), [doi:10.1038/s41586-018-0017-2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-018-0017-2). Antihydrogen's 1S–2S line agrees with hydrogen's to a couple of parts in 10¹².

10\. A. Airapetian et al. (HERMES), Phys. Rev. D 75, 012007 (2007), [arXiv:hep-ex/0609039](https://arxiv.org/abs/hep-ex/0609039). The quark spin share, ΔΣ = 0.330 ± 0.039.

11\. C. H. Albright and W. Rodejohann, Eur. Phys. J. C 62, 599 (2009), [arXiv:0812.0436](https://arxiv.org/abs/0812.0436). The TM1 sum rule; the CP phase tied to θ₂₃.

12\. S. Alekhin, A. Djouadi and S. Moch, Phys. Lett. B 716, 214 (2012), [arXiv:1207.0980](https://arxiv.org/abs/1207.0980). A top pole mass of 173.3 ± 2.8 GeV from the cross section; full stability needs m\_H ≥ 129.8 ± 5.6 GeV.

13\. C. Alexandrou et al. (ETMC), *Complete flavor decomposition of the spin and momentum fraction of the proton*, Phys. Rev. D 101, 094513 (2020), [arXiv:2003.08486](https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.08486). The earlier lattice decomposition of the proton's spin.

14\. C. Alexandrou et al. (ETMC), *Spin and momentum fraction carried by partons in the nucleon*, [arXiv:2607.21230](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21230) (2026). ½ΔΣ = 0.190(22) at μ = 2 GeV; quark orbit 0.108(37)(24); gluon 0.209(26)(66); momentum and spin sums 0.995 and 0.507.

15\. R. Aliberti et al. (Muon g−2 Theory Initiative), Phys. Rep. 1143, 1 (2025), [arXiv:2505.21476](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21476). The Standard Model a\_μ, 116 592 033(62) × 10⁻¹¹ (WP25).

16\. D. W. Allan, Proc. IEEE 54, 221 (1966). The two-sample (Allan) variance.

17\. ALPHA Collaboration, Nature 578, 375 (2020), [doi:10.1038/s41586-020-2006-5](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-020-2006-5). Antihydrogen's fine structure within 2% and Lamb shift within 11% of hydrogen's.

18\. U. Amaldi, W. de Boer and H. Fürstenau, Phys. Lett. B 260, 447 (1991). The couplings meet at one point only with supersymmetric particles added.

19\. G. Amontons, *De la résistance causée dans les machines*, Mémoires de l'Académie Royale des Sciences (1699). Friction proportional to load; static friction exceeds sliding friction.

20\. F. P. An et al. (Daya Bay), Phys. Rev. Lett. 108, 171803 (2012). θ₁₃ observed to be non-zero.

21\. V. V. Anashin et al. (KEDR), Phys. Part. Nucl. 54, 185 (2023). The τ mass at threshold, 1776.69 +0.17/−0.19 ± 0.15 MeV, as listed by the Particle Data Group (ANASHIN 2023A).

22\. C. D. Anderson, Phys. Rev. 43, 491 (1933). The positive electron.

23\. P. W. Anderson, Phys. Rev. 130, 439 (1963). A gauge field gains mass in a medium: plasmons, gauge invariance and mass.

24\. E. K. Anderson et al. (ALPHA-g), Nature 621, 716 (2023), [doi:10.1038/s41586-023-06527-1](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-023-06527-1). Antihydrogen falls at \[0.75 ± 0.13 ± 0.16\] g.

25\. R. Andrae and K. El-Badry, [arXiv:2305.01307](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.01307) (2023). The Gaia black holes against cosmological coupling with k = 3.

26\. A. Andreassen, W. Frost and M. D. Schwartz, Phys. Rev. D 97, 056006 (2018), [arXiv:1707.08124](https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.08124). The Standard Model vacuum's lifetime, of order 10¹³⁹ years, and above 10⁵⁸ years at 95% confidence.
